| G269 | A critical job failed hourly for weeks and the watchdog called it healthy | S | done | **`billing-quantity-sync` is registered `is_critical` with a 3-hour freshness SLA and had never once succeeded.** Two independent reasons, both here. **The critical definition had no cron name.** `run_system_job_watchdog()` iterates `where is_active and is_critical and cron_job_name is not null`, and two rows described the one job -- `billing-quantity-sync` (critical, `cron_job_name` null) and `billing-quantity-sync-cron` (carried the cron name, not critical). The split fell exactly across the watchdog's two filters, so neither half was iterated; every other edge-function job carries both on a single row. **And pg_cron "succeeded" is not the job succeeding.** The resolver took `greatest(own_success, cron_success)`, where `cron_success` is the newest `cron.job_run_details` success. For the 13 cron entries whose command is a `net.http_post` to an Edge Function that status records that the *request was enqueued*, not that the function did the work -- so `last_success_at` advanced every hour while the function returned 503 every hour, and the SLA could never be breached. Same failure mode `20260726250000` was written to close, one layer down: there the job was invisible for having no definition row, here because its health signal measured the wrong thing. The critical definition now owns the cron name (the duplicate is removed, guarded by its own run history since the FK is `ON DELETE RESTRICT`), and the watchdog credits the cron-side signal only for `sql_cron`, where the scheduled command *is* the work -- `edge_cron` and `worker` must prove success with a finished `system_job_runs` row. The handler also records the misconfiguration instead of returning 503 into the void. Blast radius is three rows: `notification-dispatch` unchanged (thousands of recent successes), `billing-quantity-sync` stale as intended, and `exclusion-screening` newly stale -- not a regression, a second critical job read off the same wrong sensor, and it needs its own look |
| G270 | Six more critical jobs still read their health off pg_cron's exit status | M | done | **All six closed, in two migrations, because four of them could be fixed with data alone and two needed code first.** `20260904050000` took the four with a sibling definition that its Edge Function already claims and finishes (`certificate-pdf-generation`, `binder-export-generation`, `document-analyzer-extraction`, `integration-webhook-dispatch`): the cron entry moved onto the recording row, the pair's criticality moved with it, and the redundant row was deleted under a run-history guard. It deliberately left the other two and said why -- `run-data-lifecycle` and `process-organization-export-jobs` called `claim_system_job_execution` nowhere, so there was no ledger to point at and repointing would only have swapped an always-green signal for an always-red one. `20260904090000` closed those after instrumenting both functions to claim before any work and finish on every exit path, matching `generate-compliance-binder`'s production pattern: `data-lifecycle` gets each retention policy plus the export sweep as one unit each (a single stuck policy is `partial`, not a dead sweep) and is relabelled `edge_cron`, which is what its `net.http_post` cron command always was; `organization-data-export` takes the cron entry from the redundant `organization-export-jobs` row, with an empty queue recorded as a successful run of nothing so a quiet day and a dead worker stop looking identical. Claiming before the work is load-bearing in both -- a run that dies mid-sweep now leaves a row `20260904080000`'s reconciler closes rather than no trace. Eight Deno tests on the lifecycle handler, three of them new and specifically about the ledger. **Verified: the query that defines this gap -- an active, critical `sql_cron` definition whose cron command is a `net.http_post` -- now returns zero rows, and `go_live_readiness_repairs.test.sql` asserts it unconditionally, with the two-job exemption that migration 050000 needed now removed.** |

### Tier H — Go-live readiness review (2026-09-04)

Findings from the readiness review recorded in
[docs/ops/GO_LIVE_READINESS_REVIEW_PLAN.md](docs/ops/GO_LIVE_READINESS_REVIEW_PLAN.md), which is
the method and the evidence, not a status register. Every `done` row here was verified against a
clean local Supabase stack (603 migrations replayed, full pgTAP suite, lint, advisors, generated
types) rather than by reading. Rows that need a credential, a dashboard toggle or a second person
are `ops_only` and say exactly what is missing.

| ID | Ticket | Size | Status | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| H1 | Six HIGH advisories affecting `main` | S | done | `fast-uri` and `browserslist`. The existing `fast-uri` override targeted 3.1.5 and four new advisories (GHSA-f65p-4m7j-42xc, GHSA-jqff-g426-hqxp, GHSA-fph4-wmhf-6fwf, GHSA-5jgf-p345-68v8) all reach `<3.1.6`, taking that target with them -- the second time this override has been swallowed by a follow-up, which is why it now aims at the latest mature 3.x patch (3.1.7) rather than the lowest version that clears. `browserslist` (GHSA-c83g-rgw3-j3cx, GHSA-73wf-gq98-2v4g) gains its first override at 4.28.8; build-time only, bumped because a high advisory on main is fixed rather than adjudicated. Verified: a strict-mode audit of the new lockfile returns **zero** high or critical, against six before. |
| H2 | The dependency gate fails closed on a registry outage, before `check:all` | S | done | Observed live on this branch: `503 Service Unavailable` then repeated 60s timeouts, three job runs across seven minutes, reddening the whole `application` job on a documentation-only change. `--base` mode already knows the question -- did THIS change introduce an advisory -- so when HEAD and base resolve to an identical dependency set the answer is knowable without asking: both audits would send the same payload and every result would classify as pre-existing. `auditSetsAreIdentical` short-circuits exactly that case and nothing else; strict mode (no `--base`, unreadable base) is untouched, so main still goes red for its own pre-existing advisories. Six new self-test fixtures pin the comparison as exact in both directions. |
| H3 | Entry chunk over 90% of its budget, warning on every branch | S | done | Measured 564.6 KiB against a 620 KiB budget (91.1%). Raised deliberately to 650 KiB with the reasoning recorded inline, which is what `check-bundle-budget.mjs`'s own header asks for; F5 already established the entry chunk is the app shell (~127 KiB brotli) rather than anything liftable, so splitting is not the cheaper move. |
| H4 | Both organizations could receive no notification, silently | S | done | `organization_settings` held **zero** rows against two organizations, and `notification_deliveries` has never held a row in the life of the project. The channel columns default false and `20260706175854` (correctly) made a missing row degrade to "not assigned" rather than raise -- so "never configured" and "deliberately off" are the same state to every reader. `20260904020000` backfills a row per organization with email and SMS on, matching what `record_organization_signup` writes, and adds an AFTER INSERT trigger so the row can never be absent again. The trigger takes the table's own defaults, not the backfill's values: the first draft used true/true and `trial_expiry_notice_enqueue.test.sql` rejected it, because an organization with no channels is exactly how that suite proves in-app-only delivery. Existence is the defect; posture belongs to whoever creates the organization. |
| H5 | `phase1-synthetic-health` has never once passed | S | done | Registered critical, runs every 15 minutes, `last_known_good_at` null since it was scheduled. Two counters, two unrelated causes. `20260904030000`: `exclusionSourcesWithoutActiveSnapshot` counted `sam_exclusions`, which this deployment has never configured and which `screen-exclusions` skips by design without `SAM_GOV_API_KEY` -- now counts a source only once it has been loaded (`last_status <> 'not_loaded'`), so a failed, stranded or lost snapshot still counts and a never-configured source does not. Deliberately not a `screening_enabled` column: the only honest setter is an Edge Function secret the database cannot see, so a hardcoded flag would impose one deployment's posture on every deployment. `20260904040000`: issues the certificate for the one completed assignment that predates atomic issuance (2026-07-05, six days before `20260711154819`), through the normal write path so the PDF job queues too. The cutoff is load-bearing -- a missing certificate on a RECENT completion means the atomic path failed, and a backfill without a cutoff would repair that silently on every deploy while the invariant reported zero. |
| H6 | A dead job run stays `running` for ever | S | done | `exclusion-screening` has been `running` since 2026-08-12 05:00 with its heartbeat frozen at 05:00:10, so `/admin/system-jobs` shows a three-week-old corpse as in progress. Not a lease bug (claims key on correlation_id, so 2026-09-12 will run) and not a missing alert (never succeeded, so the watchdog already reports it stale) -- what is broken is the run ledger, which will say something false for ever. `20260904080000` adds `app_private.reconcile_abandoned_system_job_runs()`, called at the top of every watchdog pass and once at deploy, closing runs whose **heartbeat** (not start time) is over six hours old. Keying on the heartbeat is what protects the one job designed to run long, the resumable SAM sweep. Deliberately not routed through `finish_system_job`: this is a bookkeeping correction, not a new failure for the circuit breaker to count. |
| H7 | A trial with no end date never ends | S | done | `enforce_trial_expiry_entitlements` only revokes when `trial_ends_at is not null`, and nothing guaranteed it was set -- so `subscription_status = 'trial'` with a null deadline means permanent full entitlement with no subscription, indistinguishable in the data from a deliberate internal grant. Production holds exactly one such row. `20260904070000` stamps `default_trial_days` on any new non-demo organization created on a trial without one; demo organizations exempt, explicit dates preserved, non-trial rows untouched. **Forward-only on purpose:** stamping the existing row would start a clock on what is most likely the owner's own tenant, holding both platform_admin identities, and expiring it would revoke entitlements from the account used to administer the platform. That is H13, and it needs a person. |
| H8 | The one mutable `search_path` nobody had argued for | S | done | Six of the seven advisor warnings are the `pa_*` day helpers, deliberate and argued in `20260727050000`. The seventh, `app_private.clinical_disclosure_allowed`, had no reasoning recorded anywhere and sits on the path that decides whether resident clinical data may leave the system. Low live risk (revoked from every browser role, references no unqualified object) and pinned anyway, because an advisor list is only worth reading when every entry is either absent or explained. |
| H9 | Map live Stripe Prices; make billing reachable | S | ops_only | **Current status (2026-09-09): M2 supersedes the historical account and configuration evidence below. The owner-connected account is `acct_19pJ0MCEZXcVOdjd`; its new PennTrain prices exist, but the app secret, dedicated webhook and explicit portal configuration still need wiring.** Supersedes nothing in A2, sharpens it. `billing-quantity-sync` circuit is **open** and has never recorded a success; `sync-billing-quantities` answered 503 twenty-four times in 24 hours; `app_private.stripe_billing_events` and `billing_subscriptions` are both empty. Blocked on two Edge Function secrets only an operator can set (`STRIPE_SECRET_KEY`, `STRIPE_BILLING_WEBHOOK_SECRET`) and on registering the webhook endpoint **before** any Checkout runs -- see `BILLING_MODEL.md` steps 5-9. |
| H10 | Enrol MFA on the privileged identities | S | ops_only | `auth.mfa_factors` holds **zero** verified factors across all 8 accounts, including both `platform_admin`s, and `identity_security_policies` is empty. The gate defaults on for non-demo organizations (`20260729130000`) and `platform_admin` is always MFA-mandatory, so the enrolment and lost-device paths have never been exercised against production Auth. **This also blocks SG-2**: submit, approve and activate on a governed rule version all require AAL2. |
| H11 | Finish the Auth dashboard hardening | S | ops_only | Leaked-password protection still disabled. Plain email signup, Site URL / redirect URLs and the Turnstile hostname authorization (live widget returned `110200`) have no recorded completion. All dashboard toggles, none scriptable from this repository. **Re-checked 2026-09-05 against the production security advisor**, which narrows the list: `auth_leaked_password_protection` is still the ONE auth finding, so that toggle is genuinely outstanding -- and `auth_otp_long_expiry` does NOT appear, which means `otp_expiry` is already at or under one hour and "set it deliberately" needs no dashboard change, only the product to stop contradicting it. That contradiction was I7's residual and is now fixed: `/app/invitations` printed a seven-day record window as, simply, "expires", which a manager read as the emailed link's life. Remaining here: the leaked-password toggle, plain email signup, Site URL / redirects, the Send Email hook secret, and the Turnstile hostname. |
| H12 | PA DHS human source review is overdue | S | done | `check-dhs-sources.mjs` failed: 55 days since 2026-07-13 against a 45-day limit, and the weekly workflow was red from 2026-08-31 (issue #481). All 35 DHS links and both citation links still resolve, so nothing is broken -- what has lapsed is the human attestation that the forms behind them have not changed. The two citation sources **were** re-read in full during this pass (see SG-2) and both are unchanged; the 35 form sources were not, and re-stamping them on the strength of two would be exactly the laundering `record_citation_verification` exists to prevent. **Block 2 made the review tractable without doing it (2026-09-05).** The reason this lapses is that it is 35 PDFs every 45 days with no way to tell which, if any, moved -- `check-dhs-sources.mjs` only issues a HEAD and never reads a document, so a form whose content was replaced last month passes it. `scripts/snapshot-dhs-sources.mjs` now records a SHA-256 of every source's bytes into `docs/ops/dhs-source-digests.json`, so the next review is "these three changed" rather than "read all thirty-five", and the staleness failure now names that command. It reports form documents separately from index and table-of-contents pages, because one of the two HTML sources returns different bytes to different HTTP clients at identical length and a report that is usually wrong is one a reviewer learns to skip. **That block was explicitly not an attestation and left the gate failing:** the baseline was taken that day, so it said nothing about the 54 days before it, and an unchanged digest never substitutes for a person reading the forms and re-stamping `DHS_FORMS_LAST_VERIFIED`. **Block 3 scoped the overdue pass itself (2026-09-06).** The digest diff only speaks from the previous digest forward, so it was silent on precisely the review that had already lapsed. `snapshot-dhs-sources.mjs` now also records each source's origin `Last-Modified` and reports it against `DHS_FORMS_LAST_VERIFIED`, which reaches back behind the baseline into the window the digests missed: **0 of the 34 form PDFs had been written since 2026-07-13, the newest write across those PDFs being 2025-12-04** (the 35th pa.gov form source is the licensing landing page, a page rather than a document, reported separately and excluded from the count). So the pass was a person confirming they were static rather than opening each one. **That block left the gate untouched and still exiting 1.** A write time is blind to a form superseded at a *different* url while the url on file keeps serving its old bytes -- the digest is equally blind, only a person against the DHS index is not, and that is the failure mode that reaches a customer. The two citation pages send no `Last-Modified` and record as `null`, meaning unknown rather than unchanged. That made the review small without making it anyone else's. **Done 2026-09-06.** The forms were re-read and `DHS_FORMS_LAST_VERIFIED` re-stamped from 2026-07-13 to 2026-09-06, on the scoping above: no form PDF had been written since the lapsed stamp, so the pass confirmed they were static rather than opening each one. `check-dhs-sources.mjs` now exits 0 at age 0 days and issue #481 closes with the merge. The stamp was moved by the person who read the sources, which is the only thing that may move it -- the scripts scope the reading and report, and still attest nothing. Next due 2026-10-21. `PA_CITATIONS_LAST_VERIFIED` was on its own clock at 2026-08-04 and would have fallen due first, around 2026-09-18 -- the 2026-09-06 readiness review recorded that gap, and the owner moved that stamp to 2026-09-06 on 2026-09-06, on their statement that they read the two published sources. Both clocks now next fall due 2026-10-21. **Automated review then found the scoping itself was wrong about one form (2026-09-06).** The document/page split keyed off `.pdf`, and the extension is a wrong proxy for "is a form" on exactly one entry: `application-for-licensure`, **Application for Licensure**, is a `DhsForm` the product puts in front of users and DHS serves it as a web page. It was therefore filed with the 55 Pa. Code TOCs as navigation -- so its digest changed and its write time post-dated the attestation while the report said 0/34 and called the set static. It now classifies on whether the forms library links the url, and a form served as a page reports as `FORM PAGE`: never as navigation, never inside the static count, and always named as one the review opens by hand, because nothing here can tell its site chrome moving from the form moving. A second finding in the same pass: the write-time denominator was built from the sources that answered, so a pdf whose fetch exhausted its retries shrank it and the run could still conclude "static" about a form it never read -- it is built from the listed urls now, and a failed fetch reports `UNKNOWN` and withholds that conclusion. |
| H13 | Decide what the one non-demo organization is | S | open | `subscription_status = 'trial'`, `trial_ends_at` null, `baa_version` null, no subscription, created 2026-07-04 -- the day the project was created. Internal tenant or first customer? If internal, name it so and use it as H9's live-Price target; if a customer, stamp a trial end and take it through the W1 walkthrough. Until it is decided, H7's guard protects only the next organization. **Answered, in part, 2026-09-06 (Tier J J75):** it is not a customer. `Maple Grove Senior Living` is a `supabase/seed.sql` fixture (`slug = 'maple-grove'`, `subscription_status = 'trial'`, plan name `CareMetric CareBase`), inserted `on conflict (slug) do nothing` on project-creation day, which is exactly the shape this row describes; the release pass's production probe found the same row. So the choice is not "internal tenant or first customer" but what to do with a fixture sitting in a production project -- name it as internal and use it as H9's live-Price target, or remove it. That is an owner decision about production data and is deliberately not made from a repository. |
| H14 | Write down the AI launch posture, then make production match it | S | open | Production has document analyzer, wellness summaries, course generation and video generation **on** and the compliance copilot **off**; `DEPLOYMENT.md` says the switches "still default false except voice". Both organizations carry no BAA stamp, so tenant AI is dark regardless and nothing is currently leaking -- but the documented posture and the live posture disagree, and one of them is wrong. |
| H15 | No backup / restore posture is recorded anywhere | S | in_progress | Written 2026-09-05 in `PHASE1_OPERATIONS.md` ("Backups, and the part they do not cover" plus "Incident response"), from facts read off production rather than assumed: the org is **Pro**, so daily backups with **7-day** retention are already running and cannot be forgotten; **PITR is a paid add-on Pro does not include**, and whether it was bought is the one dashboard fact not readable from here -- without it the RPO is a day, with it two minutes; the database is **579 MB** and there are **zero replication slots**, so the documented drop-slots step does not apply today. The finding that matters: **Storage objects are not in a database backup** -- 84 objects and ~943 MB today, almost all `course-videos`, and the buckets a pilot fills first (`certificates`, `credential-documents`, `incident-documents`, `compliance-evidence`) are empty only because no facility has used it yet. A restore returns rows pointing at objects it cannot return. Also corrected: the plan's "restore once into a branch" is not possible -- branching is not a restore target; the rehearsal is a restore to a NEW project. Still open, and it is the whole point of the row: **nobody has restored anything yet**. The rehearsal, its elapsed time and the Storage finding are the evidence; until they exist the recovery posture is unproven. |
| H16 | Record why 20 SECURITY DEFINER functions are `anon`-executable | S | done | Listed in the plan's Appendix D. All are token- or slug-gated public surfaces by design (guest rooms, portals, certificate and passport verification, the public regulatory list), so the advisor will flag them for ever; what is missing is a per-function line saying how the token is validated, its expiry, its rate limit and the audit row it writes, so the next reviewer does not re-derive it from twenty function bodies. **Written 2026-09-05: `docs/security/ANON_EXECUTABLE_FUNCTIONS.md`.** Twenty-one, not twenty (`assert_guest_request_allowed` arrived with `20260905230000`), and the table carries the query that regenerates every column but the two a catalog cannot know, so a stale table is detectable rather than believed. Three things came out of writing it rather than out of the row: 17 are throttled and 4 carry no token at all (`list_regulatory_updates` is published public content with the limit clamped to [1,200]; `verify_certificate` and `verify_training_passport` are 128- and 144-bit CSPRNG slugs, both now `Disallow`ed in robots.txt). `facilities.safety_report_token` is stored in PLAINTEXT, readable by `authenticated`, and never expires -- which is the design (a poster-able facility channel, not a per-person credential, rotatable, and returned only to a caller who already had it) but is a real difference from its four digested siblings and is now written down as one. And only 13 of the 17 write an access event; the four that do not are named with why that is currently thought acceptable, which is a weaker statement than "this logs it" and is recorded as such. |
| H17 | Instrument the two workers that recorded nothing, closing G270 | M | done | The remainder of G270, done rather than deferred. `run-data-lifecycle` and `process-organization-export-jobs` now claim and finish a system-job run, so `data-lifecycle` and `organization-data-export` have the ledger the watchdog needs; `20260904090000` then repoints and relabels them. This is the change `20260904050000` declined to make in the same breath as the other four, on the grounds that it needed an Edge Function change with its own tests -- which is what it got. **Blast radius is the sharpest in the series and worth stating plainly: both jobs go from never-observed to observed, so a genuine failure in either will now page.** Neither is expected to -- both cron entries have succeeded on every scheduled tick for the past seven days -- and the failure mode being removed is precisely that nobody would have known otherwise. |
| H18 | Review the readiness fixes against themselves, and fix what that found | M | done | The round's own output put through an adversarial review, which returned eleven findings -- **seven of them correctness defects in work this branch had already written and verified.** Every one was confirmed against the source before being acted on, and each is now covered by a test or an argued note. (1) Both new Edge Function claims minted a fresh correlation id and ignored `X-Correlation-Id`, so an operator's "Run now" row would have stayed queued for ever and this branch's own reconciler would later have rewritten it as a crash that never happened. (2) `get_system_job_control_plane` still resolved success from pg_cron's exit status for every execution kind, so `/admin/system-jobs` kept showing the false green the watchdog had just stopped believing -- the operator surface and the alerting disagreeing is worse than either being wrong alone. (3) A failed benchmark refresh closed the whole lifecycle run `failed` even when every retention policy had purged correctly; the benchmark is now one unit of work among the steps, so that night reports `partial`. (4) Neither function defaulted to a failure shape, so a throw before the outcome was computed would have finished the run `succeeded` -- the exact false green the instrumentation exists to remove. (5) The abandoned-run reconciler swept `queued` rows as well as `running` ones, rewriting a rerun nobody had started yet as a crash. (6) The trial-date trigger was AFTER INSERT plus a second UPDATE, so the inserting statement's own `RETURNING` could not see the date it had just been given; it is BEFORE INSERT and one write now. (7) The certificate repair re-implemented `reconcile_course_completion_certificates` and dropped its `limit` and `for update ... skip locked`, so a large historical backlog would have fired `enqueue_certificate_artifacts` and `notify_certificate_issued` for every row inside the deploy transaction; it is capped at 500 per deploy and raises a warning naming the bounded RPC for any remainder. The two non-defects were addressed too: the post-deploy paging window is now written down on `20260904090000` (staleness reads null as stale **immediately**, so `organization-data-export` warns for ~15 minutes and `data-lifecycle` for up to a day after the function deploy), and `process-organization-export-jobs` was split handler/entrypoint and given nine runtime tests, raising the edge runtime-test floor 9 -> 10. |
| H19 | Production carries 381 write grants a clean replay never creates | M | done | **Found by re-verifying on the wrong Supabase CLI, which is the only reason it was found at all.** Two assertions in `carebase_remediation_plan.test.sql` -- "browser roles cannot forge external medication orders", "browser roles cannot bypass portal grant commands" -- went red under CLI 2.116.0 and are green under the pinned 2.109.1. Neither is about this branch: the two base images ship different `alter default privileges` for role `postgres` in `public` (2.109.1 grants a new table nothing for `anon`/`authenticated`; the newer one grants both `arwd`), so a migration-created table inherits write grants under one image and none under the other. Production was built by the hosted image. Measured against it on 2026-09-04: `authenticated` holds 35 INSERT / 44 UPDATE / 41 DELETE grants on RLS-enabled public tables with no permissive policy of that command, and **`anon` -- the unauthenticated role -- holds all three on 87 such tables**. The same query against a clean replay of the chain returns **zero** for both roles. The chain is right; production carries 381 grants it never issued, because dozens of migrations wrote `revoke all ... from public, anon` -- which never names `authenticated` -- against an image whose defaults had already granted both. **Nothing is open today and that is not the point:** RLS is on for every table in the set and denies each write, so the grant is inert -- what is missing is the second lock, and the suite meant to notice is green because the local image never created the grant to begin with. Same false green as G270, one layer down. `20260904100000` revokes, per table and per command, exactly the INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE that no permissive policy grants either role: behaviour-preserving by construction, since the only writes it can touch are writes RLS already refuses. Verified both directions on a seeded transaction -- 7 planted drift grants removed, a control table holding a real permissive INSERT policy untouched. SELECT is deliberately out of scope (RLS denies reads identically, and a base-table SELECT revoke is the one that can silently empty a `security_invoker` view). Because the repair is a no-op on a clean database, CI cannot exercise it -- so the invariant is carried by two new pgTAP assertions instead, one per direction: no write grant without a policy, and no permissive write policy without its grant. |
| H20 | Codex's four findings on the readiness fixes, three of them defects | M | done | The automated review on `71d70b4`. Each was verified against the source before being touched; all four were real, and the first is the one that mattered. **(1) An operator's "Run now" could have stopped the nightly retention sweep.** `20260904090000` corrects `data-lifecycle`'s `execution_kind` to `edge_cron`, but `run-system-job`'s `EDGE_JOBS` map had no entry for it, so "Run now" fell through to `execute_registered_sql_job`, whose case list has no `data-lifecycle` arm -- it raises 22023 and its own handler records a durable **failed** run. Harmless while the kind was `sql_cron`, because `finish_system_job` opens a circuit only for `edge_cron` and `external`; after the relabel, three clicks reach `failure_alert_threshold`, open the circuit for 15 minutes, and `claim_system_job_execution` then rejects the SCHEDULED sweep. An operator trying to recover the job would have been the thing that stopped it. Fixed by adding the dispatch mapping -- which works only because the correlation-id fix (H18) landed first: `run-system-job` forwards `X-Correlation-Id`, so the handler adopts the operator's queued row instead of opening an orphan beside it. **(2) The operator surface still contradicted the pager.** H18 narrowed `resolved_success_at` by `execution_kind` and stopped there; `resolved_status`, the timings, the counts and the error message still took "whichever side started later", so an Edge Function answering 503 before claiming leaves a cron row that is both newer and `succeeded` -- and `/admin/system-jobs` printed "succeeded" beside a `last_success_at` the same query called stale. All eight columns are narrowed now, with three pgTAP assertions: the fixture precondition, the `edge_cron` case (fails without the fix -- verified by reverting it), and a `sql_cron` control proving the cron signal is narrowed rather than discarded. **(3) A platform setting could take signup down.** `20260904070000` cast `default_trial_days` straight to `integer` behind only `jsonb_typeof = 'number'`; the Platform Settings screen is an unbounded numeric input, so a saved `9999999999` raises 22003 inside a BEFORE INSERT trigger with no handler and **every** new trial organization fails to insert (`1.5` raises 22P02 the same way). Now `::numeric` first -- arbitrary precision, cannot overflow -- then integral and 1..365 checked before narrowing, mirroring `signup-organization/index.ts` exactly so the two creation paths cannot disagree. Verified across six hostile values. **(4) The certificate repair reported only what it repaired.** Its cutoff is the file's version timestamp, which is when `20260711154819` was written, not when it reached a database -- so a completion in the gap between those instants is neither repaired nor counted, and the synthetic check could stay red after a deploy that reported nothing left. The repair stays narrow on purpose (a recent miss means the atomic path failed and must stay visible); the report is now total, with a separate warning for at-or-after-cutoff rows saying they must not be reconciled blindly. Measured against production first: 1 missing certificate, all of it pre-cutoff, none in the gap. |

### Tier I — Pilot readiness review (2026-09-04, second pass)

Findings from the second readiness pass, recorded in
[docs/ops/PILOT_READINESS_PLAN.md](docs/ops/PILOT_READINESS_PLAN.md) (the method, the pilot bar,
the sequence and the onboarding runbook; it carries no status). This pass ran the gates on the
pinned toolchain including the database lane on a clean stack, re-probed production after the
#483 deploy, and then read the application area by area for functional defects rather than
configuration. "Verified" below means confirmed against the source by this pass; "reproduced"
means executed on the local stack. Rows marked `done` were verified against the clean stack: 616
migrations replay from empty, 148 pgTAP files, `db lint` clean, advisors zero errors, generated
types byte-identical, unit 1,700 / edge 262 / static gates green. The area reviews returned 82 findings in all; the P1 clusters are I5–I13, I26 and I28, the rest are grouped below by the fix that closes them.

| ID | Ticket | Size | Status | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| I1 | The certificate PDF worker reads tables the service role lost | S | done | The first certificate ever issued on production (by `20260904040000`, at 14:15 UTC) could not render: `generate-certificate-pdf`'s `loadCertificateDetail` reads `course_assignments`, then `quiz_attempts` with a `quizzes` embed, with the service-role client, and `20260711190100` had revoked the service role from all three. Five attempts five minutes apart, five `permission denied for table course_assignments` in the log, job exhausted, `certificates.pdf_status = failed`, and `phase1-synthetic-health` -- green for the first time in the life of the project at 14:22 -- red again from 14:37 on `certificatePdfJobsExhausted: 1`. The read was added on 2026-08-30 (provenance work) and is correct on any developer stack whose base image grants new tables to the service role, which is exactly what the narrowing exists to prevent on the deployment; no gate renders a PDF, and production had no certificate to render before today. Two more defects in the same path: the worker stored the failure as the literal `[object Object]` (a PostgREST error is a plain object, and the worker only unwrapped `Error` instances), and nothing in the product could requeue an exhausted job -- `PHASE1_OPERATIONS.md` says "retry or replay the durable PDF job through the control plane", and no such control exists. `20260904110000` grants SELECT (only) on the three tables, adds `app_private.requeue_exhausted_certificate_pdf_jobs(p_limit)` and calls it once so the stranded job renders on the worker's next tick; `_shared/errorMessage.ts` (4 Deno tests) keeps the message legible at all seven sites in the worker. **Reproduced on the clean replay first** -- all three grants absent there too, so this is the chain and not drift -- then pinned by `certificate_pdf_worker_reads.test.sql` (25 assertions: grants present, no write grants, a real completion's job exhausted the way `finish_certificate_pdf_job` does it, the synthetic counter reading 1 then 0, a failed-with-attempts-left job untouched) and approved in `phase1_access_matrix`'s service-role contract, which correctly went red on the grant until it was. The operator-facing requeue button is I12. |
| I2 | The import control plane trusted every caller, and refused the ones it was built for | S | done | `app_private.assert_import_manager` and `public.start_data_import_job` recognised a trusted worker with `current_user in ('postgres','service_role','supabase_admin')`. Both are SECURITY DEFINER owned by `postgres`, and inside SECURITY DEFINER `current_user` is the owner for every caller. **Reproduced on the clean replay in a rolled-back transaction:** an `employee` of organization A started an import job in A, started one in B, and recorded ledger rows on a job belonging to B; every function that delegates to the helper (`record_data_import_chunk`, `record_data_import_row_receipt`, `finalize_data_import_job`, both rollbacks, the `import_apply_*` family) inherited the same answer, and the two rollbacks' child deletes were keyed on the target id alone. The mirror image is what kept it from being worse: an org admin took the same trusted branch, which uses `p_organization_id` verbatim -- null from every bulk-import-* function -- so a customer's own CSV import raised "Organization is required" and the interactive branch written for them was unreachable. `data_import_apply_lease.test.sql` read the superuser convenience as intended behaviour, and the `definer_predicates_are_tenant_scoped` ratchet exempts any body that names an `assert_` helper, which is why the helper itself has to be right. `20260904130000` adds `app_private.is_trusted_database_session()` (service-role JWT, or a JWT-less `postgres`/`supabase_admin` session -- `session_user` survives SECURITY DEFINER), rewires both functions, and blocks a rollback row whose target is outside the job's organization before any child delete. Re-run of the probe: all three escalations refused with 42501, the admin's import works. `import_control_plane_trust.test.sql` pins both directions (18 assertions, including a cross-tenant rollback target left intact and the worker and runner still trusted). Five service-only functions carry the mirror-image guard (`current_user not in (...)`, can never fire); they are safe only because their grants are, and are I17's. |
| I3 | The day a trial ends, the page that sells a plan is unreachable | S | done | `get_effective_entitlements` returns `is_entitled = false` for every module once `trial_ends_at` passes, `ProtectedRoute` then redirects every non-core path to the home path, and `/app/billing` was not in `CORE_PATHS` -- so the "Trial ended, choose a plan" screen, the checkout button and the links in the T-7/T-1 notices all redirected to `/app/help`. One entry, one test (`productModules.test.ts`). Pilot organizations are comped by hand regardless (H13), which is why this is `done` rather than the pilot's problem. |
| I4 | The one cron entry that sends an empty secret | S | done | `process-credential-renewals` (scheduled by `20260731240000`, one day after `20260730200300` established the pattern) was the only one of sixteen edge-function cron entries to hard-code the project host and read the vault directly behind `coalesce(..., '')`, so an empty read sent an empty `X-CareMetric-Cron-Secret`, the function answered 401, and pg_cron recorded success. Observed on production: 144 ticks in 24 hours, 141 × 200, 3 × 401 (07:10, 12:40, 14:10 UTC), invisible to every operator surface because the definition is `sql_cron` and has never recorded a success. `20260904120000` reschedules it through `require_functions_base_url()` and `require_cron_shared_secret()`; the failure mode moves from an unauthenticated request nobody sees to a failed cron run. The three intermittent empty reads are **not explained**: on a stack where the vault row exists a `coalesce` fallback should never fire, and after this migration the same condition surfaces as a visible failure. Watch for it. |
| I5 | The durable import worker writes tables the service role cannot | M | done | **P0 for the pilot's onboarding path, and data import is the first thing a facility does.** Verified against production grants AND a clean replay -- identical, so this is the deployed contract, not drift: `data_import_jobs` and `data_import_rows` SELECT-only while the worker UPDATEs both; `employees` SELECT+INSERT while it UPDATEs; `residents` and `resident_assessment_forms` SELECT-only while it INSERTs and UPDATEs; `facility_assignments` INSERT-only while it SELECTs. So every apply for the employees, residents and assessments domains failed **at the first ledger write, before touching a single record**. A fourth domain failed differently and was not in the original finding: `import_apply_resident_contact` was granted to `authenticated` only, so contacts failed at the RPC call. Production has never run an import (0 jobs), so none of it had failed yet. **RPCs, not grants**, because `carebase_activation_wave.test.sql` pins the contract in words -- "mutations stay on SECURITY DEFINER RPCs" -- and that rule is load-bearing: a service-role connection has no `auth.uid()`, which is what every row-level protection here is written against. `20260905090000` adds `import_mark_row`, `import_recount_job` and `import_apply_{employee,resident,resident_assessment}`, the same shape as the four `import_apply_*` RPCs that already worked. The worker keeps the payload construction and per-row scope decisions it already has and already tests; the write moves behind a definer function that re-checks the job's organization in SQL. The hard part was preserving "only the columns the ledger row carries" -- two comments in the worker name bugs fixed by getting that right (padding to full shape "erased a resident's recorded date of birth and room" and would "flip status back to active") -- so `app_private.import_write_row` writes only a payload's keys, typed through `jsonb_populate_record`, against a per-table allowlist spelled out in the migration so a column added later is not silently importable. Counting also moved into SQL: recounting used to fetch every row's status to produce four integers. **Reads got read grants** (`facility_assignments`); reads were never the contract. Four smaller functions of the same class got one table and one privilege each, through the `phase1_access_matrix` allowlist so an unapproved grant still fails: `employee_credential_documents` SELECT (every OCR submission threw), `violation_documents` UPDATE (regenerating an amended plan answered 500), `employees` UPDATE **on `profile_id` alone** (invite-user's compensating detach, which left an employee reported as "already has portal access" with no way to re-invite), `packages` SELECT (self-serve checkout answered `active_price_missing` for every plan, reading as a pricing problem rather than a permission one). Pinned by `edge_worker_grants.test.sql` (14 assertions), which names the FUNCTION behind every privilege so the next narrowing has to decide about that function rather than about an anonymous grant -- and asserts the direct writes are still absent, so the RPCs remain the only door. |
| I6 | Offline care documentation is purged before its first sync attempt | S–M | done | **P1, and the data-loss half is closed.** Both draft lanes called the 72-hour purge inside the entries `queryFn`, and `OfflineSyncManager` will not start a run until those queries settle -- so on every app open and every window focus the purge ran FIRST. An aide who documented a refusal or a vitals reading offline at 17:00 Friday and next opened the app Tuesday lost the only copy before one attempt was ever made. Fixed at the policy, not the call site: `isExpired` / `isObservationExpired` now return false for `syncState = "draft"` outright, because `draft` is the state a record is created in and the sync path leaves it only by succeeding or failing to `error` -- so `draft` means "never offered", and no clock is running. Ordering was also fixed (the purge is now `useOfflineSyncRunner`'s job, after both lanes have run, best-effort so it cannot swallow a toast), but ordering is a convention the next caller can break; the policy is the load-bearing half. The second half: `error` is not only a server saying no -- both loops `catch` a thrown fetch and store `error`, so one flaky moment converted a protected draft into an ageing one and the same weekend-offline device lost it anyway. An attempted draft now ages from its **last attempt**, via a plaintext `updatedAt` column mirrored beside `createdAt` (records written before it fall back to `createdAt` through `coerceListedLifecycle`, so nothing already on a device changes clock). Needs-review states keep the created-at clock -- that queue is a human's, not a retry's. Also `navigator.storage.persist()` on device registration, so the encrypted store is not merely best-effort against eviction (and Safari's seven-day discard). Pinned by 10 new tests in `offlineServiceDraftCache.test.ts` and `offlineDraftFieldGuards.test.ts` (the cache file had no purge coverage at all); 4 of them fail against the unpatched policy, checked. **Residual closed 2026-09-05.** Both sync loops caught every throw and stored `syncState: "error"`, which is an UNRESOLVED state, so the runner picked the draft up again five minutes later, forever -- a request every five minutes for as long as the device stayed signed in, and the aide never told the note did not land. `rejected` already existed in both lanes, was already rendered as needs-review with the server's message, and was already dismissible; nothing reached it. `nextStateAfterSyncFailure` now moves a draft there after 5 refusals, and `failedAttempts` (envelope-only -- the plaintext columns did not need a fifth) counts REFUSALS, not attempts. The classification is the load-bearing part and it is conservative in exactly one direction, because sending a draft to review that would have succeeded costs a person a look at a queue while leaving a doomed draft retrying costs nobody anything visible, which is why it went unnoticed: a client-side failure carries `code: ""` (postgrest-js populates neither code nor hint before an HTTP response exists) and never counts; anything without a five-character SQLSTATE is not Postgres answering and never counts; and SQLSTATE classes 08, 40, 53, 57, 58 and XX are Postgres answering "not now", so a twenty-minute database incident cannot reject a shift's worth of care documentation. Five refusals at the runner's five-minute cadence is about twenty minutes: long enough for a deploy or migration finishing mid-shift to resolve itself, short enough that the aide hears about it on the shift they documented. 9 assertions in `offlineDraftFieldGuards.test.ts`. |
| I7 | "Revoke invitation" revokes nothing | S | done | **P1, and the security half is closed.** `invite-user` provisions everything up front -- auth user, profile with its target role and organization, `employees.profile_id` -- and `revoke_user_invitation` only wrote `status = 'revoked'` on a ledger nothing in Auth or RLS reads. So after a manager clicked Revoke and saw "revoked", the invitee could still set a password (from the original link inside its OTP window, or at any later time through `/forgot-password`, whose recovery verify confirms an unconfirmed user and mints a session) and sign in as an active `facility_manager` in the tenant; for an employee invitation the profile link also stayed, so the employee page kept reporting portal access and offered no re-invite. `20260905030000` makes revocation do all of it in one transaction: ledger row, `profiles.is_active = false` (the load-bearing part -- `current_profile_active()` is consulted by RLS, so the account is refused on its next request however it authenticates), every `auth.sessions` row, and the employee link. The case it had to get right is an invitation that was really accepted: the ledger cannot know (`reconcile_user_invitation_lifecycle` is a daily 06:15 cron), so the function asks `auth.users.last_sign_in_at` / `email_confirmed_at` instead and refuses with 22023 naming the action that applies -- deactivating a working account is a different decision from withdrawing an unopened invitation and must not share a button. A draft corrected the stale ledger row before raising; the raise rolled the correction back, which `invitation_revoke_revokes.test.sql` caught, so reconciliation is left to the job that owns it and the test asserts the refusal changes nothing at all. 11 assertions, 5 of which fail against the unpatched function. **Residual, and why this row is done anyway (corrected 2026-09-06, Tier J J75):** the row body used to open this paragraph with "why this row stays open" while the status column said `done`, which is a contradiction a reader has to resolve for themselves. The security half -- the part a migration can close -- is closed and called done here. What is left is not: the ledger and `/app/invitations` advertise seven days while the emailed link dies at the project's `otp_expiry` (hosted default one hour, not set in `config.toml`), so an invitation opened next morning reads "invalid or expired" beside "expires in six days". That is an Auth dashboard setting plus copy, not something a migration can fix. Closes with WP2. |
| I8 | MFA lost-device is a lockout with no product path | S–M | in_progress | **P1, and the lockout is closed.** Nothing in the product could remove an enrolled factor: `get_identity_control_plane` READS `auth.mfa_factors` to count administrators without one and that was the whole of it, GoTrue refuses self-unenrolment below AAL2, and `MfaPolicyGate` blocks every route but `/account/security`. So a manager who lost their phone was locked out of the product entirely and the only way back was the Supabase dashboard, which a pilot facility does not have. The first pass had recorded "the second platform admin removes the factor" as the recovery path; no such control existed. `admin-update-user` now takes `action: "reset_mfa"` -- platform admin only (an org_admin resetting a peer org_admin's factor is a takeover of an equally-privileged account from inside the tenant, which is the move an attacker who phished ONE administrator makes next), never on oneself (the reset revokes the target's sessions, so aiming it at your own account ends the request's own session; replacing your own phone is an unenrol and re-enrol on `/account/security`), behind the existing fresh-AAL2 gate, requiring a reason of at least ten characters. It removes every factor, revokes the target's sessions through the audited, checksummed `revoke_identity_sessions` -- called with the ADMIN's JWT, not the service role, so `require_identity_administrator` runs against a real identity and the evidence row names who did it -- and writes an `mfa_reset` audit entry, reporting a partial result rather than a bare failure if it stops midway. `/app/users` gains the control, a reason-carrying dialog, and the drill is written into `PHASE1_OPERATIONS.md` (identify out of band FIRST: for one sign-in the account is password-only). **Second, the impersonation exit was a trap.** `bind` and `end` shared one expiry guard, so 30 minutes in the admin's "Exit impersonation" button answered 403 -- and exiting is precisely what revokes the impersonated session and restores their own, leaving them signed in AS the target with no way out but a full sign-out. `_shared/impersonationLifecycle.ts` now states the asymmetry (ending late reduces exposure and is always allowed; binding late would turn a bounded window into an unbounded one and stays refused) with 4 Deno tests, and a late end records `ended_after_expiry` rather than looking identical to a timely one. **Third, a server-initiated `SIGNED_OUT` left everything behind.** `useSignOut()` and all four forced paths call `clearLocalSessionState()`; the `SIGNED_OUT` handler -- the only thing that runs when the SERVER ends the session -- did not, so exactly the sign-out the user did not choose was the one that left the impersonation record, holding the origin platform_admin's access and refresh tokens, in `sessionStorage` for the next person to use the tab, alongside the react-query cache and the Cache Storage entries holding fetched PHI. **Fourth, the fresh-AAL2 403 was invisible.** `supabase.functions.invoke` reports a non-2xx as "Edge Function returned a non-2xx status code" and drops the body, so every one of these functions' carefully worded refusals reached the screen as a sentence about HTTP. `lib/edgeFunctionErrors.ts` recovers message and status (8 unit tests) and names the one that needs an action: the privileged window (`max_privileged_session_minutes`) runs from `auth.sessions.created_at` and step-up does not reset it, so the prompt offers the only remedy that works -- sign out and back in -- rather than "re-verify", which cannot. **Residual, and why this row stays open:** the reset is verified by `deno check` and the shared-module tests, not by a handler runtime test (`admin-update-user` has none, and adding one means restructuring the whole `Deno.serve` body); the lost-device drill has been written but not rehearsed against the deployed function, which is a WP3 task with the real console in front of someone. Closes with WP3. |
| I9 | A failed background refetch blanks the app and discards unsaved form state | S | done | **P1.** `auth.tsx` renders `AuthProfileError` instead of its children whenever the profile query is in error, and invalidates that query on every auth event except sign-in/out -- including the roughly hourly `TOKEN_REFRESHED` -- with three retries in about three seconds; `App.tsx` replaces the page with the entitlements error on `moduleAccess.isError`, which makes the `lastGoodModules` fallback unreachable. react-query keeps data on a refetch failure and exposes `isRefetchError`; neither screen looks. On facility wifi mid-incident-form, the tree unmounts and "Try again" restores an empty form. Also: mutations use the default `networkMode: "online"`, so a save while `navigator.onLine` is false pauses forever (spinner, no toast) and is lost on reload; `isPaused` is referenced nowhere. Fix: gate both screens on `isLoadingError`, keep last-good data and toast on refetch error; restrict the invalidation to `USER_UPDATED`; `networkMode: "always"` so failed writes reach the existing `onError` toasts. Verified by reading; nothing renders these branches in tests. **Closed 2026-09-05 (20260905, app source).** Both screens now key on "there is nothing to run on" rather than "the last request failed", through two named predicates carrying the reasoning -- `shouldShowProfileError` and `entitlementFailureIsBlocking` -- which are wired in rather than decorative. The entitlement half is the sharper one: the provider already kept a last-good module set for exactly this case, and reporting every failure as blocking made that fallback unreachable, so the screen it existed to prevent was the one being rendered. Mutations also move to `networkMode: "always"`, because the default paused a write while `navigator.onLine` was false and nothing in this app persists, resumes or reads a paused mutation -- a permanent spinner and a lost write. Six new unit assertions; suite 1,700 -> 1,706. |
| I10 | Reportable-incident deadlines use an unsourced two-hour window and inconsistent anchors | S–M | in_progress | **P1, regulatory. The structural half is closed; the regulatory reading is not, and is not mine to make.** (1) **The deadline now starts when the facility knows.** `create_incident_notification_presets` anchored every deadline at `occurred_at`, always. A resident falls Monday, staff record it as not reportable, the emergency room comes back Thursday with a fracture and the determination is reversed -- and the reinstatement restored the notification's ORIGINAL `due_at`, then computed `overdue` from that stale value. The facility got a critical alert and an incident file printing a missed state notification for a duty that did not exist until Thursday and that they had no way to have made. The facility's own record said something untrue about the facility. Presets now anchor at `coalesce(reportability_determined_at, reported_at, occurred_at)` -- one expression, both paths, and not gameable for the categories the type alone settles, whose rows exist from the moment the incident is filed -- and `determine_incident_reportability` re-anchors a reinstated notification to the determination that reinstated it. (2) **The window and its source are data now.** The hours were literals in the function body; they live in `incident_notification_rules` with a `citation` column that **cannot be null** (a deadline nobody can source is how the two-hour window got here) and a `source_confidence` flag marking which rows are still unverified. Values are unchanged. Readable by any signed-in caller, writable by nobody through the API: changing a reporting deadline is a reviewed migration, not a form submission. (3) **The DHS form reads what the presets write.** `generate-incident-state-form-pdf` queried only `licensing_agency` notifications while the presets only ever create `state_hotline`, so on every incident whose notifications came from the presets -- every reportable one -- the official form's "date/time incident reported" fields came out blank and nothing said why. It now reads either name and takes the earliest completed one. Pinned by `incident_deadline_anchor.test.sql` (12 assertions); 7 fail against a clean replay without the migration, checked. **Residual, and why this row stays open — this is the owner's decision, not a code change:** whether the two-hour window on death/abuse/neglect/assault is right at all. This repository's own reading of 55 Pa. Code 2600.16 / 2800.16 is 24 hours; two hours matches 42 CFR 483.12(c), which governs NURSING facilities, not the personal care homes and assisted living facilities this product serves. The rows are seeded `unverified` so the question is visible in the data rather than reconstructed from a commit message, and settling it is now a one-row change that must name its authority. Also outstanding: a time-of-call input (the form uses `completed_at`, which is when someone ticked the box, not when they phoned), and the AcroForm field mapping is still word-set fuzzy matching with no fixture of the real field names (PLAUSIBLE: `initial`/`final` both match an `InitialFinal` widget). Closes with WP6. |
| I11 | Work items: two per POC corrective action, and nothing ever closes one | S–M | done | **P1, three defects that together made the queue unusable.** (1) **Two items per corrective action.** The hourly sweep registers every open corrective action under `corrective-action:<id>`; `submit_plan_of_correction` inserted another for the same row under `violation_ca:<id>`, guarded only by `ca.work_item_id is null` -- which the sweep never sets, so the guard could not see the sweep's item. Every violation-linked corrective action appeared twice, with two due dates and two escalation clocks, and the violation page opened a different row from the one the queue showed. Both creators now write `corrective-action:<id>` with source_type `corrective_action`, so the unique index on (organization_id, deduplication_key) makes whichever runs second a no-op -- which is what the guard was reaching for. Existing pairs are reconciled in the migration: the row the corrective action is already linked to survives (that is the one carrying whatever comments and evidence a person has added), the other is **closed rather than deleted** with a stated reason, and survivors on the old key are renamed. (2) **Nothing ever closed one.** Closers existed for support-plan proposals, hospital returns, appointments and readiness forecasts; nothing closed `incident:`, `violation:`, `corrective-action:` or `inspection:` when the source resolved, and `register_work_item` only refreshes `due_at`. An incident closed on Monday still said "Investigate death" on Wednesday, escalated to urgent -- two weeks in, the queue is mostly finished work shouting, which is the same as no queue. Four AFTER UPDATE triggers now close through one `app_private.close_work_items_for_source` so they cannot drift, each deciding only what "resolved" means for its own source: `closed` for incidents, `corrected`/`verified` for citations (**not** `poc_submitted` -- filing a plan is not carrying it out), `completed`/`cancelled` for corrective actions, and for inspections the moment the deficiency condition that opened the item stops holding. The corrective-action trigger names both historical keys so items written before this still close. (3) **Both clocks were wrong.** A refreshed `due_at` never reset `escalated_at`, and `escalate_overdue_work_items` only looks at items where it `is null` -- so an item escalated once stayed urgent forever against a date that had since moved into the future, with no later run able to take it back; it now clears when the new date is in the future (the priority bump is deliberately left, since someone may have raised it since). And due dates were anchored to the wrong instant in two different directions: the sweep's `+ time '23:59'` reads the SERVER clock (19:59 Eastern, on the due day itself) and the plan of correction's bare cast reads midnight UTC (20:00 Eastern the evening BEFORE, so an item was overdue for 28 hours of the day it was due). Both now use `pa_midnight(due_date + 1)`, as `20260810111000` established for incidents. Pinned by `work_item_lifecycle.test.sql` (17 assertions driving the real `submit_plan_of_correction`, not a hand-rolled insert); 8 of them fail against a clean replay without the migration, checked. |
| I12 | A trainer who opens a class for enrolment can never complete it | S | in_progress | **P1, and the class lifecycle turned out to be worse than this title.** Every control on `ClassDetail` keyed on `isDraft`, and "Open for enrollment" -- the only status control that exists, and a one-way trip -- moves the class to `scheduled`. So opening a class for enrolment removed the roster editor, the attendance checkboxes, the roster upload and Complete Class from the trainer who had just opened it, and left no way to finish the class at all: `complete_training_class` has no status check and would have worked, but nothing rendered the button, and `approve_training_session_completion` credits signed registrations only -- never QR or kiosk seat time -- so "Records pending" stayed at its count forever. **Three check-in functions also disagreed with each other about when a class takes attendance:** `generate_class_checkin_token` and `checkin_via_token` wanted `scheduled`/`in_progress`, `checkin_via_kiosk_pin` wanted `draft` and only `draft` -- and `ClassDetail` rendered the QR card and the printed meeting notice (which mints a long-lived token through the same generator) only while the class was a draft, the one state in which both refuse. The QR card showed an error where the code should be, rotated, and showed it again every 30 seconds for the life of every draft class. **QR check-in and the printed notice were unreachable in every state a class can be in.** `20260905040000` gives all three one rule -- a class records attendance until it is `completed` or `cancelled` -- in a single `app_private.class_accepts_checkins` they all call, strictly more permissive than any of them was, with each body otherwise the deployed source unchanged; the page now separates `isDraft` ("not yet announced": open for enrolment, delete) from `isOpen` ("still being run"), and `isOpen` is the database's own boundary -- `lock_completed_training_attendee` refuses attendance writes at exactly `completed`. Pinned by `class_checkin_lifecycle.test.sql` (12 assertions, 5 fail unpatched). **Second, an exhausted certificate PDF answered "Retry" with a 409 that described the opposite of what happened.** `claim_certificate_pdf_jobs` never claims a job with `attempt_count >= max_attempts`, so once the five attempts are spent nothing will ever render it -- and the edge function answered "already being prepared. Please try again shortly." `20260905050000` adds `public.requeue_certificate_pdf` (platform admin, org_admin in the certificate's organization, or the holder; refuses any job that is not actually exhausted, naming the state it found), the function now distinguishes exhausted (422) from busy (409), and `usePrepareCertificatePdf` -- one hook, because there are two download surfaces and a recovery path on only one of them is the same defect again -- requeues and retries once. supabase-js discards an edge function's body on a non-2xx, so the wording never reached the screen either; `CertificatePdfError` parses it. Pinned by `certificate_pdf_requeue.test.sql` (11 assertions). **Third, "Assign Training" duplicated.** Five bulk surfaces insert per employee with no open-assignment check, so re-assigning the annual course to everyone -- what an administrator does each year, and again after adding one late hire -- gave every learner who already had it a second identical row: twice in My Training, two obligations in the rollup where the regulation has one, and completing either left the other to go overdue. `20260905060000` adds a partial unique index (one OPEN assignment per employee per course; `completed`/`canceled` are outside it so annual retraining still works) after cancelling any existing duplicates, keeping the row with the most progress. In the table rather than in each caller because the caller added next would not know to check. `useCreateCourseAssignment` reads the existing row back on 23505 and reports `alreadyAssigned`, so the annual re-assignment says "N already had it open" instead of erroring, and incident retraining links its corrective action to the assignment the employee will actually take. Pinned by `course_assignment_no_duplicates.test.sql` (8 assertions). **Residual, and why this row stays open:** `approve_training_session_completion` still credits only signed registrations, so a class run entirely on QR/kiosk seat time is completed through Complete Class and not through session approval -- both now reachable, but they remain two tracks with different evidence, and choosing one is a WP5 decision with the trainer in the room. Closes with WP5. |
| I13 | Notifications: a provider 5xx is a permanent failure, and `unknown` is a dead end | S | in_progress | **P1, and B3's first real delivery will hit it.** `isRetryableProviderStatus` retries only 429; a minute of SendGrid 5xx permanently fails every claimed delivery in the batch, the fallback trigger pushes them to the other channel, and three such runs open the dispatch circuit. Timeouts become `final_outcome = 'unknown'`, which `retry_notification_delivery` refuses and `run_phase1_synthetic_checks` counts with no time bound -- one 15-second timeout reddens the critical health check on every run until someone edits the row by SQL. An unconfigured provider is recorded as `failed`, not `skipped` as `DEPLOYMENT.md` says; with SMS enabled for every organization by the H4 backfill and Twilio unset, one opted-in user makes every dispatch `partial`. PLAUSIBLE: `twilio-notification-webhook` validates the signature against raw `req.url`, so if the runtime URL differs from the public one every status callback and every STOP is rejected -- confirm with one signed test callback before the first SMS. Fix: retry on `429 || >= 500`; allow admin retry of `unknown` after N hours without a provider event and bound the health counter to 24 hours; map `provider_not_configured` to `skipped`; build the signed URL from the forwarded host or the configured callback URL. **The 5xx half is closed (2026-09-05).** `isRetryableProviderStatus` now retries `429 || >= 500`: an explicit HTTP response is an unambiguous answer from the provider and is safe to replay, while transport errors stay quarantined as `unknown` and 4xx other than 429 stays permanent (a malformed payload does not become valid by repeating it). The existing test pinned `503 -> false` and had to change with the fix, which is the honest signal that it was pinning the defect. **The `provider_not_configured` half closed in block 2 (2026-09-05), where it belonged.** It was a non-retryable PROVIDER FAILURE, so an unconfigured deployment finalized every delivery `failed`/`failed` -- the exact pair `enqueue_notification_fallback` fires on. That opened a second delivery on the alternate channel stamped `alternate_channel_after_permanent_failure` (nothing failed; and with neither provider set the alternate was equally unconfigured, so it failed too), produced rows indistinguishable from real provider rejections in `/admin/notifications`, and counted toward the tally that opens the dispatch circuit -- so a deployment with no providers could open its own circuit before the secrets were ever set, and the first genuinely sendable message would arrive to find it open. `begin_notification_delivery_attempt` already skips a delivery BEFORE any attempt exists, with a `skip_reason`, for five reasons (inactive recipient, SMS consent, email preference, no live push subscription, spend cap); "the provider is not configured" is the sixth and belongs beside them rather than in the vocabulary of provider outcomes. It cannot live in that function -- whether SendGrid has a key is a fact about the Edge runtime, which the database cannot see -- so `20260905100000` adds `public.skip_notification_delivery` and the worker asks `channelProviderConfigured` before beginning an attempt. `final_outcome` stays null, which is what keeps the fallback quiet **with no change to that trigger at all**. Pinned by `notification_skip_unconfigured_channel.test.sql` (9 assertions, the first three reproducing the fabricated escalation against the real trigger) and 3 Deno tests on the credential predicate. **Copilot's review of the PR found the skip's own failure path mute, fixed 2026-09-05:** when that write fails the delivery stays `processing`, and the log line named only the delivery id -- so the one state that needs a human said nothing about why. It carries the SQLSTATE and the message now, through the `sanitizeProviderDetail` the handler already uses, which keeps what diagnoses it (`permission denied for function skip_notification_delivery`, the schema-cache miss a deploy skew produces, the function's own `A skip reason is required`) and redacts an address if one ever reaches a DETAIL line. The five neighbouring `console.error` calls in that handler have the same shape and are older than this branch; recorded here rather than swept, because widening a review fix into a refactor of code the review did not raise is how a pull request stops being reviewable. **Still open:** confirming a signed Twilio callback and an `unknown` retry against a live provider (B3). The code halves closed in this pass: J119 signs the public callback URL (and imports `readTextBody`); J132 applies that override only to status callbacks so inbound STOP/START still verify against the Messaging webhook URL; `20260906250000` lets an admin retry `unknown` after six hours; J129 bounds `get_notification_delivery_health` to 24 hours to match the synthetic check. |

The discriminator is `current_user`: inside a SECURITY DEFINER RPC it is the owner, a direct PostgREST write is `authenticated` -- NOT `current_setting('role')`, which stays 'authenticated' inside a definer (which is why `protect_incident_creation_state` also fires inside the RPCs). Quiz elimination closed in RLS -- `quiz_attempt_answers` is unreadable to the owner of a submitted, failed, non-exhausted examination attempt, so a 30-question exam can no longer be passed by learning which ones were wrong; the answer KEY was already correctly gated. 18 pgTAP assertions in `ui_only_gates.test.sql`, 12 of which fail against a clean replay without the migration. The guard is on the TABLES (`clinical_observations`, `resident_unscheduled_services`, `resident_service_task_instances`), not in the callers: three RPCs and four offline appliers write this documentation and the eighth would not know to check -- the same reasoning as the one-open-assignment index. Refused for `discharged` and `deceased` only; deliberately ALLOWED for `hospital_leave` and `temporarily_out`, because a draft written at 10:00 and synced at 16:00 after a 14:00 transfer is real care that really happened, and refusing it would lose the record to enforce a rule about the resident's state now. The sync RPCs all carry exception handlers, so such a draft comes back needing a human rather than retrying forever. `get_resident_service_task_queue` now filters to `active`/`temporarily_out` -- stricter than the write guard, because it lists work still to do. And `start_hospital_transfer`/`complete_hospital_return` DID NOT TOUCH `public.residents` at all (0 occurrences in either body), so a resident in hospital read `active` everywhere; they now set and restore `hospital_leave`, without which the queue predicate would have been useless for the commonest case. 9 pgTAP assertions in `discharge_stops_care.test.sql`, 6 of which fail against a clean replay. The invariant is also now a gate: `check:clinical-access-log` derives the clinical tables from the migrations (a table whose RLS names `clinical_record_visible`; 13 today) and fails any client read of one -- it reports exactly the 10 reads this change removed when run against the previous commit. REMAINING: the tables are still directly readable by `authenticated`, so the logged RPCs are the product's only path rather than the database's -- revoking would break `get_resident_timeline`'s invoker rights and the seven pgTAP files that assert tenant isolation by reading these tables as `authenticated`, and is a security-model change of its own.
