| I16 | Boundaries: the export ships confidential intake tables; facility scope stops at the bucket | S–M | done | Mostly fixed 2026-09-05 by `20260905130000`. The export catalogue now excludes `confidential\_%` by pattern, so `confidential_reporter_identities` and `confidential_incident_details` cannot leave through a bulk archive requested by the administrator a report may be about -- and a future confidential_* table is out by construction. `verify_certificate` no longer discloses the learner's final exam score to any slug holder (signature change; the page and types moved with it). `resolve_safety_report_facility`'s legacy UUID branch resolves the facility but returns no token, so rotation is meaningful again. `auditor` dropped from `request_binder_export` and `request_confidential_intake_escalation` -- both start work, and three documents call the role read-only. TWO RECORDED CLAIMS WERE WRONG and are corrected here: `binder-exports read` was ALREADY facility-scoped through `binder_export_jobs.facility_ids`; and the proposed fix for `survey-evidence-packets` (`is_assigned_to_facility(foldername(name)[2])`) would have denied every read, because the path is `{orgId}/{sessionId|binderJobId}/{sha}.zip` and segment 2 is a session id -- it is scoped through `survey_evidence_packet_exports` instead. 11 pgTAP assertions in `export_and_guest_boundaries.test.sql`, 7 of which fail against a clean replay. **Guest-surface residual closed 2026-09-05 by `20260905230000`.** The four items were: no throttle on the anonymous RPCs, failed token lookups writing no event, the move-in trio ignoring organization suspension, and move-in guest grants having no expiry cap. One gate does the first three. `public.assert_guest_request_allowed(surface, token)` is the first statement of every one of the **17** token-bearing anon RPCs -- a mechanical one-line insertion, spliced from each deployed body -- because seventeen copies of a policy is seventeen chances for them to disagree, and the eighteenth endpoint would be written without any of them. **Keyed on the caller, not the token**: a per-token limit throttles nobody, since an enumerating client presents a different token every time, which is the point of enumerating. The key is the first hop of `x-forwarded-for`, falling back to the token hash for a direct session. Unknown tokens get 10 a minute against 60 for known ones, because a real guest always presents a token that exists. **It adds no oracle**: a single unknown token is recorded and allowed through, and the calling function then gives its usual refusal; only the eleventh in a minute is answered differently, and by then the caller has said what they are doing. Failures land in `app_private.guest_token_failures` (the SHA-256 of what was presented, never the token, and unreadable from the surface being watched) and surface on Security &amp; Governance through `get_guest_access_health`, which is where a scan becomes visible **before** it succeeds -- previously the first evidence of an attack would have been its success. Suspension is checked in the same place, so it covers all five grant families rather than the move-in trio the row named. `issue_move_in_guest_grant` now caps at 30 days, matching its resident-agreement sibling (the evidence and survey-packet issuers cap at 90; this one had no ceiling at all). 14 pgTAP assertions in `guest_surface_throttle.test.sql`, two of them invariants over the whole surface so an eighteenth endpoint without the gate fails a test. The anon-reachable SECURITY DEFINER ratchet in `tenant_isolation_invariants.test.sql` moves 20 -> 21 deliberately, with the reason recorded: the addition returns void and hands the caller nothing. |
| I17 | Jobs control plane: a decorative kill switch and a partial "Run now" map | S–M | done | The dangerous half fixed 2026-09-05 by `20260905150000`. `kill_switch_enabled` is read only in `claim_system_job_execution`, reachable from SQL only through `execute_registered_sql_job` -- 10 of 46 cron entries; 20 call their function directly and 16 post to an Edge Function. And `run_system_job_watchdog` excluded every killed definition from its freshness pass, so flipping the switch on one of the 36 left the job running and stopped the alerting: the operator turned off the alarm, not the job. The watchdog now suppresses monitoring only where `app_private.kill_switch_can_stop_job` says the switch actually routes through the wrapper; elsewhere a killed job stays monitored, so flipping a dead switch changes nothing rather than changing only the alerting. Also fixed: five service-only guards (`record_user_invitation_sent`/`_resent`, `reconcile_user_invitation_lifecycle`, `queue_manager_weekly_digests`, `restore_all_demo_baselines`) tested `current_user not in (...)`, which inside a postgres-owned SECURITY DEFINER is always the owner and so could never fire; they read `auth.role()` now. 8 pgTAP assertions in `kill_switch_is_honest.test.sql`, all 8 failing unpatched. **Residual closed 2026-09-05 by `20260905240000`.** Nineteen of the twenty direct cron entries now call `execute_registered_sql_job`, which is what puts them inside the run ledger, the replay guard and the kill switch at once -- none of the three could see them before, so `system_job_runs` had no row for any of them and "Run now" had nothing to claim. Done per entry, not as a sweep, because three needed reading: `compliance-requirement-maintenance-daily` runs **three** statements and all three are the job; `generate-resident-service-tasks-daily` and `drain-integration-command-inbox` pass arguments, which move into the wrapper branch so every cron command is identical -- and the service-task window now reads `pa_today()` rather than `current_date`, because after 19:00 Eastern the server has already rolled over and the floor queue was being generated from the wrong first day. **The twentieth is left direct on purpose**: routing `system-job-last-success-watchdog` through the wrapper would make ITS switch real, and a control plane where one switch silences every other job's monitoring is a worse object than one where the watchdog cannot be turned off from the same screen. `kill_switch_can_stop_job` reports it dead, which is now the true and intended answer. Live definitions with a working switch: **29 of 46**, up from 10. **Five missing EDGE_JOBS entries**, not the four recorded: `binder-export-generation`, `document-analyzer-extraction`, `process-credential-renewals`, `regulatory-update-polling` and `sam-sweep-continuation`. Without them "Run now" fell through to the SQL wrapper, whose case list has no arm for any of them, so it raised 22023 and recorded a durable FAILED run against a job nobody had asked to do anything -- and for the two critical ones, three clicks open the circuit and stop the real schedule. **And the operator is told before they flip it**: `get_system_job_control_plane` returns `kill_switch_enabled` and `kill_switch_can_stop`, and the System Jobs card says "Disable will not stop this job" on a switch that cannot act. 11 pgTAP assertions in `cron_routes_through_the_wrapper.test.sql`, one of which cross-checks the cron entries against the wrapper's own case list so a future entry pointing at a key with no branch fails a test instead of opening a circuit. **And the last two, closed 2026-09-05 by `20260905250000` as a separate change in the order `20260904090000` established -- instrument, deploy, then relabel.** `process-credential-renewals` and `regulatory-update-polling` were labelled `sql_cron` while their cron commands post to an Edge Function, so both were judged by pg_cron's exit status -- which for a `net.http_post` succeeds the moment the request is ENQUEUED. `process-credential-renewals` answered 401 three times on 2026-09-04 (I4) and neither the watchdog nor the console said a word. Both functions now claim a run before doing any work and finish on every exit path, and an empty queue or source list is a SUCCESSFUL run with nothing attempted -- staying silent when idle would make a quiet interval and a dead worker look identical, which is the whole class of bug. Relabelling first would have been worse than leaving it: a null last-success on a non-`sql_cron` kind is stale immediately rather than after the SLA, so both would have gone stale on the first tick and stayed there. Expect one deploy window of known-cause warnings, as `20260904090000` describes; neither is critical, so neither pages. |
| I18 | Compliance calendars: fire drills, POC versions, a nullable due date | S | done | Fixed 2026-09-05 by `20260905160000`, `20260905170000` and `20260905180000`. **(1) A monthly drill that was never monthly.** 55 Pa. Code 2600.132/2800.132 require a drill in every calendar month; the product modelled that as `inspection_interval_days = 30`. A drill on January 31 set the next deadline to March 2, so February could be skipped with every screen reading "compliant", and twelve drills at 30-day spacing span 360 days rather than a year. `public.inspection_item_next_due_date` now holds the rule in one place -- the last day of the month AFTER the month of the last drill, and for a program with no drill yet the end of its own month, because this month's is already owed. **(2) A six-month cadence nobody computed.** `inspection_events.is_sleeping_hours_drill` has existed since `20260705054756`, the detail page asks for it by name and the tracker PDF prints it, and no due date was ever derived from it: a facility could go three years without one and look current. Each `fire_drill_program` now carries a derived `sleeping_hours_fire_drill` item (new `derived_from_inspection_item_id`, a trigger that opens and retires it with its parent, and a re-derive on the nightly fleet pass so a deleted one comes back). It reads the program's own events filtered to the sleeping-hours ones, so an aide logs one drill and both schedules move; an event written against the derived item is refused with a message that says where to log it. Every existing consumer -- the status ladder, the `inspection_due` alert, the Survey Day row, the paged compliance report -- picks it up unchanged. **(3) Survey Day read "ready" with nothing on file.** `entrance_conference_items.item_types` was added by `20260706181330` so each `inspections` prompt could name the schedules it asks about, and the frontend half never landed -- all four rows showed the same whole-table verdict, and a facility with no fire drill program at all had zero outstanding items and read Ready. `InspectionReadiness.tsx` now scopes by `item_types` and treats an empty scoped set as attention. The fire-drill row covers both cadences and the 3-day supply row is scoped at last (`20260706181330` left it unscoped because no `emergency_supply_check` item type existed, which stopped being true one day earlier). **(4) An "immutable version" with nothing frozen in it.** `plan_of_correction_versions` has carried `pdf_storage_bucket`/`pdf_storage_path`/`pdf_sha256` since `20260801021000` and nothing ever wrote one, while `generate-poc-document` upserts a single live PDF per violation and overwrites it on every regeneration -- so "show me what you filed before the amendment" had no answer, and the rows could be rewritten by anything holding the service role. `submit_plan_of_correction` now stamps `snapshot_sha256` in the transaction that freezes the snapshot, so the integrity claim never depends on a worker running; a BEFORE UPDATE/DELETE trigger makes the content columns actually immutable (the cascade from a deleted violation is let through deliberately, so a violation stays deletable); and the Edge Function renders each frozen snapshot to `<org>/<facility>/<violation>/v<N>.pdf` with `upsert:false`, hashing the stored bytes if the object already exists so a re-render can never leave a digest that disagrees with the file. `record_plan_of_correction_version_pdf` refuses a second stamp and returns the existing row, making the render idempotent. Deliberately NOT a new cron entry, job definition and watchdog signal: the client calls the function after every submit and it back-fills any unrendered version on each later invocation, and the page shows each version's digest, document state and last render error. **(5) A nullable DHS deadline.** Every consumer of `poc_due_date` is written `is not null` -- the C4 escalation, the weekly digest tally, the work queue -- so a violation recorded without one was a deadline nothing warned about, escalated or counted, and indistinguishable from one with time left. The column is NOT NULL now (zero rows in production; the migration backfills `inspection_date + 30` and says so in a notice), and both forms require it. **(6) An empty cycle is not a cycle, part two.** `20260805120000` fixed the manual case by carrying the roster forward and then discarded the count it asked for, so a declarative campaign whose predicates no longer match anyone still opened an empty cycle, advanced its recurrence and reported success. `spawn_due_policy_campaign_cycles` now reads the count for both targeting modes: an empty cycle is rolled back, `next_occurrence_on` is left where it is so the next run retries, and the reason lands on the campaign where its owner sees it. 34 pgTAP assertions across `fire_drill_calendar.test.sql` (18) and `poc_version_is_frozen.test.sql` (16). Six existing fixtures were corrected in the same change set: four supplied no `poc_due_date`, `policy_campaign_recurrence`'s far-off series had no roster to carry forward, and `inspection_item_schedule_recalc`'s interval-ladder fixture was a `fire_drill_program` on a 90-day cadence that the calendar rule makes meaningless. `20260905180000` is a separate finding the invariants caught while this ran: `incident_notification_rules` (`20260905080000`, I10) was created after `20260904100000`'s grant sweep, so the hosted image's default privileges re-granted INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE on it to `anon` and `authenticated`. RLS denied every one of those writes -- a lock left open, not a door -- but two tests say so out loud, including I10's own "nobody can change a reporting deadline through the API". Two new trigger functions in this change set had the same shape and are revoked at the point they are defined. |
| I19 | Build and shell: demo credentials in the customer bundle, a silent rollback | S | done | Fixed 2026-09-05 by `20260905190000` and the build guard in `vite.config.ts`. **(1) Demo passwords in the customer bundle.** `Demo.tsx` reads `import.meta.env.VITE_DEMO_ACCOUNTS_JSON`, and Vite replaces that expression with the literal at build time -- so the value ships in a public chunk of every production bundle built with the variable set, regardless of what the code does with it. `parseDemoAccounts` returns `[]` when `VITE_ENABLE_PUBLIC_DEMO` is absent, which hides the login picker and cannot unship the string; `deploymentReadinessChecks` reported it only on a post-deploy admin page nobody has to open; and the build guard warned on blank values only. Railway variables can be set at PROJECT scope, where they reach every service, so this was one checkbox from a customer bundle. The `build` guard now throws when the JSON is non-blank without the flag, naming the project-scope case. Verified both ways: the build fails with the variable set alone, and succeeds with `VITE_ENABLE_PUBLIC_DEMO=true` (the dedicated demo host). **(2) A rollback that could not delete anything.** Creating a ticket was two inserts with a client-side compensating `delete()` between them -- against a table with no DELETE policy and no DELETE grant to `authenticated`. It had never removed a row. A failed first message left a subject-only ticket in the platform support queue, the UI reported a failure, and the retry made a second one: support sees two empty tickets and a customer who says they could not file anything. `public.create_support_ticket_with_message` now writes both rows in one transaction and restates the two RLS policies it stands in for rather than assuming them -- `created_by` and `organization_id` come from the caller's profile, not from arguments. The attachment stays a second step because it has to (the bucket's write policy reverse-joins to `support_tickets`, so the ticket must exist first), through `attach_file_to_support_ticket_message`, which accepts one file on the caller's own first message of their own open ticket; a failure there now costs the file and nothing else, and the client removes the orphaned object. **(3) A redirect loop for a Compliance- or Billing-only tenant.** Bare `/app` belongs to the CareBase module, so an organization licensed for Compliance or Billing alone cannot reach it. The facility-type gate redirected there on a non-match, the module gate bounced it to `moduleAccess.homePath` (`/app/inspection-readiness` or `/app/resident-finance`), and both of those are PCH/ALF-only -- so a tenant with no Personal Care Home or Assisted Living Facility site got an infinite redirect instead of a page. The gate now goes to the module home, which terminates after at most one hop because the second arrival has `currentPath === homePath`, and renders an explanation inside the normal chrome: which facility types the page covers, and links to Facilities and Help. Small but worth having even though a Compliance-only tenant is not a pilot shape -- it is the difference between an unusable browser tab and a sentence. 14 pgTAP assertions in `support_ticket_is_atomic.test.sql`, two of which pin the absent DELETE policy and grant so the old rollback cannot be reintroduced as though it worked. |
| I20 | Learning runtime: SCORM completion without a certificate, a dead offline video link | S–M | done | Fixed 2026-09-05 by `20260905200000` and four client changes. **(1) A package finished the course and issued nothing.** `bridge_learning_runtime_completion` ran from an AFTER INSERT trigger on `learning_runtime_commits` and set `course_assignments.status = 'completed'` itself. Issuance lives in `complete_course_assignment` -- the certificate row, credential number, examination score, provider snapshot, expiry -- so a learner who finished a packaged course was marked complete with nothing to show a surveyor, and the completed assignment is exactly what stops them completing it properly afterwards. On a comprehensive version it was worse: the bridge's UPDATE tripped `require_comprehensive_self_completion`, which since `20260810161000` aborts the transaction, so the learner's commit failed with "Could not save learning progress" and the package result was lost, every time. And a package is one block: reporting it done ended the course from the middle, skipping every quiz, attestation and applied response after it. The bridge and its trigger are **removed** rather than reduced -- `commit_learning_runtime_state` already records the result on the commit ledger, and a second copy on `course_progress.learning_tools` would be worse than none because the course player rewrites that column wholesale from its own notes map on every save. **(2) The requirement that makes deferring safe.** `complete_course_assignment` now refuses a course whose version carries a `scorm` block until a runtime session for that assignment has reported completion. It reads `learning_runtime_commits`, not `learning_runtime_sessions.state`: a session is one row per package+assignment and `start_learning_runtime_session` reactivates it on every launch, so a state-based gate would forget a finished package the moment the learner reopened it. `canAdvanceCourseStep` gains the same gate (a `scorm` step had no gate at all, because the package's own completion used to end the course), the player says which completion it is recording, and it now reflects a package finished on an earlier visit instead of asking for it again. **(3) A dead offline video link.** `prepare_offline_course_bundle` returns the block's stored locator, which for an org-authored video is `storage://course-videos/<path>` -- not a URL -- and `OfflineCourse` rendered it as an href. Dead even online, and doubly so since the bucket went private in `20260714233041`. It resolves through `useCourseVideoUrl` now, the same as the live player. **(4) A 15-minute URL with no way back.** The signed course-video URL lasts 15 minutes and plenty of lessons are longer, so a learner watching straight through hit an expired signature mid-playback: the browser reported a media error nothing was listening for, the video stopped with no message, and a reload lost the place. `useCourseVideoUrl` exposes a `refresh()`; the player re-signs on the media error (capped at two attempts, so a deleted object says so instead of spinning), saves `currentTime` first and restores it on the new URL. Deliberately NOT refetch-on-focus: that would swap the `<video>` src every time a learner tabs away and back. 9 pgTAP assertions in `package_step_defers_completion.test.sql` and 3 unit assertions on the step gate. `phase4_governed_learning.test.sql`'s two bridge assertions were inverted in the same change set -- they asserted the defect ("completion bridge flips the assignment to completed") and now assert that a package result completes nothing. |
| I21 | Notifications and billing: recipients, consent, event ordering | S | done | Fixed 2026-09-05 by `20260905210000`. **(1) Consent somebody else gave for you.** `update_profile_contact_preferences` let an org admin -- or a facility manager scoped to the employee's facility -- pass `p_sms_opt_in = true` for another person's profile, and stamped `sms_consent_at = now()` as though that person had given it. That column is the record a TCPA complaint asks about, and it could be written by an ordinary "tidy up the roster" edit on a screen that never mentions consent. Opting IN is now the recipient's own act; the refusal is scoped to the transition rather than the caller, so an administrator can still fix a name, correct a phone number and turn texting OFF (an opt-out needs nobody's permission). The condition refused is exactly the one that would stamp a new consent, including moving an opted-in profile to a phone number the administrator typed in. **(2) A placeholder that outranked the real subscription.** `checkout.session.completed` inserts a stub `billing_subscriptions` row so trial expiry cannot lock out a customer who has just paid, stamped with the CHECKOUT event's timestamp -- and the `customer.subscription.created` upsert only applies when its `(provider_event_created_at, provider_event_id)` sorts strictly higher. Stripe emits both within the same second and their `created` values do not order reliably, so the real event could lose: seat quantity stuck at 1, no billing period, and `billing_subscription_items` never populated, which is precisely what the hourly billing-quantity-sync reports as `partial`, indefinitely, on a paying customer. The stub now carries `is_provider_placeholder` and any real provider event supersedes it whatever the clocks say; between two real events the existing ordering is untouched. **Verified by replaying the two events in the failing order** -- checkout at 12:00:05, subscription.created at 12:00:00 -- against the deployed function body: 3 of the 14 assertions fail unpatched and pass after. **(3) Forty aides nobody could reach.** Every `enqueue_*` resolves its recipient through `profiles`, and the reminder jobs join `employees.profile_id is not null`; the import worker writes `employees.phone`, which no delivery path reads. A facility that imports its roster and invites nobody gets zero reminders, zero deliveries and zero failures -- the system does exactly what it says and nothing on the deliveries dashboard shows it. Deliberately NOT fixed with a `skipped` delivery row per employee per notification: that is one row per aide per reminder per day describing a condition that does not change between them, spliced into eight separate queue jobs. What was missing is a number somebody sees. `get_notification_reach()` gives it continuously, surfaced on `/admin/notifications` (every tenant) and on an organization's own Settings page beside the notification switches that do not reach those people -- and `PHASE1_OPERATIONS.md` now says onboarding is not complete while it is above zero. 14 pgTAP assertions in `consent_reach_and_event_order.test.sql` (a dedicated file rather than an addition to `phase2_billing_integration.test.sql`, which is 58 assertions of unrelated ground). One existing assertion in `notification_operations_completion.test.sql` was retargeted in the same change set: it drove the phone-change consent case AS THE ORG ADMIN, which is what made the hole visible; the rule it is really about -- a changed number needs fresh consent rather than inheriting the old one -- is unchanged and now acted by the recipient. |
| I22 | Signup cap counts failures; break-glass confers nothing | S | done | Fixed 2026-09-05 by `20260905220000`. **(1) A limiter that charged the customer for our outage.** `reserve_signup_attempt` counted every `signup_attempts` row for an email address in the last day whatever became of it, against a cap of three -- and the failures that land there include `invite_failed`, which is exactly what a misconfigured SMTP produces (B3/H11), plus `settings_unavailable`, `turnstile_not_configured` and every unexpected error. A first customer who tried three times during an outage of ours was then refused for the rest of the day, by a limiter defending us against them for a fault that was never theirs. On a pilot, that customer is the pilot. `signup_attempts.counts_toward_rate_limit` now carries the attribution, stamped by `finalize_signup_attempt` from a named list of platform faults, and both the per-email and per-IP caps read it. A success still counts (a second organization for one email address is what the cap is for), an in-flight reservation still counts, and a caller-side failure -- a failed Turnstile, an invalid redirect -- still counts. Historical rows are backfilled by the same rule. **(2) Break-glass granted nothing.** `grant_identity_break_glass` requires a platform admin, a fresh AAL2 session, a separate requester, a written reason, a ticket reference and an expiry -- and then writes one row and stops. Nothing reads `identity_break_glass_events` for authorization: not `has_effective_permission`, not the role helpers, not one RLS policy; only `get_identity_control_plane`, which lists it. The console called it "time-boxed elevated access" and said a profile was "receiving access", so an operator at 3am could grant it, watch it succeed, and believe somebody could now do something they still could not. **Relabelled, not wired**: making this table confer permissions would add a row that silently widens a session, which is a deliberate security decision and not one to take quietly in a P2 sweep on the way to a pilot. What the two-person rule, the reason, the ticket and the expiry actually produce is an authorization RECORD made at the moment of the decision, which is worth having on its own; the access is granted the way it always was, by a role change or support impersonation, each separately audited. The table comment, the function comment, the card, the hook and every toast now say so. 12 pgTAP assertions in `signup_limiter_and_break_glass.test.sql`. Three of them are invariants rather than behaviour: that no function outside the two writers and the read-only control plane reads the break-glass table, that no RLS policy consults it, and that `has_effective_permission` has never heard of it -- so if a later change wires it in, that privilege-escalation path fails a test instead of arriving silently. |
| I23 | The P3 long tail from the area reviews | S | open | Recorded so nobody rediscovers them: impersonation banner rendered inside the MFA gate; `scim-provision` not SCIM 2.0-interoperable (custom header and body); **`admin-update-user` returning raw Postgres text and both emails -- closed 2026-09-05.** Eight failure paths returned the raw error verbatim, and one built a string carrying the OLD and NEW login email in a message a toast displayed and client error reporting captured. Blanket-replacing all eight would have been worse than the leak, because the useful ones are our own raises ("org_admin cannot grant platform_admin") and losing those leaves an administrator with "something went wrong". So the line is drawn where the codebase already draws it: a SQLSTATE we chose carries a message we wrote and passes through (P0001/42501/22023/02000/23505); anything else is logged with a correlation id the response names. The split-identity case now says the two are out of step, without either address. **`signup-organization` blaming the address for any mail-hook failure -- closed 2026-09-05.** One sentence answered every cause: "We could not send an invitation to THAT EMAIL ADDRESS. If you already have an account, sign in or reset your password instead." A mail hook down, SendGrid unreachable, our own send ceiling -- all of them told a prospective customer their address was wrong and sent them to reset a password they had never set. Three causes now, three answers, and `20260905330000` classifies the new `invite_rate_limited` as a platform fault that does not count against the caller's cap; `invite_email_in_use` deliberately does count, or the signup form becomes an account-enumeration oracle with no budget. **the SMS gate copy vs `VITE_MFA_SMS_ENABLED` -- closed 2026-09-05,** and the row was half wrong: the ROOT `.env.example` documents it at length. It was missing from `artifacts/caremetric-carebase/.env.example`, which is the file a developer building that workspace opens. **impersonating a never-accepted invitee confirms them -- CONFIRMED and closed 2026-09-05.** The session is minted with `generateLink({ type: "magiclink" })`, and verifying that token confirms the address -- so a support action marked an invitation as accepted, on the invitee's behalf, without telling them. Refused rather than compensated: restoring the flag after Auth has cleared it is a race against every other writer, and the refusal is the truer statement -- nobody has ever signed in to that account, so there is no session to step into. incident alert text in UTC and an overdue badge that flips hourly beside a live stage; **kiosk exit needs no credential -- closed 2026-09-05.** The page runs on a device left on a table for a queue of aides to type PINs into, in the TRAINER's signed-in session, and "Exit Kiosk Mode" was a plain `Link`: one tap from that queue into the trainer's whole account. `SessionSecurityGates` already demands a password when the kiosk idles out (`lock_reason = 'kiosk_timeout'`); leaving deliberately now asks for the same thing, through the same `markExplicitPasswordSignIn` idiom. **seat-time edge cases -- closed 2026-09-05 by `20260905300000`.** Both real: `complete_training_class` credited `v_class.duration_hours` -- the SCHEDULED length -- whenever a check-out was missing, so signing in at the door and leaving after five minutes produced a completed compliant record for the full four hours, a claim nobody made in the file a surveyor reads; and the status was the literal `'compliant'` regardless of hours, so 09:00 to 09:01 passed too. `correct_completed_class_attendee` held the identical two lines in a second copy. Both now call `app_private.class_attendance_credit`, which records the hours evidenced (null when unknown) and sends anything under 90% of the scheduled duration to the Pending Review queue `PendingApprovals.tsx` already works, with both numbers in `review_comments`. Neither case is refused, `recalculate_compliance_core` already preserves `pending_review`, and `coalesce(hours, 0)` means an unknown-hours record credits nothing until a human says what it was. The 90% is a product default, not a citation, and is stated as one in the function. **`recalculate_course_assignment_statuses` and the due-reminder job on UTC `current_date` -- does NOT reproduce:** both already read `public.pa_today()`, and no function in either schema uses `current_date` at all. **What WAS there, found by sweeping for it (`20260905310000`):** nine `to_char()` calls across eight functions rendering a `timestamptz` in the SESSION time zone, which is UTC for every cron job and service-role call -- including `recalculate_incident_notifications`, whose alert names the time an incident was reported and whose deadline runs from it. An 8:30 PM report read as "Sep 06, 12:30 AM". `public.pa_local` is the counterpart to the existing `pa_day`, and all nine go through it; `compute_audit_event_hash` keeps its explicit UTC, because a hash input must be canonical. **The incident overdue badge** -- real, and worse than hourly: `buildIncidentFollowThrough` read `new Date()` at render time and nothing re-rendered, so a two-hour reportable-notification deadline passing with the page open kept reading as in time until a refetch. `useMinuteClock` ticks it, aligned to the whole minute. watch gate and seat time client-only for legacy courses; **`window.open` after `await` on iOS Safari -- CONFIRMED and closed 2026-09-05, and it was not two call sites but 34.** Safari on iOS, and popup blockers generally, only honour `window.open` while the browser still considers itself inside the user gesture, and an `await` ends that -- so on the device an aide is most likely holding, the certificate, the class notice, the POC document, the binder export and thirty more did nothing at all: no tab, no error, no download. `openDocumentUrl` navigates the current tab when the open is refused, which is one line at each site and changes nothing where popups work. Deliberately not the two-phase alternative (open `about:blank` on click, set `.location` later): it is what a from-scratch design would do, and it means a forgotten `cancel()` leaves a blank tab on every error path across 34 sites. `check:source-integrity` now refuses a raw `window.open` in production source. It skips comment lines so the file that EXPLAINS this bug in prose does not trip it -- and **that skip was itself too broad, found by Copilot reviewing the PR and corrected 2026-09-05.** It tested only how a line BEGINS, so `/* why */ window.open(url)` walked straight past the gate; the same hole sat in the continuation branch Copilot did not name, where ` */ window.open(url)` did too. A block-comment line counts as prose now only when nothing but whitespace follows its last `*/`, and the gate carries a `--self-test` wired into `check:all` beside its eleven siblings -- run against the old predicate it fails on exactly those two plus `/* a */ /* b */ window.open(url)`. A gate with a hole in it is worse than no gate, because it is believed. **quiet hours deferring critical incident SMS -- closed 2026-09-05 by `20260905320000`.** `prepare_notification_delivery` carried the comment "These are non-emergency compliance/training messages" and then deferred EVERY SMS with no test of what the message was: a fall at 2 AM waited until 08:00 while the 55 Pa. Code notification clock ran. A narrow exemption rather than a weakening -- `incident_reported`, `shift_handoff_escalated`, and any `delivery_type = 'escalation'` -- through one predicate all THREE places quiet hours is applied now ask (the trigger, the retry and the attempt claim, which would each have re-deferred what the others let through). Overdue types stay deferred: a day does not turn at 3 AM. An unidentifiable message defers, because an unknown 3 AM SMS is how a facility switches the channel off entirely. 14 assertions. **`{{action_url}}` = `/` -- closed 2026-09-05.** Every templated email and SMS said "review it" and linked to the marketing homepage; the recipient, told it was urgent and usually on a phone, had to go and find the thing. `actionUrlFor` uses `notifications.link`, the same destination the in-app notification already uses, made absolute -- and accepts only same-origin app paths, because an action URL is the one part of a notification a recipient is invited to click and "only we write it" is how an open redirect starts. It lives in `_shared/notificationDelivery.ts` beside its two siblings rather than in the handler, so its unit test does not make `check:edge-functions` count dispatch-notifications as runtime-tested when it is not. **The "generic fallback" half is not a defect:** `renderProviderMessage` is deliberately generic for `resident_compliance_due`, `support_ticket_update` and `incident_reported` -- resident and support free text must not leave the app in an email or an SMS. module RLS covering only `product_module_resources` tables; **`request-demo` and `email-savings-model` logging raw error objects -- closed 2026-09-05.** The responses were already sanitized; the `console.error` passed the whole error object, and an unexpected throw from validation, the database or SendGrid can carry the submitted payload on it -- so a visitor's address landed in a function log with a different audience and retention from the row it belongs to. Now the shape only: name, `scrubEmails`-ed message, and the `internalDetail` the throw site chose to say. render-blocking Google Fonts; **`robots.txt` -- closed 2026-09-05,** and the sweep found two more: `/verify/` and `/passport/` carry a named person's certificate and training history and exist to be opened from a link, not found by searching that person's name. `/report-safety` is deliberately left indexable: a public reporting channel that cannot be found is not one. unlabelled remove buttons in the incident form and unbounded emergency inputs; a lazy page's first load hiding the whole shell; PLAUSIBLE stale shell after a deploy on a >4 s HTML fetch; in-memory survey packet zip built twice; the service-lane sync replay ignoring a device revocation (the observation lane fixed it); Measured while there: 8 of 110 routes grant a role their nav catalogue withholds; the other 7 (`/trainer`, `/me/schedule` and five more) each need their own judgement about whether the narrower advertisement is deliberate, and are recorded rather than swept. **`duplicate` discarding a differing offline note** -- does NOT reproduce: every save mints a fresh `draftId` AND a fresh `idempotencyKey`, there is no edit path that mutates a draft's content, and the server returns the ORIGINAL outcome rather than a blanket `duplicate`, so the discard is on a draft already recorded. **FHIR write-back duplication** -- substantially addressed already: the worker sends a conditional create (`If-None-Exist` keyed on a deterministic identifier) and retries the completion RPC three times before logging the external id at error level. What is left is a non-conforming EHR ignoring the header, which is the customer's server, not this code. `search_path = 'public'` pins that should be `''` -- recorded as five, MEASURED as 144 on 2026-09-05 (`select count(*) from pg_proc where proconfig @> array['search_path=public']`), which makes it a sweep rather than a tidy-up: each one needs every unqualified reference in its body schema-qualified before the pin can be tightened, and none of them is exploitable while the pin is set at all -- the danger `''` closes is a caller-controlled search_path, which `SET search_path` already neutralises. `match_exclusion_list_against_roster_core` was tightened in `20260905270000` because that migration was rewriting it anyway; the rest stay recorded. |

The error is deliberately asymmetric: a false positive costs a minute in a queue that a human already staffs, a false negative costs the facility its Medicare and Medicaid billing for everything that person touches.
