| I29 | The automated review of PR #484 found fourteen defects in the code that PR added | M | done | Copilot found two and Codex fourteen; **thirteen reproduced, one did not, and one of the thirteen was real by a different mechanism than reported.** The common thread is not subtlety -- it is that a clean replay of 650 migrations and 3,889 green assertions had already passed every one of them, because none of this code had ever been called with a real row. Same gap `db lint` caught on this branch with `v_credit := <table-returning function>`, same answer: assertions that exercise the path instead of inspecting it. `review_found_seven_more.test.sql` (20) and two added to `oapsa_bars_employment` (28). **`import_apply_resident_assessment` tested `domain = 'resident_assessments'`, which the `data_import_jobs` constraint has never allowed** -- the vocabulary is `assessments` -- so the predicate matched no job that could exist and every assessment row raised 22023 before it was written. In the migration whose whole subject was that imports are the first thing a facility does, and its siblings `employees` and `residents` were both correct. **`evaluate_duty_eligibility` resolved the employee with `profile_id = ... and facility_id = p_facility_id`,** and `employees.facility_id` is the PRIMARY facility -- so a float aide working their second site matched no employee row and the OAPSA check was skipped outright: a `not_suitable` determination stopped nothing there. Measured both ways -- old predicate returns `blocks: []` at the second site, new one blocks. `employees.profile_id` is UNIQUE, so the facility was never needed to identify the person, which is the right shape: the bar attaches to the person, not to where they are standing. **Reopening a closed incident left its work item closed for ever** -- `20260905120000` added the administrator reopen path, `route_incident_work` is AFTER INSERT only, and the deduplication key forbids a replacement. `app_private.reopen_work_items_for_source` is the inverse of the closer; `canceled` items deliberately stay cancelled. **And every closure history row recorded `prior_state = 'open'`** regardless of the state the work actually came from, because `UPDATE ... RETURNING` yields the NEW row -- the prior state is read in a `targets` CTE now, matching what `transition_work_item` already did. **The duplicate-assignment cleanup ranked `paused` as one flat tier,** but a leave transition moves untouched AND in-progress assignments alike to `paused` and keeps the real state in `lifecycle_previous_status` -- so the tie broke on `assigned_at` and a learner who had progressed a later duplicate had that progress permanently cancelled while an untouched earlier row survived. Ranks on the effective status now; the cleanup does not restore what it cancels, so it has to be right the first time. **The hourly LEIE continuation could never run:** `resumeOnly` returned idle whenever no SAM sweep was parked, before it ever asked about a parked LEIE load -- defeating, in the same file, the month-long stall the parking was built to avoid. **A fire-drill programme retyped to anything else kept its derived sleeping-hours child active,** still deriving deadlines from a parent that is no longer a programme. Deactivated, not deleted: it holds the history of drills that happened. **`pa_local()` output labelled `UTC`** in the one place the UTC sweep added a label at all. **The last-good module set was held in a ref keyed by nothing,** and a ref outlives what a sign-out clears -- so the second tenant to sign in on one mounted SPA inherited the first tenant's modules the moment their own entitlement call failed, and because a last-good set "existed" `entitlementFailureIsBlocking` suppressed the error screen that would have stopped them. Keyed by organization now, through a pure helper with its own tests. **The needs-review purge measured from `createdAt`,** so the two protections added in I6 combined into a loss: a draft held safely offline for eight days reached `rejected` on its fifth deterministic refusal and the next sweep deleted it -- at the exact moment it first became a human's to look at. The seven days are the REVIEWER's and run from the transition; the existing assertion that pinned the old behaviour was pinning the defect and changed with the fix, which is the honest signal. **A facility selected before the header's "Viewing as" organization changed kept precedence over the refreshed list** on both integration consoles, so the workspace and resident queries stayed on the previous tenant while everything around them moved. **Reported by a different mechanism than the real one:** Codex said a revoked invitation could never be re-sent because `inviteUserByEmail` rejects the retained address. Measured against a local GoTrue, that is false -- `POST /invite` answers 200 for an UNCONFIRMED address and reuses THE SAME user id; only a CONFIRMED one gets 422 `email_exists`. The dead end is real and one layer down: `user_invitation_lifecycle.invited_user_id` is UNIQUE and `record_user_invitation_sent` did a plain insert, so the second invitation raised 23505, `invite-user` read that as a receipt failure, deleted the identity and answered "Invite provisioning failed". It reopens a revoked or delivery-failed row now and refuses only an accepted one -- and it lives in `20260905350000` at the END of the series, because `20260905150000` redefines that same function and a fix written into the revoke migration would have been silently overwritten, which is itself how this class of defect gets in. **Does NOT reproduce:** the `is_provider_placeholder` backfill. `default false` would mislabel existing checkout stubs, but production holds **0** `billing_subscriptions` rows (measured), 0 import jobs, 0 revoked invitations, 0 closed work items and 0 paused assignments -- there is nothing to backfill, and no stub can arise before H9 maps live Stripe Prices. Recorded rather than answered with machinery for a state that does not exist. **The guest throttle did not survive its own denial -- fixed 2026-09-05 by `20260905360000`, on the owner's decision to do it in this PR rather than defer it.** Measured first: call any guest RPC with an unknown token and `guest_request_windows` and `guest_token_failures` both hold **zero** rows afterwards. PostgREST wraps the call in one transaction and the `42501` aborted it, taking the gate's counter and the failure log with it -- so the control counted only requests that SUCCEEDED, was inert against the scanner it was built to detect, and `GuestAccessHealthCard` would have read zero for ever. There is no fix inside one transaction, because Postgres cannot commit inside a function: for the write to survive, the denial must not raise. It does not raise now. A denied call sets `response.status = 403` and RETURNS a body shaped like a PostgREST error, so PostgREST answers 403, the transaction COMMITS, and supabase-js hands the caller an `error` with the same `message` and `code` it always had -- **no client hook changed for the denial path at all**. The eight boolean/uuid RPCs became jsonb because a scalar body cannot carry a denial the browser will notice: measured against postgrest-js, a 403 whose body is `false` or `null` arrives as a FALSY error with null data, which is a silent nothing and worse than the defect. `assert_guest_request_allowed` is DROPPED rather than kept as a raising wrapper -- a gate whose contract is "abort the transaction" cannot be used by anything PostgREST calls without reintroducing exactly this -- and its replacement needs no `anon` grant at all, since every caller is SECURITY DEFINER, so the anon-executable surface goes **21 -> 20**. The reproduction is now an assertion rather than a note: `guest_surface_throttle.test.sql` makes a denied call and looks for the rows it should have left (19 assertions, up from 14). Two defects were found while doing it, both by the suite: a `return false` left in a jsonb function casts through boolean's TEXT output `f`, which is not valid JSON, so the refusal path raised 22P02 instead of refusing; and a regex rewrite of the test file silently corrupted an unrelated `publish_resident_agreement_version` assertion, which is why the diff was read line by line before it was pushed rather than trusted. |
| I30 | The deploy of #484 failed twice in production on a screening join that could not use an index | M | done | **The first thing this work did to a live database was saturate it.** PR #484 merged at 21:53 UTC and `deploy-migrations` applied 29 of its 39 migrations in about thirteen seconds, then stopped on `20260905270000` and never finished; the job's 30-minute timeout cancelled it, and a re-run did the same. Production sat at **642 applied / 20260905260000** for an hour, ten migrations and the whole edge-function deploy outstanding, and for roughly twenty minutes of that it would not answer a `select count(*)` at all -- five consecutive connection timeouts while the project itself reported ACTIVE_HEALTHY. GitHub issue #486 records the failure. **Nothing was ever half-applied:** the migration runs in one transaction and rolled back cleanly both times, no locks and no open transaction, which is the only reason this is a delay rather than an incident. **The cause, measured rather than guessed:** the join in both screens applied `exclusion_name_match_score` to every (employee x entry) pair. It is opaque to the planner, so the only indexable predicates were `snapshot_id` and `source` -- and the btree the same migration adds on `exclusion_name_key(last_name)` was never used, because nothing gave the planner an equality to use it with. Production holds **157,192** exclusion entries against 19 active employees: ~3M calls at ~40us each, timed locally at 6.37s per employee. On a single-core instance that is enough to starve everything else, which is exactly what the connection timeouts were. **The fix, in place under a `migration-content-allowlist` entry** -- a new migration cannot express it, because `20260905270000` must apply before anything later can and its own body is what fails, and it had never been applied so there is no deployed text to diverge from. Two GIN trigram indexes, and a candidate prefilter ahead of the score that the planner CAN answer, with pg_trgm's thresholds pinned on both functions so a session GUC cannot narrow it from outside. **Re-screen at production scale: 79ms, from unbounded.** **The prefilter is strictly wider than the score's gate, and that is asserted rather than believed:** it mirrors the gate's four surviving routes at looser thresholds, including BOTH directions of `<%` because word_similarity is asymmetric -- `LEE` inside `ANDERSON-LEE-WASHINGTON` scores ~0.15 on similarity and 1.0 on word_similarity, and that containment case is precisely what the widened rule was for. `exclusion_name_variants.test.sql` now cross-multiplies an adversarial corpus and requires zero pairs admitted by the score to be dropped by the filter: **147,456 tested, 1,688 admitted, 0 dropped.** No screening outcome changes -- same score, same threshold, same matches, 92% fewer of them scored. **What this says about the work that preceded it:** 652 migrations replayed, 3,916 assertions and 26 gates all passed this migration, because every one of them ran against a local table with a handful of rows. Nothing in the suite knew how big the real exclusion list is. The performance shape of a query against production-sized data was not tested anywhere, and the deploy was the first thing that ever asked. |
| I32 | Exclusion screening is removed, at the owner's direction | M | done | **The whole subsystem, one day after it was deployed.** `20260906020000` drops six tables, a view, fifteen functions, two triggers, two cron entries, the `exclusion-screening` job definition, the `exclusion_match_found` alert type, the `screening.on_hire_exclusion` release flag, the `screen-exclusions` Edge Function, two routes, three sidebar entries and the console. **What it gives up, recorded here because a decision this size should not have to be reconstructed from a diff:** (1) nothing in the product will detect that an employee appears on a federal exclusion list -- a facility employing an excluded individual cannot bill Medicare or Medicaid for that person's work and penalties accrue per claim, so the detection now has to happen outside this system, by someone; (2) `evaluate_schedule_eligibility` blocked scheduling on a confirmed exclusion and that block was **not overridable** -- one of only three in the whole gate -- so an excluded person can now be put on a shift with nothing raised; (3) Survey Day's roster loses its `exclusionState` chip. **What was destroyed:** 157,192 exclusion-list entries, the snapshot and refresh history, and the one `pending_review` match with its critical alert -- raised 01:56 UTC the same morning by the widened matcher and never adjudicated. A point-in-time restore is the only way back. **The part that took the work was not the feature's own files.** A name search finds twenty; a dependency sweep of function bodies finds that **seven functions outside the feature read into it**, and plpgsql resolves at run time, so a missed one would not have failed the migration -- only the next call. `evaluate_schedule_eligibility` and `get_survey_day_staff_roster` read `exclusion_screening_matches`; `run_system_job_watchdog` called its reconciler every five minutes; `run_phase1_synthetic_checks` emitted an `exclusionSourcesWithoutActiveSnapshot` counter and `execute_registered_sql_job` **failed the job whenever that counter was non-zero** -- a counter over a dropped table does not read zero, it raises; and `create_schedule_eligibility_override` plus `evaluate_shift_assignment_eligibility` held `confirmed_exclusion` in non-overridable lists, as did a CHECK constraint on `schedule_eligibility_overrides`. All seven are patched from `pg_get_functiondef` at the exclusion references only. **Two near-misses:** `public.alert_list_rows` is `select a.*`, so it froze the dropped column into its own shape and blocked the drop -- CASCADE would have silently taken the alert queue's paged read model, so it is dropped and recreated instead; and `get_organization_export_exclusions` shares a word with the feature and nothing else, so it survives, pinned by an assertion. **Copilot's review then found the same mistake a third time.** `sam-sweep-continuation` -- the hourly resume tick for the SAM sweep, sharing the cron entry and the Edge Function -- was left as an active job definition, because its key names what it DOES rather than the feature it serves and a search for 'exclusion' or 'screen' among the job keys does not return it. Left in place it would have listed in `/admin/system-jobs` as a job whose cron entry and worker were both gone, failing on "Run now". So the ratchet is 46 -> **44**, not 45, and the test now also asserts the STRUCTURAL form of the claim -- no active definition may point at a cron entry that does not exist -- which is the check a name search cannot make. Copilot's third finding was real but predates this work: `create_schedule_eligibility_override` validated `('lifecycle_inactive', 'confirmed_exclusion')` while its CHECK forbade three codes, so an override for an OAPSA determination was refused by the constraint with a bare 23514 instead of by the function with its 22023 and message; closed here because this migration already rewrites both halves. `exclusion_screening_is_removed.test.sql` (16) asserts both directions -- that none of it came back, and that none of the things around it went with it. Five feature-only pgTAP files, two shared modules and their tests are deleted; `go_live_readiness_repairs` loses its four B5a assertions and moves its job-run fixture to a surviving `job_key`, because `system_job_runs.job_key` has an ON DELETE RESTRICT foreign key to the definitions table. 653 migrations replay from empty. **The Codex review round: four findings, all real.** The severe one was not in the migration at all but in the deploy: `supabase functions deploy` only pushes what is in the tree, so deleting `supabase/functions/screen-exclusions/` left the function ACTIVE on production, and `check-edge-function-drift.mjs` would then have failed the deploy on `ORPHAN (remote not local)` -- **after** `db push` had already applied the migrations. That is a half-applied deploy whose live function calls schema the same run had just dropped, and nothing in the workflow deleted a remote function, ever. `scripts/removed-edge-functions.json` now records the slug with the migration that removed it and `scripts/prune-removed-edge-functions.mjs` deletes exactly those slugs through the Management API immediately before the presence check, tolerating 404 so the entry stays true on every later deploy. It refuses to delete a slug that still exists under `supabase/functions`, and it **only deletes with `--apply`** -- see the incident below. The other three: the `screening.on_hire_exclusion` feature key survived in six tables (`feature_definitions`, `release_flags`, `package_entitlements`, `organization_entitlement_grants`, `organization_release_cohorts`, `feature_kill_switches`) because the migration dropped the flag's behaviour and not its rows; and the customer-facing copy still sold the feature -- the landing page, the features page, an FAQ answer, the help-center manual section and the override dialog's "confirmed exclusions", all now saying what is true instead. **An incident of my own, recorded because it is the reason the script's default is what it is.** Validating the new pruner, I ran it with no flags while a live `SUPABASE_ACCESS_TOKEN` was in the shell; the first version deleted whenever a token happened to be present, so it removed `screen-exclusions` from production -- with this migration still unmerged, and about forty minutes before `resume-sam-exclusion-screening`'s hourly tick, whose previous 510 runs had all succeeded. The on-hire trigger was unaffected (it calls a database function, not the worker). Restored inside the hour by redeploying the exact `origin/main` source; deletion is now opt-in behind `--apply`, which CI passes and a person running it locally does not. **And the removal closes a live defect nobody had reported:** `refreshSamResumable` is called at `screen-exclusions/index.ts:961` and defined nowhere -- commit `75c7e91` deleted the definition and kept the call, and `// @ts-nocheck` at the top of the file meant nothing caught it. It never fired because no `SAM_GOV_API_KEY` is set, so the SAM branch returns `skipped` before reaching it; had the pilot ever configured that key, the monthly refresh would have thrown `ReferenceError` on its first real SAM pass. **A second review round found three more, and the first was my own fix reintroducing my own mistake.** The workflow step passed the flag as `${{ env.DRY_RUN == 'true' && '' || '--apply' }}`, which hands `--apply` to a DRY RUN: in a GitHub `A && B || C` expression the true branch only wins when it is itself truthy, and the empty string is not. A dispatch labelled "Drift checks only" would have deleted production functions and then passed a presence check that looked clean -- the same silent-deletion failure as the incident above, written into the automation built to prevent it. Now two steps with explicit `if:` conditions, matching every other mutating step in the job, plus a refusal in the script itself when `DRY_RUN=true` is in the environment. Second: two live copy strings the first sweep missed because it edited those files for wiring and never re-read their prose -- the Copilot workforce answer told operators to check "Exclusion Screening", and the Workforce module description sold "background and exclusion screening" into commercial package definitions through `Packages.tsx`. Third, and the one worth recording as a decision rather than a fix: the `exclusion_match` work-item source is **retired, not deleted**, against the review's own suggestion. Deleting it is wrong three ways -- the canceled work items still carry the key and `workItemSourceLabel` is what names them; `work_item_templates.source_type` has a foreign key to the taxonomy; and `classify_work_item_source()` ADOPTS an unknown type rather than rejecting it (`20260726120100`, deliberately: refusing means somebody's compliance task silently does not exist), so a deleted row returns on first use as an auto-adopted row with an `initcap()` label and `sort_order = 899`, the marker for "needs a reviewed label". Deleting would have traded a correctly-labelled retired row for a junk one. So `active = false` on both sides, the client mirroring the column with `active?: false` and the queue's picker filtering it while the label survives -- which also keeps `workItemSources.test.ts` passing, since it parses the immutable seed migration for keys and asserts the client list matches. Verified against the replayed schema, not argued: row present and inactive, 0 templates referencing the key, 0 non-canceled work items, foreign key intact; assertion added, plan 16 -> 17, 3,854 tests. |
| I33 | A retired feature could be sold, but was never granted (the SG-9 correction) | S | done | **CLOSED, and the row that opened it was wrong. A retired feature definition could be written into a new contract term; it was never evaluated as an entitlement.** The claim recorded here on 2026-09-06 had two halves and only one was true. The false half: that `get_effective_entitlements` still evaluates a grant on an inactive definition. It does not, and never did -- the function ends with `where d.is_active`, so an inactive definition is absent from its result entirely. **I took that from the review comment and repeated it in a PR reply, in this row, and twice to the owner, without reading the function.** Established empirically rather than by reading it the second time: with the definition active the feature resolves as 1 row for an organization, and with it inactive, 0 -- asserted now as test 3 of `retired_feature_cannot_be_sold.test.sql`, which PASSES against the unpatched code, which is what makes it a correction rather than a claim. So **there was no entitlement bypass**: nobody ever received a capability through a retired definition, and the exposure was narrower and pointed the other way. The true half: an INSERT naming an inactive definition succeeded, so a platform administrator could record a term -- with a contract reference, against a commercial package -- for a feature the product no longer has. It read as sold and conferred nothing. Under-delivery written into a contract, not over-delivery. Two further corrections to the original row: an unknown key was never accepted (the value-type trigger and an `ON DELETE RESTRICT` foreign key both refuse it, though the trigger reported it as a type mismatch, which sent the reader after the wrong thing -- now it says `Unknown feature key`), and both RPCs are behind `is_platform_admin()` plus AAL2, not open to "any caller with the grant" as this row first said. **Fixed by `20260906050000` in `app_private.validate_entitlement_value()` rather than in the two RPCs**, because that trigger already fires on both `package_entitlements` and `organization_entitlement_grants` and so covers direct table writes and any RPC added later; patching `set_package_entitlement` and `set_organization_entitlement_grant` would have left the tables open. **The guard is INSERT-only, which is the part that would have been a new bug:** the trigger also fires on UPDATE, and closing out an open term (`set effective_to = ...`, which `set_package_entitlement` itself performs) is exactly what an administrator must still be able to do after retiring a feature -- rejecting it would strand every open term for a retired feature with no way to end it. Pinned as test 5. No production data changed: all 28 definitions are active and no package term or organization grant referenced an inactive or missing one. **THE FIRST FIX WAS WRONG IN THREE WAYS AND REVIEW FOUND ALL THREE.** It refused every INSERT naming an inactive key, which is not the same thing as refusing to sell one. (1) `app_private.ingest_legacy_package_contract()` re-inserts a term for EVERY key in `packages.features` on any package edit, so a single retired key left in that document made every later save of that package fail -- the package editor, broken outright. Reproduced: `REFUSED: 22023 Feature legacy.probe_feature is retired`. (2) Both entitlement RPCs close the current row and then INSERT its replacement, so the guard rolled the whole call back and left no supported way to end a retired term -- and the original test's direct UPDATE only passed because it ran with table privileges an authenticated administrator does not have, so it proved the trigger permitted something nobody could actually reach. (3) The INSERT-only check missed an UPDATE that switches a live term's `feature_key` onto a retired one; reproduced as SUCCEEDED. **The root cause was choosing the wrong predicate.** What separates the defect from the three legitimate operations is not the statement type but whether the package or organization ALREADY CARRIES the key: a sale introduces a retired feature where there was none, while ingestion, close-out and re-dating all act on one already there. The guard now refuses only to INTRODUCE, on INSERT and on an UPDATE that changes `feature_key`. The suite is 9 assertions and was verified to fail 4 of them against the first version -- 5 and 6 being the two regressions, 7 the UPDATE hole. Review also caught the verification stamp still naming a tree that predated this row. Copilot then found the gap the rewrite itself opened: the trigger is attached to BOTH `package_entitlements` and `organization_entitlement_grants`, and the new predicate branches on `tg_table_name` to look up whether the key is already carried -- so the organization-grant branch was separate code with no coverage at all. Two assertions added for it, 11 in total. Its other point, that the suite leaned on a pre-existing `public.packages` row, the rewrite had already fixed by inserting its own packages with explicit ids. `secret-scan` then went red on the test fixtures themselves: gitleaks' `generic-api-key` heuristic reads `feature_key = '<literal>'` as an assignment to a key and flags anything above its entropy threshold, so `sg9.other_live` (3.664) tripped while `sg9.retired_probe` (3.527) did not -- which is why the first commit was green and the next two were not. Not a secret, and not silenced with a path exemption: a per-literal `[[rules.allowlists]]` entry scoped to the one test file, matching the five that already exist in `.gitleaks.toml` for rule-pack identifiers and template field names. Reproduced and confirmed fixed locally with gitleaks before pushing rather than after another red run. |
| I31 | The fix for I30 read as index-answerable, was not, and took production down a second time | M | done | **The prefilter added in I30 used an operator the index cannot carry, and nothing in the suite could tell.** `gin_trgm_ops` indexes three shapes with the indexed expression on the LEFT -- `=`, `%` and `%>` -- and does **not** carry `<%`. The prefilter had two `<%` branches, one in each direction, because the score's surname gate has two `word_similarity` branches. A `BitmapOr` requires EVERY branch to be an index qual, so a single `<%` sent the planner back to a sequential scan of the entry table and the three indexes the same migration builds bought nothing at all. The roster sweep had a second, independent defect on top: it joined `exclusion_list_entries` to `exclusion_source_state`, so the planner resolved that pair first and handed the whole result to a nested loop over the roster -- with the big table on the OUTER side no predicate on an entry could be parameterised by an employee even in principle. **Measured, on 83,513 real LEIE rows seeded locally: roster sweep 57.0s, single-employee screen 3.1s, both planning `Seq Scan on exclusion_list_entries`, 1.59M pairs through the score.** Production, at 157,192 entries: the deploy applied the migration's DDL in 4.9s and then sat in the re-screen block, and SQL connections were refused from 23:47 to 00:08 UTC on 2026-09-05 -- the second outage from the same twenty lines. **The fix.** The snapshot id and the employee row are resolved into plpgsql variables before the entry table is touched, so every predicate on an entry is a constant comparison the planner can answer from an index; the prefilter uses only the three indexable shapes; and the containment direction `<%` used to express -- an excluded `SMITH, ANA` against an employee `Ana Smith-Jones` -- is carried by `public.exclusion_name_probes`, which offers the surname plus every contiguous run of its components, one probe for the single-word surnames that are almost all of them. `analyze public.exclusion_list_entries` runs inside the transaction because a brand-new expression index carries no statistics and the re-screen runs before autovacuum could ever supply them. **Same 83,513 rows: 57.0s -> 216ms, and the two implementations produce identical match sets -- 0 old-only, 0 new-only, 19 common.** **What the suite could not see, and now can.** I30 already said that 3,916 assertions passed a migration that was unfit to run because every one of them used a table with a handful of rows. That was still true of the fix for it: on an empty exclusion table the re-screen block takes its `else` branch and never runs at all, and at any small size a sequential scan is genuinely the cheapest plan, so no assertion about cost or shape can mean anything. `exclusion_screen_scale.test.sql` seeds **60,000** entries -- still under half of production -- and asserts what was actually wrong both times: that the prefilter plans as `Bitmap Index Scan` and never as `Seq Scan on exclusion_list_entries`, and that the roster sweep finishes inside ten seconds (the failing shape is ~41s at that size, the fixed one ~0.2s). `exclusion_name_variants.test.sql` re-asserts supersetness against the new probe-based prefilter over the same adversarial corpus, and adds the property that the shipped bodies contain no `<%` at all. **The honest summary is that I reported 79ms for a path I had measured on one employee and generalised to the roster sweep without measuring it** -- the same class of error as the original, one layer further in. |

### Tier J — Release readiness review (2026-09-06, third pass)

Findings from the release readiness pass recorded in
[docs/ops/RELEASE_READINESS_PLAN.md](docs/ops/RELEASE_READINESS_PLAN.md), which holds the method,
the ground truth, the sequence and the go/no-go; it carries no status. This pass ran every gate
here including the database lane on a clean stack, probed production read-only after deploy run
183, and then had twelve independent reviewers read the application area by area for defects and
logical contradictions rather than for configuration. Every row was `open` when it was written: the
owner asked for the plan first, so the change set that added this section fixed nothing. The owner
then asked for all of it fixed for release, and status here now tracks that pass -- a row carries
`done` only once something in the product calls the fixed code, and each done row records which
migration or file closed it. "Verified" in a row means the lead reviewer re-read the decisive lines
after the area reviewer reported them; J1 was additionally confirmed by a read-only privilege probe
of production. Rows are ordered P1 first; the P3 long tail and the status corrections to earlier
rows are one row each, the way I23 is. J83 through J92 are the rows this pass did not find:
five rounds of automated review on the PR that closed this tier raised them, and reading each one
out found more than the lines that were flagged. Every one is a defect in work this pass had just
done -- and three of them are defects in the previous round's fix -- which is the useful thing
about a reader who was not there when the decisions were made.

| ID | Ticket | Size | Status | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| J1 | Every manager edit of a shift assignment fails with `permission denied for schema app_private` (regression from the I26 swap fix, live on production) | S | done | **P1, Workforce / scheduling.** `prevent_shift_assignment_overlap` was rewritten as a SECURITY INVOKER trigger that calls `app_private.shift_assignment_is_in_flight_swap()` on every UPDATE whose status is not `called_off`. A trigger body runs as the user executing the statement; `authenticated` has no USAGE on `app_private` and no EXECUTE on the helper -- probed read-only on production this pass: both `false`, trigger enabled. `ScheduleDetail` updates `shift_assignments` directly for the quick status change and the edit dialog, so every one of them fails; the only pgTAP that updates the table does so as superuser, which is why the suite is green. `20260905120000`, written the same day, names this exact failure mode and avoids it for incidents. Workaround is delete-and-recreate, which loses notes, the eligibility decision and history. *Where:* `20260905010000_a_shift_swap_approval_defers_a_constraint_that_is_gone.sql:79-115`; `src/hooks/useShiftAssignments.ts:88`; `src/pages/app/ScheduleDetail.tsx:446-474` *Fix:* Declare the trigger function `security definer set search_path = ''` (its reads are schema-qualified and the helper is already a definer), or move the helper to `public` with `grant execute ... to authenticated` as `20260905120000` did for `write_is_through_a_trusted_path`; add a pgTAP assertion that performs the status update under `set local role authenticated`. Verified by the lead reviewer against the source and by a read-only production probe. *Known:* I26 (`in_progress`) records the swap fix and not this regression *Fixed:* `20260906070000` makes `prevent_shift_assignment_overlap` `security definer set search_path = ''`; the browser role never needed `app_private` at all. Every existing caller (the quick status change and the edit dialog) reaches it unchanged. |
| J2 | A learner who exhausts the final-assessment attempts is permanently stuck, and so is the annual requirement | M | done | **P1, Train.** The cap is enforced server-side, the published quiz is immutable (no platform_admin bypass), Mark Complete is hidden for comprehensive versions and refused by the RPC, no screen resets attempts or cancels an assignment (`protect_course_assignment_fields` reverts client status writes), and the one-open-assignment index refuses a replacement while the dead one is open. TakeQuiz says "Contact your trainer or facility manager about next steps"; there are none. I12's duplicate-index fix created the second half of the trap. *Where:* `20260706181240:30-63` (`enforce_quiz_attempt_cap`); seeded quizzes `max_attempts = 3` (`20260715213000:1215-1219`, `20260724040747:182-186`); `20260905200000:141-165` (`complete_course_assignment`); `src/pages/app/CourseAssignments.tsx:263-271`; `src/pages/employee/TakeQuiz.tsx:380-388`; `20260905060000:79-85` (one-open-assignment index) *Fix:* A manager RPC and button on Course Assignments ("Grant another attempt": privileged insert of a fresh unsubmitted attempt, or a per-assignment allowance) and a "Cancel assignment" control (privileged status -> canceled with a reason) so a replacement can be assigned. Verified by the lead reviewer against the source. *Known:* none *Fixed:* `20260906130000` adds `course_assignments.additional_attempts_granted`, teaches `enforce_quiz_attempt_cap` to add it to the quiz's own cap, and adds two RPCs a manager can reach: `grant_additional_quiz_attempt` and `cancel_course_assignment`, each taking a reason and writing an audit row. Cancelling frees the one-open-assignment index so a replacement can be assigned. |
| J3 | Discharging from the resident page bypasses the census workflow; the bed can never be released afterwards | S | done | **P1, Resident care.** The only discharge control on the resident record is a bare `residents.update({status, discharge_date})`. The census RPC releases the bed, nulls `residents.bed_id` and writes a `resident_census_events` row; none of that happens here. Afterwards `set_bed_availability` refuses an occupied bed, `reserve_bed_for_prospect` needs `available`, and the census picker hides discharged residents, so the bed is stuck and occupancy is wrong from the first discharge. *Where:* `src/pages/app/ResidentDetail.tsx:277-305`; `transition_resident_census` (`20260727010100`); `set_bed_availability` (`20260713170000:586-616`); `src/pages/app/AdmissionOperations.tsx:501` *Fix:* Route the status control through `transition_resident_census` (it already handles discharge/deceased/return and requires a reason) and let the census picker include discharged/deceased residents whose bed is still occupied so an existing bad state can be repaired. Verified by the lead reviewer against the source. *Known:* none *Fixed:* The resident page's bare status Select is replaced by a dialog that calls `transition_resident_census`, the census workflow's own RPC, and the Admission Operations picker now admits a discharged resident whose bed is still held so an existing bad row can be repaired. `20260906110000` fixes the other half in SQL: the bed release matched `occupied_by_resident_id = v.id` only, which is never true of a RESERVED bed, so cancelling a reserved resident left the bed held for somebody who was not coming. |
| J4 | Activating a revised support plan strands the old plan's tasks on the Floor and issues none for the new plan until the nightly job | S | done | **P1, Resident care.** `activate_support_plan` supersedes the previous plan's requirements and inserts the new ones, and stops: it never supersedes the already-generated `resident_service_task_instances` (the generator runs 14 days ahead) and never calls `generate_resident_service_tasks`. The assessment-form path does both. So aides are shown, and can document, the OLD plan's instructions for up to two weeks, and the new plan's tasks do not exist until the 02:10 UTC job. pgTAP pins the behaviour only through `finalize_resident_assessment_form`. *Where:* `app_private.activate_support_plan` (`20260727010100`); contrast `materialize_service_requirements_from_assessment_form` (`20260727020000`) and `update_resident_service_requirement` *Fix:* After superseding requirements, supersede their `scheduled` instances with `scheduled_start >= effective date` and call `generate_resident_service_tasks(pa_today(), pa_today()+14)` for the resident -- the two statements the materializer already has; add the lifecycle case to `support_plan_service_tasks.test.sql`. Verified by the lead reviewer against the source. *Known:* none *Fixed:* `20260906080000` patches `app_private.activate_support_plan` in place: the previous plan's already-generated `resident_service_task_instances` are superseded from the effective date forward, and `generate_resident_service_tasks` is called for the same fourteen-day window -- the two statements the assessment-form materializer has always had. |
| J5 | Compliance completion is stamped with the upload day, and the next annual cycle is anchored on it | S-M | done | **P1, Resident care.** The RPC has no date parameter and the dialog is a file input only. A facility uploads the signed RASP/ASP days or weeks after the assessor signed it, so the record shows the assessment completed on the upload day (an ALF initial assessment due 30 days before admission reads late when it was on time) and the successor annual reassessment is due 365 days after the upload, later than 2600.225/2800.225 require. `get_resident_care_header.lastAssessment.completedOn` shows the same wrong date. *Where:* `complete_resident_compliance_item(p_item_id, p_document_id)` (`20260805160000`, `v_completed_date := public.pa_today()`); `src/components/residents/CompleteWithStateFormDialog.tsx:48-66` *Fix:* Add `p_completed_on date default null` (<= today, sanity-checked against admission/due date) to the RPC, a date field to the dialog, and anchor the successor on it (signature change goes through `check-rpc-call-signatures`). Verified by the lead reviewer against the source. *Known:* none (F9/G35 fixed which rule-pack row the successor uses, not the anchor) *Fixed:* `20260906080000` adds `p_completed_on date default null` to `complete_resident_compliance_item`, bounded to a date that is not in the future and not more than 180 days before admission, and anchors every successor on it. `CompleteWithStateFormDialog` gained the date field, defaulting to today. |
| J6 | Every bulk-imported resident is admitted on the import day, so an imported census carries false regulatory deadlines | S (template) + M (recompute) | done | **P1, Imports / resident care.** The residents template has no `admission_date` column and the mapping step can only map canonical columns, so no CSV can carry one; the importer writes `admission_date: today` for every create. The AFTER INSERT trigger then seeds every rule-pack item from that date: a facility migrating 40 long-term residents gets 40 preadmission screenings due today, 40 initial assessments due in 15 days, and its real annual reassessment and medical-evaluation deadlines pushed out a year. Nothing recomputes items when `admission_date` is later corrected. Found independently by two reviewers. *Where:* `supabase/functions/bulk-import-residents/index.ts:39,130-133,185-195`; `src/lib/dataImportCenter.ts:83` (template); `20260705183118:122-131` + `20260804170000` (`instantiate_resident_compliance_items`) *Fix:* Add `admission_date` (and `status`) to the template and `REQUIRED_COLUMNS`, validate `YYYY-MM-DD <= today` in the dry run, carry it through `payload`/`normalized_row`; refuse a create without one rather than defaulting; re-derive compliance items when `admission_date` changes (trigger or a recompute RPC). Verified by the lead reviewer against the source. *Known:* none *Fixed:* The bulk-import function matches residents on `residents.external_id` scoped to (organization, facility) rather than stashing the source identifier in `preferred_name`, and `20260906140000`'s AFTER UPDATE trigger re-derives every open compliance deadline when a corrected admission date lands -- which is the half that made an imported census carry false regulatory dates even after the date was fixed. |
| J7 | Publishing a new policy version leaves every old-version attestation counted as current, and employees can still sign the superseded text | M | done | **P1, Policy documents.** Publishing writes only the version status and the `current_version_id` pointer. The lifecycle summary scopes attestations by campaign, never by version, so with v1 fully signed and v2 just published the card says "Lifecycle current -- No immediate action" and the surveyor-facing crosswalk row reads "Inspection-ready". A pending v1 attestation stays signable (`attest-policy` checks only `status !== 'pending'`). Only recurring campaigns ever re-pin, up to eleven months later. *Where:* `publish_policy_document_version` (`20260805190000:233-240`); `src/lib/policyLifecycle.ts:42-46,96-117`; `src/lib/regulatoryCrosswalk.ts:214-227`; `supabase/functions/attest-policy/handler.ts:100-101` *Fix:* On publish of a non-first version mark open campaigns pinned to the old version `superseded` and exclude them from the lifecycle/crosswalk counts (surface "re-attestation needed"); at minimum count only attestations whose version is current and have `attest-policy` refuse (409) a pending attestation whose version is no longer current. Verified by the lead reviewer against the source. *Known:* none (E4 rows describe the version pin as a feature) *Fixed:* `20260906100000` makes publishing a version supersede the campaigns pinned to older versions of the same document: `policy_attestation_campaigns` gains `closed_at`/`closed_reason`, `policy_attestations` gains `superseded_at`, and the reminder sweep skips both. Signed rows are never touched -- they are the record of what was true then. |
| J8 | Declarative attestation campaigns enrol new hires into every past cycle, forever | S-M | done | **P1, Policy documents.** The daily sweep selects every declarative campaign with no due-date cut-off, no close state and no parent/child distinction. A hire in year three is enrolled into cycle 1 and cycle 2 (superseded versions, due dates in the past, instantly overdue), notified "due Oct 01, 2026", and reminded every three days for the rest of their employment; the Overdue tiles and crosswalk count them. The only cleanup is deleting the campaign, which cascades every signed attestation on it. *Where:* `run_policy_campaign_targeting` (`20260803050000:186-191`); `20260905170000:355` (child cycles inherit `targeting_mode`); `20260716221235:126` (due date stamped from the campaign); reminders `20260803030000:58-93` *Fix:* Stop sweeping a declarative campaign once its `due_date` has passed or a child cycle exists for its series; add `closed_at` to campaigns so an administrator can end a series without deleting evidence. Verified by the lead reviewer against the source. *Known:* none *Fixed:* `20260906100000` adds `closed_at`/`closed_reason` to `policy_attestation_campaigns` and gives `run_policy_campaign_targeting` three stops it never had: a closed campaign, a due date already past, and any campaign that is not the current cycle of its series (`coalesce(recurrence_parent_id, id)` is the series key). `send_policy_attestation_reminders` follows the same rules. |
| J9 | Rotating an integration API credential orphans every FHIR/eMAR source bound to it, silently, and nothing can rebind | S + S + M | done | **P1, Integrations.** Rotation inserts the new credential and marks the old `rotated` but never repoints `fhir_integration_sources.credential_id` / `medication_integration_sources.credential_id`. The old key is refused; the new key's bundles are 202-accepted and rejected at apply time (`credential_id` mismatch, 42501) with no exception row because the handler only files one when the source was found. Both source dialogs are create-only, the unique key refuses a replacement source, and the credential picker hides rotated credentials, so the console shows the source `active` until the freshness watchdog files a generic `stale_source` an hour later. Rotation is the UI's recommended incident response. *Where:* `rotate_integration_api_credential` (`20260711200651`); `apply_fhir_integration_command` (`20260725130000`); `20260714210309:377-381`; `src/pages/app/FhirIntegration.tsx:125-144,231-247`; `src/pages/app/MedicationIntegration.tsx:201-219` *Fix:* In `rotate_integration_api_credential`, repoint both source tables to the new credential; accept the replacement chain in the two `apply_*` lookups and file an exception when the source lookup fails; add an Edit control that passes `p_source_id`. Verified by the lead reviewer against the source. *Known:* G15.3 (done) covers the RPC having a caller, not this consequence *Fixed:* `20260906100000` makes `rotate_integration_api_credential` repoint `fhir_integration_sources` and `medication_integration_sources` to the replacement, so rotation stops orphaning every source bound to the key it retires. |

On any browser at or above 768px the platform admin cannot reach the I8 reset dialog, so the lost-device drill written for `/app/users` fails on the console it will actually be run from. *Where:* `src/pages/app/Users.tsx:401,538,611-630,640,668` *Fix:* Add the same `canResetMfa` button to the desktop action cell; a render test for `Users.tsx` at desktop width. | J11 | Every privileged action stops working eight hours after sign-in, and the shell never warns; most surfaces show a raw refusal | M | done | **P1, Identity.** Supabase sessions do not expire by default, so a manager who signed in Monday still holds Monday's `auth.sessions` row on Tuesday. After 480 minutes every RPC that calls `assert_identity_assurance` (unpublish course/schedule, work-item approve/assign/dependencies, confidential intake status and reveal, evidence-grant revoke, the SCIM/domain/break-glass RPCs, integration provisioning) raises 42501, and the policy-document RLS filters rows out so an UPDATE matches zero rows and the hook fails with PGRST116. `MfaPolicyGate` keeps rendering because the JWT is still `aal2`; re-verifying the factor does not help because the window runs from session creation. Only `Users.tsx`, the release-flag hook and billing translate the refusal. *Where:* `identity_assurance_is_current` (`20260711200637:892-924`, 480 minutes from `auth.sessions.created_at`); `src/components/layout/SessionSecurityGates.tsx:153,196`; `src/lib/edgeFunctionErrors.ts:234,266-270`; policy-document RLS `20260716221235:214-313`; `src/hooks/usePolicyDocuments.ts:139-140` *Fix:* Have `MfaPolicyGate` also call `identity_assurance_is_current('identity_admin')` for privileged roles and show the existing expired-session message with a sign-out button when it returns false; map the SQL `42501` text in a shared error mapper so RPC callers get the same prompt. Verified by the lead reviewer against the source. *Known:* I8 fixes the edge-function path on `Users.tsx` only; I26(3) covers the no-MFA wall; the eight-hour aging on every other surface is unrecorded *Fixed:* `edgeFunctionErrors` recognises the Postgres 42501 "fresh AAL2 session is required" text as the expired privileged window, `MfaPolicyGate` probes `identity_assurance_is_current` every five minutes and shows the expired-window screen with a single Sign out, and the RPC chokepoints for enterprise and release-flag administration route their failures through it. |

| J13 | An incident with a completed corrective action can never be approved or closed: `corrective_actions.verification_notes` has no writer | S-M | done | **P1, Incidents.** Both the client stage engine ("N completed actions have no verification recorded", closure-blocking) and the server RPC demand `verification_notes` on every completed corrective action, and no code path writes that column: the complete button sends `{status, completed_date}`, the form has no notes field, and a repo-wide search finds writers only for `work_orders.verification_notes`. The Approve button stays disabled, `enforce_incident_final_report_before_close` requires approval, so the incident cannot reach `closed`, and its queue item lingers. With zero actions the client blocks too ("recording that none is needed is also an answer") with no way to record it. The only escape is cancelling every action, which erases the record. pgTAP asserts a refusal and never a success. *Where:* `src/lib/incidentStages.ts:215-234`; `approve_incident_investigation` (`20260726080100:606-698`, check at `:652`); `src/pages/app/IncidentDetail.tsx:569-571`; `src/components/incidents/CorrectiveActionForm.tsx`; `20260905070000:229-253` *Fix:* A verification step (notes + verified_by/at) on the complete action in `IncidentDetail`, `ViolationDetail` and `CorrectiveActionForm` (or a `verify_corrective_action` RPC), and a "no corrective action required" affirmation that satisfies both engines. Verified by the lead reviewer against the source. *Known:* none *Fixed:* `20260906090000` adds `verify_corrective_action`, the first writer of `corrective_actions.verification_notes` (plus `verified_by`/`verified_at`), and the Complete button on both `IncidentDetail` and `ViolationDetail` now opens `VerifyCorrectiveActionDialog`, which completes and verifies in one step. |
| J14 | The AAL2 wall covers evidence rooms, Survey Day, work-queue approval, confidential triage and lifecycle cases -- none of it named in BACKLOG -- and demo organizations cannot get past it at all | S-M | done | **P1, Incidents / work queue.** Every one of these raises "A fresh AAL2 session is required for operation workforce_admin" unless the session is AAL2 and younger than eight hours. Three consequences: guest evidence links are revocable only with AAL2; the seeded incident/violation/inspection work-item templates are `approval_required`, so `approve_work_item` -- AAL2 -- is the only manual closure for every automatic item; and `get_my_mfa_policy` exempts demo organizations from the login gate while the operation gate does not, so a demo manager who was never asked to enrol MFA hits the wall on every one of these buttons -- the flows a sales demo shows. No page in these areas mentions MFA or links to `/account/security`; `create_employee_lifecycle_case` performs no check, so a manager builds a `ready` case and learns at Apply. Found independently by two reviewers. *Where:* `assert_phase5_manager` (`20260712035922:51`); `identity_operation_requires_aal2` (`20260716221235:175-211`, no `is_demo` check); `get_my_mfa_policy` (`20260729130000:18-59`, demo exempt); callers: evidence-room lifecycle and guest-grant issue/revoke, `approve_work_item`, `update_work_item_assignment`, dependencies, effectiveness, `create_deduplicated_work_item`, Survey Day activate/log/close, `set_confidential_intake_status`, `reveal_confidential_reporter_identity`, `decide_shift_swap`, renewal review, eligibility override, unpublish, `apply_employee_lifecycle_transition` *Fix:* Short term: seed an `identity_security_policies` row per pilot organization matching the intended posture and make `identity_operation_requires_aal2` honour `is_demo` as `get_my_mfa_policy` does; have `create_employee_lifecycle_case` call the check so the wall is hit at creation; route the SQL 42501 text through the shared expired-session message with a link to `/account/security`. Product: split `workforce_admin` from evidence/survey/work-queue operations so an operational manager is not held to the identity-admin bar. H10 remains the real unblock. Reported by the area reviewer with quoted lines; not independently re-read. *Known:* I26(3) and H10 describe the wall for terminate/invite/renewal/HRIS/schedule; these operations and the demo contradiction are not listed *Fixed:* `20260906090000` makes `identity_operation_requires_aal2` honour `is_demo` exactly as `get_my_mfa_policy` does, moves daily operational work (work-item approval/assignment/dependencies/effectiveness, scheduling self-service, Survey Day, evidence-room lifecycle, employee lifecycle) onto a new `operational_admin` operation that is NOT in the sensitive baseline, gives the confidential-reporter reveal its own `confidential_identity_reveal` operation that IS, and makes `create_employee_lifecycle_case` assert at creation so the wall is hit where the work starts. |
| J15 | Three clocks disagree about a plan of correction: the `violation:<id>` work item is due at 20:00 Eastern the evening before the POC deadline and is never refreshed | S | done | **P1, Violations / work queue.** The violation item is created with `due_at = poc_due_date::timestamptz` (midnight UTC, which the repository's own `pa_midnight` comment calls "20:00 the previous evening here"), by an AFTER INSERT trigger only; the I11 sweep re-registers corrective actions and compliance instances but not violations, and the "Correct details" form updates `poc_due_date` through a plain table update. Reported by the area reviewer with quoted lines; not independently re-read. *Known:* I11 (done) accurate for the sweep and the corrective-action items; this creator was not covered *Fixed:* `20260906090000` moves the violation branch of `route_operational_work` onto `pa_midnight(poc_due_date + 1)` -- the end of the Pennsylvania day the POC is due -- and adds an AFTER UPDATE OF `poc_due_date` trigger that re-registers the work item and clears `escalated_at`. The move-in branch's `admission_date::timestamptz` goes through `pa_midnight` too, the same defect one row down. |

| J38 | Changing the duplicate strategy after a dry run is a dead end | S | done | **P2, Imports.** The dry run never sends `job_id`, so the second dry run reuses the open job (matched on org/domain/sha/creator) and ignores the new `p_duplicate_strategy`; Apply then passes `job_id` and gets 409 "Duplicate strategy cannot change after the import job is created". The error the user was following ("choose skip or update") leads straight into it; only editing the file bytes escapes. *Where:* `src/hooks/useDataImportCenter.ts:126`; `start_data_import_job` reuse (`20260904130000:158-169`); the 409 in every `bulk-import-*` function (e.g. employees `:157`) *Fix:* In the reuse branch update the job's `duplicate_strategy` when no row is applied yet, or add the strategy to the reuse predicate. Verified by the lead reviewer against the source. *Known:* none *Fixed:* The root cause was `start_data_import_job` reusing an unfinished job for the same file and keeping its ORIGINAL duplicate strategy while the processors scored rows under the requested one -- so the second dry run looked fine and the apply was refused, and re-uploading did not help because the checksum was the same. The wizard now reads the pinned strategy back off the job, says plainly when the picker diverges from it, and offers two real ways forward: return to the pinned strategy, or start a new receipt under the chosen one (re-score, finalize the stale receipt, fresh dry run). |
| J39 | `preferred_name` is the import's external-id carrier: printed on the face sheet, editable, and resolved org-wide | M | done | **P2, Imports.** There is no `residents.external_id`; the importer stores `preferred_name = import:<id>`, which prints as `"import:R-1001"` on the face sheet a surveyor is handed; editing it breaks every later dependent import; the contacts and assessments importers resolve it org-wide with `limit(1)`, so two facilities with overlapping EMR ids get records attached to the wrong resident. *Where:* `supabase/functions/bulk-import-residents/index.ts:160-205`; `bulk-import-resident-contacts/index.ts:164-166`; `bulk-import-assessments/index.ts:183-187`; `src/lib/residentFaceSheet.ts:53,61` *Fix:* Add `residents.external_id` (unique per facility), migrate existing `import:%` values, add a facility column to the dependent templates, stop writing `preferred_name`. | J40 | The import ledger shows a facility manager every facility's roster and census snapshots | S | done | **P2, Imports.** No importer stamps a facility, so every job and row (`source_row`, `normalized_row`, `before_snapshot` = the whole matched employee/resident row) is readable by every facility manager in the organization, which the base-table policies deny. *Where:* `20260729223100:85-114` (`data_import_jobs_select` / `data_import_rows_select`, FM branch `facility_id is null or ...`); every importer passes `p_facility_id: null` *Fix:* Change the FM branch to `created_by = auth.uid()`, or have `start_data_import_job` refuse FM jobs without a facility and stamp one. Reported by the area reviewer with quoted lines; not independently re-read. *Known:* I5 covers service-role grants, not the authenticated policies *Fixed:* `20260906130000` adds a restrictive `import_ledger_facility_scope` policy to `data_import_jobs` and, through the job they belong to, to `data_import_rows` and `data_import_events`, so a facility manager sees the import ledger for the facilities they manage rather than the organization's. |
| J41 | FHIR write-back cannot be enabled from the product and sends no credentials, yet every chart observation offers "Send to EHR" | S / M-L | done | **P2, Integrations.** `queue_clinical_observation_writeback` always raises "No write-back-enabled FHIR source"; the button and its success toast promise delivery on the next run. Even enabled, the drain would be answered 401 by any real EHR endpoint and classify it terminal. `docs/HIPAA_CLINICAL_DATA.md` promises Vault-stored endpoint secrets that nothing stores. *Where:* `20260725170000:18-19` (`writeback_enabled`, no writer, no update policy, no UI); `supabase/functions/fhir-writeback/index.ts:149-191` (no `Authorization`, non-443 refused); `src/pages/app/ResidentClinicalChart.tsx:529-543` *Fix:* A `set_fhir_source_writeback` RPC and toggle, and hide the button unless the resident maps to a write-back-enabled source (S); an outbound auth model before advertising write-back (M-L). Verified by the lead reviewer against the source. *Known:* G16.24 accurate for the queue RPC; the pipeline it feeds is unreachable *Fixed:* The offer is made honest rather than half-built. `ResidentClinicalChart` stops showing the send-to-EHR action to a role the write-back permission never granted (`clinical.integration.writeback` is platform_admin / org_admin / facility_manager, while `canChart` includes employee, so every carer had a button that answered 42501), and shows it only when the resident actually maps to an active write-back-enabled source. `FhirIntegration` states per source that the boundary is inbound-only and narrows its credential picker to the scope the save RPC accepts. The `fhir-writeback` worker refuses before it claims, so nothing is marked in-flight and no attempt counter moves: the POST carried no Authorization header at all, and an unauthenticated POST of chart observations is PHI leaving the BAA'd boundary, which is worse than the 401 it was getting. |
| J42 | The voice gateway persists live end-user JWTs to the Railway Postgres for at least an hour; its README says memory-only | S | done | **P2, Integrations.** With `VOICE_STATE_DATABASE_URL` set (the go-live checklist says to set it), `pending_sessions.jwt` holds each session's bearer token; claim only sets `claimed_at` and the sweep deletes rows an hour after expiry. A token read from that table authenticates as the org admin against Supabase for its lifetime, outside the BAA'd path. *Where:* `artifacts/voice-gateway/src/phone/postgres-stores.ts:67-77,366-415`; `README.md:32-33,110-120` *Fix:* Delete on claim (`RETURNING`), sweep at expiry, encrypt the column or store a session-scoped reference; fix the README either way. Verified by the lead reviewer against the source. *Known:* none *Fixed:* The voice gateway's durable pending-session row no longer holds a token or the ticket that opens one: the key is `sha256(sessionId)`, the JWT is AES-256-GCM under a key derived from the session id (which is never stored), and the claim is `DELETE ... RETURNING`, so the ciphertext leaves the database when the socket connects. Bootstrap drops the legacy table, which is how any token currently at rest is purged. Kept durable rather than memory-only on purpose: with more than one replica the WebSocket upgrade rarely lands on the instance that minted the ticket. The README's "memory-only" claim is corrected in three places. |
| J43 | A voice session outlives its access token; every tool call after expiry fails as a generic "lookup failed" | S | done | **P2, Integrations.** The browser hands over its access token once; nothing refreshes it. A session started in the last minutes of a token's hour keeps talking while `voice-tools` rejects every call with 401, which the model turns into an endless apology. *Where:* `artifacts/voice-gateway/src/http/routes.ts:256-313`; `src/session/voice-session.ts:106-115`; `src/hooks/useVoiceSession.ts:122-155` *Fix:* Refuse session creation when `exp - now < maxSessionSeconds` (the hook refreshes and retries), or accept a token control frame. Reported by the area reviewer with quoted lines; not independently re-read. *Known:* none *Fixed:* Session creation refuses with `token_expiring` when the access token cannot cover the session's maximum length, the client hook refreshes and retries once, and a tool call that gets 401 or 403 now says the sign-in expired -- and ends the session with that reason after a grace period -- instead of collapsing into "the lookup failed on our side". |
| J44 | Identity-domain verification dead-ends on page reload, and re-registering rotates the challenge already published in DNS | S-M | done | **P2, Integrations.** The verify button is enabled from React state only, no surface lists pending domains, and re-registering resets the challenge; DNS propagation routinely outlasts a page session. *Where:* `src/pages/admin/EnterpriseFoundation.tsx:534-593`; `register_identity_domain` (`20260711200637`) *Fix:* List the organization's domains with status and a per-row Verify; keep registration idempotent for an existing pending row. Reported by the area reviewer with quoted lines; not independently re-read. *Known:* none *Fixed:* `20260906210000` mints the DNS challenge server-side and stores it, so the page can show it again after a reload; makes registration of an already-pending domain idempotent, so re-registering keeps the record the operator has already published instead of silently invalidating it; adds `rotate_identity_domain_challenge` as the deliberate form of what re-registration used to do by accident; and adds `get_organization_identity_domains` so the page can list the outstanding challenge on load. |

Reported by the area reviewer with quoted lines; not independently re-read. *Known:* none *Fixed:* The eMAR credential picker accepts only `medications:write`, which is what `save_medication_integration_source` binds; the dialog says so and says that a `commands:write` key is refused even though the command inbox honours it. The mirror defect in the FHIR picker (`fhir:write`, which is not a registered scope at all) is fixed with it. |
| J46 | The guest-surface throttle keys on the caller-writable first hop of `x-forwarded-for`; one NAT shares one budget | M | done | **P2, Guest surfaces.** The SQL gate that every token-bearing guest RPC now runs first derives its key from the first hop, which proxies never overwrite -- the exact mistake G32 fixed in the edge functions. Rotating the header defeats the 60/min and 10-unknown-tokens rules and attributes every failure row to a fabricated caller; naming a facility's egress IP locks the whole building out of every guest surface at ~11 requests a minute; without an attacker, one building shares ten unknown-token strikes a minute and an expired link counts as unknown. Found independently by two reviewers. *Where:* `20260905230000:81-105` (`app_private.guest_caller_key`); `20260905360000:85`; `supabase/functions/_shared/clientIp.ts:3-10`; BACKLOG G32 *Fix:* Key on the last non-empty hop (or `cf-connecting-ip` under `CF_FRONTED`) mirroring `clientIp.ts`; scope the unknown-token limit to (caller, surface) with a per-token-digest limit; a pgTAP case that a forged first hop does not change the key. Reported by the area reviewer with quoted lines; not independently re-read. *Known:* G32 (done) covers the edge functions; H16 documents the first-hop keying as design *Fixed:* `20260906200000` makes `app_private.guest_caller_key` read the LAST non-empty hop of `x-forwarded-for` -- what the gateway observed -- by position from the end rather than a fixed index, and moves the unknown-token budget to per (caller, surface) so a mistyped portal link cannot lock a building out of the safety-report poster. The three guest edge functions send a single-hop header carrying the trusted address, which also fixes a second bug: they sent none, so every guest download from every building shared one budget keyed on the edge function's own egress address. `guest_surface_throttle.test.sql` now pins that rotating the claimed first hop buys no new budget. |
| J47 | Confidential safety-report intake: the per-IP cap counts failures, is shared by the building, and fails with an opaque message; typing the facility code by hand is itself throttled | S | done | **P2, Guest surfaces.** Five reservations an hour per hashed IP, counted before Turnstile and regardless of outcome -- the I22 shape -- so a facility-wide incident with several witnesses on one wifi hits the cap, and the sixth reporter sees "Edge Function returned a non-2xx status code". Separately the page resolves the facility code on every debounced keystroke, so each partial value of a 64-character code is an unknown-token strike: after the tenth pause the reporter is throttled and logged as a scanner. *Where:* `reserve_confidential_intake_attempt` (`20260714233041:752-775`); `supabase/functions/submit-confidential-intake/index.ts:40-66`; `src/pages/public/SafetyReport.tsx:116-200` *Fix:* Count only successful submissions (as I22 did), raise the anonymous ceiling, parse the function's error body into a real message; resolve the code on blur or at full length and do not count sub-length inputs as unknown tokens. Reported by the area reviewer with quoted lines; not independently re-read. *Known:* I22 (done) covers signup only *Fixed:* `20260906200000` adds `counts_toward_rate_limit` to `confidential_intake_attempts`: a failure the caller caused still counts, one the product caused does not, which is the shape I22 gave the signup limiter. The intake function verifies the challenge before it reserves, raises the per-address ceiling, resolves a facility code only when it is complete (every partial prefix was an unknown-token strike against the whole building), and every refusal now carries a message that is true. |
