| J99 | An annual-hours card that read compliant off training the server never credits | S | done | **P1, workforce compliance.** Asked for as a comprehensive debug of the whole application before release. Every existing gate was already green on this tree before anything changed -- typecheck, 1,934 CareBase and 91 voice-gateway unit tests, every static gate in `check:all`, the production build with CI's placeholders, `check:startup`, `check:bundle`, `check:dependencies` (no vulnerabilities), `check:dhs-sources` (37 of 37 official PA links resolve), the Deno edge suite (311 tests), and the database lane against a live local stack: 678 migrations replay from empty, 186 pgTAP files and 4,137 assertions pass, `db lint` clean, `db advisors` zero errors, and `database.types.ts` byte-identical to types regenerated from that stack. So this pass was spent on the things a green gate does not cover. **The finding.** `EmployeeDetail`'s annual-hours card recomputes earned hours on the employee's own training year rather than the stored calendar-year bucket (J28), and `bucketHoursInWindow` documents itself as mirroring `recalculate_compliance_core`'s `legacy_earned` + `course_earned` CTEs "including their exclusions". It mirrored one of them. Those CTEs join `creditable_types`, which admits a training type only while it `is_active`, its `state` equals the facility's, its `applies_to_facility_type` is the facility's own or `BOTH`, and the employer's current audience decision for it is neither `pending_review` nor `not_applicable`. The client folded EVERY type carrying an `hour_bucket` into its map, because `useListTrainingTypes()` is called here with no filter at all -- all six facility types' catalogs, every state's, and deactivated ones. **Measured on the running stack rather than argued.** A PCH employee with 4 creditable hours plus 8 hours recorded against `HHA-AIDE-ANNUAL` (a Home Health Aide in-service) gets `required_hours 12.00, completed_hours 4.00, status incomplete` back from `recalculate_compliance_core`; the card summed 12.00, which against a 12-hour requirement reads as met. Both numbers sat on the same card, the wrong one first and larger, and the one a surveyor would act on said the employee was short. **Where the uncountable hours came from is the same page.** The Record Training dialog's type picker is that same unfiltered list, so a personal care home administrator is offered "Home Health Aide Annual In-Service", "Group Home Direct Service Worker Annual Training" and seven more that apply to nobody at that facility. Picking one writes a real record, which the training matrix then shows -- `get_training_matrix_page` renders a type outside the employee's facility type as `not_applicable` rather than `missing`, deliberately, because "a real record always wins over that scoping" -- and which earns nothing, with no feedback anywhere that it will not count. **The fix, in one place.** `trainingTypeCreditsFacility()` states the server's applicability rule once; `bucketHoursInWindow` filters through it and the picker is built from it, so the figure and the way to change it cannot drift apart again. The audience exclusion became type-level too, matching `current_training_audience_status()`'s own ordering (`audience_decision_at desc nulls last, created_at desc, id desc`) instead of skipping only the record that carries the decision: a renewal INSERTS a fresh row rather than editing the old one (20260906240000), so more than one record per (employee, training type) is the normal shape, and an employer who marks a requirement not-applicable after hours were logged against it is exactly where the two readings parted. It is scoped the way the server scopes it -- `not tt.audience_verification_required or current_training_audience_status(...) not in (...)` -- so a type that never asks for a decision is still credited whatever its records say, rather than trading the old divergence for a new one. Thirty-one vitest cases; the three that carry the fix were each verified non-vacuous by reverting the guard they cover (20 hours where 4 is right, a bucket that reappears, and a bucket that wrongly vanishes). The picker now also says so when nothing applies, instead of rendering an empty dropdown. And the card holds the anniversary figure back until every input it needs is in -- which types are creditable depends on the FACILITY query, so computing it from what has arrived so far does not give a partial answer, it gives a different wrong one in the position the card weights most; until then the row shows the server's own calendar-year figure and the server's own status, which are always safe to show. **Deliberately NOT mirrored, stated rather than left implied:** `applicable_types`' `distinct on (employee, bucket)`. It narrows nothing further unless two types in one bucket both survive the three exclusions above, which in the seeded catalog means only the two Chapter 6400 group-home types and only for an employer who has confirmed both the direct-service-worker and the other-staff audience for the same person -- a contradiction in the data, not a case to model. The denominator beside this numerator comes from the server's own bucket row either way, so a second copy of that tie-break would be a thing to drift rather than a gap closed. *Checked and found correct, so recorded rather than changed:* the Chapter 6400 24-hour/12-hour split looked at first like a `distinct on` that always picks 24 for every group-home employee; `annual_training_audience_applicability.test.sql` shows it is the audience mechanism that selects between them, and asserts the 12-hour outcome (`required 12.00, completed 6.00`) for a confirmed other-staff employee. The proposed migration was dropped -- it would have let a `not_applicable` record's hours leak into that 12-hour bucket, which is the leak that test exists to prevent. |
| J100 | `db:reset:demo` stopped marking the demo tenant a demo tenant, and reported success | S | done | **P2, local demo and the documented developer flow. Found in a browser, on the production build, against the live stack.** Signing in as the seeded `admin@sunrisehealthcare.com` / `demo123` lands on the MFA enrollment gate -- "Multi-factor verification required" -- and no route but `/account/security` is reachable. AGENTS.md documents the opposite in as many words: demo orgs "stay exempt so `admin@*` / `manager@*` still log in with just `demo123`". **Why.** `supabase/seed.sql` sets the exemption with `update public.organizations set is_demo = true, demo_seed_version = 1`. `protect_organization_subscription_fields` (20260704050042, extended by 20260906060000 / J76 to cover the demo posture) copies `is_demo`, `demo_seed_version`, `demo_reset_at` and `trial_ends_at` back from OLD for any writer that is neither `is_platform_admin()` nor running with `app.privileged_write` set -- and `supabase db reset` runs this file as `postgres` with no JWT, so it is neither. The trigger reverts the row and Postgres still reports `UPDATE 1`. Verified directly on the stack: the update inside a transaction leaves `is_demo` false and `demo_seed_version` null, and the same update after `set_config('app.privileged_write','on',true)` leaves them `t` and `1`. **The second cost is larger than the gate.** The last statement in the file is `select app_private.seed_demo_organization(id) ... where slug = 'sunrise-healthcare' and is_demo`. That is a WHERE clause, so a false flag produces zero rows, no error, and no output -- and that function is what seeds the demo tenant's learners and trainers, training types and classes, residents, units, buildings, rooms, shifts, schedules, inspection items, support plans and assessment forms on top of the handful of rows this file inserts directly. Measured across the fix on the same stack: `db:reset:demo` produced 3 employees, 0 residents and 0 training classes for the demo tenant before, and 7 employees, 3 residents and 1 class after. Every local demo since J76 came up missing that content while the reset reported success. **Not a production defect, and worth saying why:** the trigger is `BEFORE UPDATE` only, and `provision-demo-tenant` sets `is_demo: true` on INSERT, so self-serve demo tenants in production are flagged correctly; CI never saw it either, because the browser job builds its own tenants with the service role rather than running `db:reset:demo`. **Fixed with the escape hatch the trigger itself offers**, used the way the definer RPCs use it and turned off again immediately so the rest of the file writes as an ordinary caller does. A `do $$ ... raise exception $$` guard now closes the file: the flag is asserted after the demo seeding runs, so any future trigger, grant or policy that takes the write away again stops the reset with a message instead of handing back a half-seeded tenant. The guard was run against the broken state first and does fail there. Verified after the fix on a clean reset: `is_demo` is `t` with `demo_seed_version` 1, `get_my_mfa_policy()` returns `required: false` for the demo org_admin, and that account signs in through the production build and lands on `/app/today` with the app shell rendered. |
| J101 | "Start new draft" could open a blank revision of a care plan that had content, and reported it as if that had been asked for | S | done | **P1, resident care plan. Found by the browser, on a run that failed where the run before it had passed on the same code.** The resident-lifecycle journey's step 7 asserts that a revision carries the plan in force forward; it came back `draftInherited: false`, and the rows say why: version 2 was written with 0 needs and 0 services against a version 1 that has 3 and 3. The previous run of the same commit produced 3 and 3 on both. **The race.** `create_support_plan_draft(p_resident_id, p_assessment_form_id, p_prior_plan_id)` copies the prior plan when it is given one -- `coalesce(v_prior.needs,'[]'::jsonb)` and the same for goals, services and interventions -- and writes an empty version when it is not. `ResidentSupportPlanSection` passed `effectivePlan?.id`, and `effectivePlan` is `(plansQuery.data ?? []).find(state === "active")`: `undefined` when there is no active plan AND `undefined` while the query is still in flight. Those two must not be the same value, because they have opposite and unrecoverable outcomes. The button that reads it sits in the `CardHeader`, outside the `plansQuery.isLoading` guard that wraps the `CardContent`, so it is clickable for the whole of that window -- and the toast afterwards says "Blank draft created", which is what the code would say if the user had asked for a blank one. **What that costs.** The RASP/ASP is the regulated care plan, and the new version becomes the record: every need, goal, service and intervention in the plan in force is absent from it, and each service that would have generated staff tasks generates none. A facility would have to notice the emptiness and retype the plan; nothing else in the flow objects, because an empty draft is a legitimate state for a resident who has never had a plan. **The fix.** `supportPlanRevisionStart(plansLoaded, plans)` in `lib/supportPlanLifecycle.ts` now holds the rule: no answer, no revision. It keys on `isSuccess` rather than `!isLoading`, because a failed query is not an answer either and a revision built on a guess about the plan in force is worse than one the user retries. The button is disabled until it can answer, and the handler refuses and says why if it is called anyway. Four vitest cases, verified non-vacuous by dropping the `plansLoaded` term (the "refuses until the query has answered" case fails). Then verified where it failed: the same journey, on a clean reset of the same stack, wrote a version 2 carrying 3 needs and 3 services. **Not fixed, and named rather than implied:** the same shape -- a header action reading a body query's data through `?? []` -- is a pattern rather than one site, and auditing every page for it is a larger change than this pass should make unasked. This one was fixed because a journey caught it writing a wrong record. |
| J102 | Editing a resident's contacts before the record loaded deactivated every contact the form does not rebuild | S | done | **P1, resident records. The pattern J101 named and left alone, now that the ask is to find and fix all of it.** J101's row ends by saying the shape it fixed -- a header action reading a body query's data through `?? []` -- is a pattern rather than one site, and that auditing every page for it was more than that pass should do unasked. It was then asked. A script swept every `.tsx` under `src/` for a mutation argument built from a query result through `?? []` or an optional chain, which is what makes "absent" and "still loading" the same value; of the candidates it returned, two were writes that destroy data on the wrong guess, and both are this row. **What the server does with that argument.** `save_resident_administrative_master(p_resident_id, p_profile, p_contacts)` does not merge. Its first act on the contact table is `update public.resident_contacts set active = false, is_primary = false where resident_id = v_resident.id and active`, and it then re-activates only the rows present in `p_contacts`. So that argument is the resident's COMPLETE contact set across all twelve stored types -- emergency_contact, designated_person, guardian, power_of_attorney, primary_care_provider, dentist, pharmacy, case_manager, hospice_agency, home_health_agency, insurer, other -- and anything missing from it is deactivated. **The two sites.** OverviewTab's "Contacts & Supports (Part I)" dialog built it from `administrativeMaster?.contacts ?? []`, rebuilding only the four types it synchronizes; on an unanswered or failed master query the other eight -- emergency contact, guardian, power of attorney, pharmacy, hospice and home-health agencies, insurer -- were sent as absent and deactivated without a word. On a personal care home resident that is the information the facility reaches for in an emergency and is required to keep on file. `ResidentAdministrativeMaster`'s own editor seeded its draft the same way, so opening it on an unloaded record and saving cleared every contact at once. **It is not only a race.** A FAILED master query leaves the data undefined for as long as the page is open, so every save from that state wipes those contacts deterministically, not occasionally. Both components already drew the distinction for DISPLAY -- OverviewTab passes `isError || isLoading` down as `dataUnavailable`, and the master panel renders "Contacts unavailable." from it -- so the rule was understood; it just never reached the write. **The fix.** `officialContactEditStart(masterLoaded, contacts)` in `lib/residentOfficialContacts.ts` refuses until the query has answered, keying on `isSuccess` rather than `!isLoading` for J101's reason: an error is not an answer either. Both editors open from it and both buttons wait for it. It returns a COPY, and OverviewTab snapshots that copy in a ref at open time and diffs the save against it -- the way that component already snapshots informal-support ids -- so a background refetch while the dialog is open cannot change what the save is about to replace. Four vitest cases, each verified non-vacuous by reverting the term it covers. **What this is NOT covered by, said rather than left to be assumed:** no browser journey touches either contacts editor -- `resident-lifecycle.spec.ts` never opens "Contacts & Supports" and no spec references `save_resident_administrative_master` -- so the 57 passing journeys on this tree are evidence for the care-plan fix (J101) and not for this one. The evidence here is the RPC's own text, re-read against the current definition (`20260713183435`, unchanged by anything later), plus the unit cases. `resident_administrative_master.test.sql` asserts the four-contact set is "retained" across a save, which is the server-side half; the client-side half -- an editor that opens on a set it has not actually read -- is what no test reached, and is why this was found by sweeping the source rather than by a failure. |
| J103 | A client rule that claimed to mirror the server refused terms the server accepts, and a taxonomy test that read a stale subset of its own migrations | S | done | **P2, platform billing and work-item queue. Found by auditing every client-side restatement of a server rule, not by a failure.** Nine helpers in `src/lib` document themselves as mirroring a specific SQL condition. Each was read against the function or constraint it names. Seven are exact -- including `canSelfEnrollInCourse`, whose `completion_recorded_at ?? completed_at` fallback looked like an invention until the table's `check ((status = 'completed') = (completion_recorded_at is not null))` showed it is unreachable for the rows that function considers. Two had drifted. **The entitlement term.** `entitlementTermIssues` said it mirrored "the server's own refusal: a current term that already starts at or after the new one" and implemented `from < now - 86_400_000`, a ban on backdating by more than a day. The server's rule is neither about now nor about the package: `set_package_entitlement` refuses only when an open term FOR THE SAME FEATURE has `effective_from >= p_effective_from`, and otherwise closes that term at the new start and inserts. So the client both over-blocked and under-blocked, and the over-blocking is the one that costs: recording a contract signed three weeks ago -- the ordinary reason a term is entered late -- was impossible through the UI, with the button disabled and no round trip to argue with, even for a feature with no open term at all. The card already loads `effective_from` per term, so the real rule was implementable and now is; the open terms are computed before the validator that needs them. When that query has not answered the collision test is skipped rather than guessed: unlike J101 and J102, a wrong guess here costs a round trip and the server's own message, while blocking costs the operation. Six new cases; the collision, the per-feature scoping and the unknown-terms branch were each verified non-vacuous by reverting them separately. **The taxonomy.** `workItemSources.test.ts` says it exists so that client drift from `work_item_source_types` is caught in CI rather than found on somebody's queue. It read two hard-coded seed migrations and compared only the keys, and it had already gone stale: `20260729215900` re-upserts `inspection_war_room` with a different label and description, and `20260906020000` retires `exclusion_match` -- neither file was being read. The client was carrying the superseded label, confirmed against the live table (39 rows, keys identical, one label differing). The test now replays every migration that writes the table, in filename order, honouring `on conflict do update` versus `do nothing`, and compares label, category, sort order and active flag as well as the key; a parser guard fails the test if it stops matching rows rather than silently comparing two empty sets. Both directions of drift were verified non-vacuous by reintroducing them. *Deliberately still unreplayed, and said rather than implied:* the `insert ... select distinct` adoption statements read `work_item_templates` / `work_items` at migration time and cannot be resolved from a file. They are `on conflict do nothing`, so they can only ADD a key; `work_item_source_taxonomy.test.sql` checks that direction against a real database, and `app_private.classify_work_item_source()` registers an unknown type on first use with `initcap(replace(key, '_', ' '))` -- which is exactly what `workItemSourceLabel` already renders for a key the build has not heard of. |
| J104 | Every permanent read failure in the app was retried three times before the page said so | S | done | **P2, whole application. A rule the codebase had already worked out three times, one query at a time, and never stated once.** react-query's default is `retry: 3` with exponential backoff, and it applies that to every rejection without asking what the rejection was. For a dropped connection that is right. For a refusal the database will repeat verbatim it is not: the same request goes out four times and the user watches a spinner for roughly seven seconds -- 1s + 2s + 4s of backoff -- before the page finally shows what the first response already said. **The rule was already known here.** `authProfileErrors.ts` exists to draw exactly this line, in its own words: PGRST116 "is a definitive 'this account has no readable profile' signal" while "everything else (Failed to fetch, timeouts, 5xx) is retryable" -- and `auth.tsx` acts on it with `retry: (n, e) => !isDefinitiveProfileAbsence(e) && n < 2`. `useGetEmployeeByProfileId` moved to `maybeSingle()` partly because "react-query's default retry: 3 would retry that 'error' with backoff for several seconds before finally giving up". Ten more queries carry a bare `retry: false` or `retry: 1`. Twelve call sites had each rediscovered the same fact; every query not yet visited still waited out the backoff -- including `MaintenanceScan`, whose "Maintenance QR code not found. The label may have been retired or may be outside your facility access." card is the correct answer to a PGRST116 and appeared seven seconds after the server gave it, on a page reached by a caregiver holding a phone at a scanned sticker. **The fix.** `lib/queryRetry.ts` classifies a failure as permanent when it cannot possibly succeed on a retry of the identical request, and `queryClient`'s query defaults use it: a missing row (PGRST116), an RLS refusal (42501 or the sentence itself, matched through the existing `isRlsViolation`), a request PostgREST could not parse or resolve (PGRST100/102/103/106, PGRST202/203/204, 42883, 42P01, 22P02), and an RPC's own raised rule (22023, 23514, 23503, 23505, P0002). Anything else keeps all three attempts, so a wifi blip still self-heals; and an option set on an individual query still wins over the default, so the twelve existing overrides are untouched. **Deliberately excluded, and the omission is the point:** PGRST301 and a bare 401. An expired JWT is the one authorization failure a retry can fix, because supabase-js refreshes the token underneath it -- classifying it as permanent would surface a signed-in user's ordinary hourly token refresh as an error. Nine vitest cases, verified non-vacuous both ways: disabling the code table fails four, and adding PGRST301 to it fails the case that exists to keep it out. |
| J105 | PostgREST's 1,000-row cap, measured rather than assumed, and what it does and does not reach | S | open | **P3, investigated and deliberately not changed -- recorded because the number was not written down anywhere.** `PGRST_DB_MAX_ROWS=1000` on the running stack (read off the `supabase_rest` container, not inferred from the CLI default), and `supabase/config.toml` sets no `[api] max_rows`, so the same cap applies in production. A read that returns more than a thousand rows is truncated with no error and no marker -- which is a visible bug in a list and an invisible one in a number. Every uncapped read of a table whose row count grows per employee, per record or per day was checked against its callers. **Nothing computes a compliance figure off a truncatable read:** `useListTrainingHourBuckets` is only ever called with an `employeeId` (three buckets per year for one person), `useListCourseCompletionCredits` and `useListQuizAttempts` are scoped per employee or per assignment, and `useAuditLogs` -- the one list where a dropped row would matter most -- already pages with `.range()` and orders on `created_at, id` so the sort is a total order across page boundaries. **What remains is a capacity ceiling, not a defect:** `useListEmployees` fetches the whole filtered roster for pickers and rollups, so a tenant past a thousand employees would see a short list. The Employees page itself already has a separate server-paginated hook; converting every other consumer is a larger change than this pass should make unasked, and it is the kind of thing to do against a known target tenant size rather than a guess. Left **open** rather than closed, because the ceiling is real and the right time to lift it is when a tenant approaches it; written down so that person has the number and the list of what was already checked rather than starting the sweep again. |
| J106 | An emergency-event page read every profile on the platform before it knew whose event it was, plus a clean result on the authorization sweep | S | done | **P3 fix, P1 question. Two sweeps whose main product is a negative, recorded because the negative is what the next pass needs to not repeat.** **The authorization sweep, and what it found: nothing.** For all 475 RPCs the client calls, the role guard was read out of each function's latest server definition and compared with how its call site is gated. Thirty-six refuse anyone but a platform admin outright (`if not public.is_platform_admin() then raise`, the guard as the sole condition -- a first pass that also matched `if not is_platform_admin() and v_org_id <> current_org_id()` reported 43, and those are org-scoping rules, not platform gates; `revoke_user_invitation` matched the same way because its `then` opens a nested role check rather than a refusal). Every one of the thirty-six is reached only through a route the router gates on `PLATFORM_ADMIN`: all 40 `/admin` routes carry it, and the single exception is `/admin/work-orders/:id`, a bare `<Redirect>` to a protected route. `publish_course_version` -- the one that would matter most if it were wrong, since a trainer who cannot publish their own course is a dead feature -- is platform-admin only on the server and `canManage = user?.role === "platform_admin"` on the page. **No UI action is offered to a role the database will always refuse.** **The one fix.** Forty-six queries apply their scoping filter only `if` truthy, which for a list page is the intended "absent means all" and for a caller that means to scope is a fetch with no boundary at all. `useListEmployees` already documents this shape and carries an `enabled` option for it, naming the cost: "that wasted (and, for platform_admin, cross-org) first fetch". Every call site passing an optional value into a scoping filter was checked; all but one pass `user?.organizationId`, which is settled before a `ProtectedRoute` renders its component. The straggler was `EmergencyEventDetail`: `useListProfiles({ organizationId: event?.organization_id })`, where `event` comes from another query, so the first render fetched with no organization filter at all. That matters on this page specifically because `platform_admin` is in `EMERGENCY_ROLES` and `profiles_select` is `is_platform_admin() or id = auth.uid() or organization_id = current_org_id()` (read off the running stack, not assumed) -- so for that role the unscoped read is every profile on the platform, ordered by last name and silently stopped at PostgREST's 1,000-row cap (J105). `useListProfiles` now takes the same `options.enabled` `useListEmployees` has, defaulting to `undefined` so no existing caller changes, and that page holds the query until the event has answered. |
| J107 | Editing a training record from the matrix credited a certificate nobody had reviewed | S | done | **P1, workforce compliance. Found by finishing the mirror audit J103 started, and measured on a live stack rather than argued.** J103 checked nine client restatements of server rules. A sweep for the claim itself -- `Mirrors`, anywhere in `src/lib` -- found **47 claims across 30 files**, so nine was the set an earlier pass had listed, not the set that exists. Working through the rest found one wrong. **The omission.** `complianceDates.computeStatus` documents itself as mirroring the recalc's status formula and reproduces five of its six branches. The missing one is the FIRST: `when r.status in ('not_applicable','pending_review') then r.status`. It is there because neither value is a position on a clock -- one says a certificate is waiting for a reviewer, the other says the requirement does not apply to this person -- so recomputing either from a date discards somebody's decision. **Why the omission was invisible.** One of the three callers NEEDS it: approving a certificate in `PendingApprovals` is precisely the act of moving a record out of `pending_review`, which the recalc would never do on its own, and that page's own comment says so. The other two do not. `TrainingMatrix` and `EmployeeDetail` both offer an ordinary edit on an existing record and wrote the recomputed status straight over the stored one. Nothing stops them: `save_training_record` merges the payload with `jsonb_populate_record` and writes `status = v_candidate.status` with no guard, and `authenticated` holds UPDATE on the table. **What it costs, measured.** A `pending_review` record carrying 8 hours contributes nothing to the employee's annual bucket, because `legacy_earned` joins `r.status not in ('pending_review','not_applicable')`. Write `compliant` over that status -- exactly the payload the matrix's save produced -- and re-run `recalculate_compliance_core` on the same stack, and the bucket reads `required 12.00, completed 8.00` while the record still carries `approval_status = 'pending'` and a null `verified_at`. A certificate nobody has looked at is already counting toward a regulated annual requirement, and a manager correcting a typo'd date is all it takes. **Bounded, and the bound is worth stating.** The row stays in the approval queue, which filters on `approval_status` rather than on `status`, so it is not hidden from the reviewer -- and a later REJECT does clean it up, writing `status: 'missing'` and clearing the dates, with a comment saying it must not "leave behind a completion/due date that could read as partial compliance elsewhere (e.g. TrainingMatrix)". So the exposure is the window until somebody reviews it, which for a queue is its normal state, and the cross-screen interaction was already half-understood on the other side of it. **The fix.** `computeStatus` takes the record's current status and reproduces the server's first branch; the two editing callers pass it and the approving caller deliberately does not, with a comment at each site saying which it is. `TrainingMatrix` passes `entry.status` only when `entry.trainingRecordId` is set: `get_training_matrix_page` returns `coalesce(r.status, <derived from facility type>)`, so on a cell with no record that value is a `not_applicable` the server invented for display, and freezing a NEW record in it would trade this defect for a worse one. `EmployeeDetail` resolves its existing record before computing the status rather than after. Five vitest cases, verified non-vacuous in both directions: removing the branch fails three, and widening it to preserve any status fails the one that exists to keep `missing`/`expired` recomputable. *Also corrected while there:* the comment cited `recalculate_all_compliance` in `20260704053624`, superseded by `recalculate_compliance_core` in `20260715210000`; the CASE is unchanged across that move and is now quoted in full beside the code that mirrors it. |
| J108 | Policy write controls can still be offered to a role whose identity assurance has lapsed | S | open | **P3, Policies & Procedures. Found by finishing the mirror audit (J107); recorded with the measurement rather than fixed, because the fix is a feature and not a correction.** `policyPermissions.ts` exists because "every write control on Policies & Procedures rendered for every role that can *read* the page" -- J74 -- and its own comment says "a hidden button is not a boundary; RLS is". Its role list is **exact**: `policy_documents_write`, `policy_document_versions_update` and `policy_attestation_campaigns_write` all read `is_platform_admin() or (organization_id = current_org_id() and current_role() in ('org_admin','facility_manager'))`, matching `POLICY_DOCUMENT_WRITE_ROLES` exactly (read off the live catalog, not the migration). **What it does not mirror.** Each of those policies is `identity_assurance_is_current('policy_document_admin') AND (...)`, a conjunct added after that comment was written. `identity_operation_requires_aal2` puts `policy_document_admin` in its baseline, so for a non-demo organization with no `identity_security_policies` row it applies to `org_admin` and `facility_manager`; and `identity_assurance_is_current` fails not only on `aal <> 'aal2'` but on a session older than `max_privileged_session_minutes` (default 480). So a user with the right role and a genuinely verified factor can still be refused, and the buttons will be enabled when they are. **Why it is narrow, said rather than assumed:** the app's MFA gate already stops a user reaching `/app` at all when the org requires AAL2, so the `aal1` half is unreachable from the UI; and `IdleSessionLock` re-verifies AAL2 on every unlock, which for any org whose idle timeout is shorter than its privileged-session ceiling keeps assurance fresh indefinitely. The reachable case is an organization that sets `max_privileged_session_minutes` BELOW its idle timeout, where a continuously-active admin crosses the ceiling without ever idling -- a configuration the product allows and nothing warns about. **Why not fixed here.** The client has no notion of identity assurance at all: `SessionSecurityGates` owns the only step-up flow and it is bound to the idle-lock unlock, not to "this operation needs current assurance". Doing this properly means exposing assurance state to the client and offering step-up at the control -- new auth plumbing, not a copy fix, and larger than this pass should take unasked. The failure mode is also a 42501 toast, not a wrong record: unlike J102 and J107 nothing is written. Left **open** with the mechanism written down. *Checked and found to need nothing:* `policy_attestation_campaigns_delete` narrows further to `org_admin` alone, which would be a mismatch for `facility_manager` -- except no campaign-delete control exists in the client at all, so there is nothing to gate. |
| J109 | The mirror audit itself: 47 claims, 30 files, and what the other 44 turned out to be | S | done | **P3, recorded so the next pass starts from the list rather than rebuilding it.** J103 checked nine client restatements of server rules and this register said so; a sweep for the claim itself -- the word `Mirrors`, anywhere in `src/lib` -- found **47 across 30 files**. Nine was a previous pass's list, not the population, and the difference is how J107 was found. **The three that were wrong** are J99 (`annualTrainingHours`, one of three exclusions mirrored), J103 (`packageEntitlementTerm`, the wrong rule entirely; and `workItemSources.test.ts`, guarding against a stale subset of its own migrations) and J107 (`complianceDates`, five of six branches). **The ones read against their SQL and found exact**, each against the live catalog or the current function body rather than the migration the comment happens to name: `supportPlanLifecycle`'s transition table (all fourteen pairs, plus its reason-required rule and its claim that `active` is deliberately absent as a target), `serviceDeliveryContract` (seven task kinds, seven completion responses, the qualification-key regex, the non-empty rule), `certificationAttempt` (all three outstanding-item branches including the `not_applicable` escape), `governedContentRevision` (four separate claims), `courseAvailability` (the thirty-day renewal window and the open-status set), `enterpriseRoleTemplates` (two check constraints and an RPC guard), `enterpriseAccessGrants` (a reason rule and `p_effective_to <= v_grant.effective_from`, quoted verbatim), `hrisImportRuns`, `admissionPipeline` (fourteen stage keys in order), `documentAnalyzer` (a four-field approval rule and `MAX_PACKET_JOBS`), `personalFundsStatement` (`greatest(now() + interval '1 day', coalesce(v_latest_at, now()))`, both branches), and both offline draft `outcome` unions -- which correctly narrow to the per-`draft_kind` subset the check constraint allows rather than the union across kinds. `policyPermissions` is exact on roles and silent on a conjunct (J108). **One divergence is deliberate and already documented:** `dataImportCenter` requires `facility_name` for employees where the processor accepts `facility_name` OR `facility_id`, because `facility_id` is a raw UUID the mapping step never offers -- so the client cannot over-block a column it does not expose. **The remaining claims are not server mirrors at all** and were classified rather than chased: `appDomains` mirrors `App.tsx`, `csv` mirrors RFC 4180, `comprehensiveReport` and `assessmentTemplates` mirror other client modules, `residentCareHeader` mirrors a constant, and `residentAssessmentFormSchema` mirrors a DHS paper form. Client-to-client drift is a different and much cheaper failure than client-to-server drift, and worth separating so a future audit spends its time where the consequences are. |
| J110 | The audience ordering depended on one serialisation of `timestamptz`, and the obvious repair would have lost microseconds | S | done | **P3, from the first automated review round on PR #501.** Copilot flagged `isMoreRecentAudienceRecord` for ordering `timestamptz` fields by string comparison, warning that "any format variation ... can change the ordering and pick the wrong latest record, which then drives the type-level audience exclusion and hour crediting", and proposed comparing parsed timestamps. **Verified before acting, both halves.** The premise is right and the proposed fix is a regression, and each was measured rather than argued. *Right:* the raw comparison IS chronological for the one shape PostgREST emits -- `json_build_object` on the live stack returns a fixed date and time, a fractional part with trailing zeros trimmed (`.500000` comes back `.5`), then a constant `+00:00`, and sorting `[22+00:00, 22.05+00:00, 22.123456+00:00, 22.5+00:00]` lexicographically reproduces chronological order exactly, because `+` sorts below every digit and fractions compare as left-aligned decimals. It is right for that shape and silently wrong for any other, which is a bad property for a comparison to have: `new Date().toISOString()` ends in `Z`, `Z` sorts ABOVE `+`, and one such value mixed into a page of `+00:00` ones reverses the pair. These two columns are server-written today -- a trigger sets `audience_decision_at`, a default sets `created_at` -- so nothing produces that mix now; the defect is that nothing would notice if something started to, and this codebase does write `toISOString()` into other timestamptz columns. *A regression:* `Date.parse` maps `...22.123400Z` and `...22.123500Z` to the SAME number, because Postgres stores microseconds and JavaScript dates hold milliseconds. Two records a tenth of a millisecond apart, which one statement can produce, become a tie that falls through to the `id` tie-break -- an ordering the raw strings got right. **The fix does both.** `comparableInstant()` normalises to a canonical UTC string with a six-digit fraction, shifting a non-UTC offset to UTC by instant rather than wall clock, and returns an unparseable value unchanged so it still compares against itself deterministically instead of every odd value collapsing into one bucket. Comparison stays textual, so the microseconds survive. Five vitest cases, verified non-vacuous in both directions the finding created: reverting to raw string comparison fails the two mixed-format cases, and applying the suggested `Date.parse` fails the microsecond case. |
| J111 | J107's fix would have frozen every audience shell, and the retry set would have made a deploy's schema-cache lag a hard error | S | done | **P1 and P2, from the first Codex review round on PR #501. Both findings were real and both were mine.** **The P1: `pending_review` means two things and J107 only knew one.** J107 stopped `TrainingMatrix` and `EmployeeDetail` recomputing over a `pending_review` status, on the reading that it always means "a certificate is waiting for a reviewer". It does not. For a training type with `audience_verification_required`, `training_types.audience_verification_required`'s own column comment (20260715210000) says the auto-instantiated requirement "remains pending_review and is excluded from annual-hour rollups until an employer confirms this exact audience BY CHANGING THE RECORD TO AN ACTIVE REQUIREMENT STATUS" -- so on those cells, recording training IS the confirmation, and preserving the status is exactly wrong. Measured on the seeded stack: **47 rows** carry `status='pending_review'` with `approval_status` NULL and `audience_verification_required`, against zero certificate-review rows; under J107 as first written, a manager recording training on any of them would have saved the completion and the hours while `recalculate_compliance_core` went on excluding them. The J107 probe had actually SHOWN this -- the bucket was absent before the status flip and appeared after -- and it was noted as odd and passed over rather than chased. **`approval_status` is the only column that separates the two**, because the reviewer's queue filters on it and a shell has never been through that queue. The matrix RPC did not return it, so the client could not tell: `20260907211500` adds `approvalStatus` to the cell, which is additive and type-neutral (the cell is built with `jsonb_build_object` into a `jsonb` return, so no generated type moves). `computeStatus` now preserves only when `status = 'pending_review' AND approval_status = 'pending'`; `not_applicable` is the same audience decision from the other side -- `audience_decision_at` is stamped "when the audience decision category changes among pending_review, not_applicable, and applicable" -- so it graduates too. Re-verified on the stack in both directions: an audience shell confirmed by recording training now yields `required 12.00, completed 8.00`, and the certificate case from J107 is still held. Six cases, non-vacuous both ways (the too-broad rule fails the two audience cases; no preservation fails the certificate case). **The P2: a schema-cache miss is not permanent.** J104 put `PGRST202`/`PGRST203`/`PGRST204` in the no-retry set as "the function or column is not in the schema cache under the name this build asked for; a deploy fixes that, a retry does not". That is backwards for the case that actually produces them: PostgREST answers from a cached schema, and during and just after `supabase db push` it serves these for an RPC or column the migration has ALREADY added, until the reload lands -- so the identical request succeeds on the next attempt, which is what the backoff exists for. Classifying them permanent would have turned a few seconds of deploy-time cache lag into a hard error on every affected page, during a deploy. Removed; the parse errors (`PGRST100`/`102`/`103`/`106`), which are about the request text and cannot be fixed by resending it, stay. |
| J112 | Manager "Called Off" on a published schedule skipped the only open-shift producer | S | done | **P1, Workforce / scheduling.** J73's producer is `record_shift_call_off`. J70 left the published-schedule status dropdown as the post-publication control, and that path PATCHed `shift_assignments.status` directly. No trigger filled the queue. Week-one pattern: manager marks Called Off, hole in coverage, empty claim cards, no unfilled-shift work item. *Fixed:* `ScheduleDetail` opens a category/reason dialog and calls the RPC; `20260908010000` refuses a direct `called_off` write on a published schedule (INVOKER, so `write_is_through_a_trusted_path` can see `authenticated`). Drafts stay a planning mark. |
| J113 | Billing checkout demanded a raw AAL2 JWT after J14 exempted demo orgs | S | done | **P1, Billing / identity.** `create-billing-session` called `getAuthenticatorAssuranceLevel()` and refused `aal1` before `identity_assurance_is_current('billing_admin')`, which honours `is_demo` the way J14 taught the SQL gates. Demo checkout and any org whose policy does not require MFA hit "Set up MFA". J74 fixed the same bug only for `verify-identity-domain`. *Fixed:* the handler uses `requireFreshAal2(..., "billing_admin")` only. |
| J114 | Emergency "Close after review" and "Mark stabilized" ignored the rules the card already stated | S | done | **P1, Emergency operations.** The card says closure needs an approved after-action and stabilization requires everyone accounted for. The buttons enabled on any non-empty reason. The RPCs refuse: reason ≥ 5, no `expected`/`unaccounted` residents or staff before stabilize, approved after-action before close. After-action save and corrective-work create had the same shape (summary ≥ 10; submitted/approved need findings; due must be in the future). *Fixed:* `emergencyCommand.ts` mirrors those rules; the page disables the buttons. |
| J115 | Work-item Closed was offered without the evidence and dependency gates Approve already used | S | done | **P2, Work queue.** `allowedTransitions` hid `closed` only when `approval_required`. Templates that do not require approval still refuse close when required evidence or blocking dependencies are missing. *Fixed:* `canDirectCloseWorkItem` applies the same gates as Approve. |
| J116 | Complaint "Approve closure" ignored its own readiness checklist | S | done | **P2, Complaints.** The checklist is rendered (ack, investigator, notes, findings, response, open actions, monitoring) and the button only required a 5-character reason. *Fixed:* `complaintClosureReady` disables Approve closure until every database-enforced item is known and satisfied. |
| J117 | `CorrectiveActionForm` still completed actions without `verification_notes` | S | done | **P2, Incidents / violations / inspections. J13 residual.** J13 added `verify_corrective_action` and the Complete→Verify dialogs. The shared edit form still offered `completed` and wrote `{status, completed_date}` with no notes, recreating the incomplete state approval refuses. *Fixed:* `completed` is no longer an editable status; completion stays on `VerifyCorrectiveActionDialog`. |
| J118 | Roster and employee edit still offered status, hire date and facility, which the lifecycle trigger refuses | S | done | **P2, Workforce. I26 residual.** `protect_employee_lifecycle_fields` refuses those columns unless `app.lifecycle_transition` is on. The edit dialogs still sent them, so a manager changing a name could not save if they also touched status. *Fixed:* those fields are read-only on edit, with a link into the lifecycle wizard; the update payload no longer includes them. Create still collects them. |
| J119 | Twilio status webhooks signed `req.url` instead of the public callback URL | S | done | **P2, Notifications. I13 residual.** Dispatch advertises `TWILIO_NOTIFICATION_STATUS_CALLBACK_URL`. The webhook verified the signature against `req.url`, which in a proxied invoke is a different host, so every legitimate status callback 403s and deliveries stay `unknown`. *Fixed:* `resolveTwilioWebhookUrl` rebuilds the signed URL from the configured public base plus the incoming query. The same change called `readTextBody` without importing it; `deno check` failed until the `requestBody` import landed. Codex on #504 found the override was then applied to STOP/START as well (J132). |
| J120 | Complaint "Approve closure" still ignored an open appeal | S | done | **P2, Complaints. J116 residual.** `update_complaint_case` refuses close when `appeal_requested_at` is set and `appeal_outcome` is under 5 characters. J116's checklist never asked. *Fixed:* `complaintAppealComplete` is a closure item. |
| J121 | Leave, terminate and transfer called off published shifts without the open-shift producer | S | done | **P1, Workforce / scheduling. J112 residual.** `disposition_workforce_lifecycle_dependents` UPDATEs future `scheduled`/`confirmed` rows to `called_off` on a trusted path, so J112's PATCH guard does not fire and nothing posted `open_shift_opportunities` or the unfilled-shift work item. Ending employment with a live roster emptied coverage the same way the manager dropdown did. *Fixed:* `20260908020000` posts through `app_private.post_unfilled_published_shift` after each lifecycle call-off. |
| J122 | Published "No Show" still skipped the open-shift producer | S | done | **P1, Workforce / scheduling. J112 residual.** Analytics treat `no_show` as non-covering; the grid still PATCHed it. No category is required, so unlike Called Off it stays a status mark. *Fixed:* an AFTER UPDATE trigger posts coverage when the parent schedule is published and the shift has not ended. Drafts stay a planning mark. |
| J123 | `due_soon` credentials and training records hard-blocked shifts they were still valid for | S | done | **P2, Workforce / scheduling. I26 residual.** Recalc sets `due_soon` while `expiration_date` / `due_date` is still in the future. `evaluate_schedule_eligibility` required `status = 'compliant'`, so a still-valid license blocked every gated shift for the warning window. *Fixed:* `compliant` and `due_soon` both count; the date columns remain the authority. |
| J124 | Approved time off never wrote an availability window | S | done | **P2, Workforce / scheduling. I26 residual.** `evaluate_shift_assignment_eligibility` promotes `outside_availability` to a hard block, but `decide_time_off_request` only updated the request. Auto-fill scheduled over approved PTO. *Fixed:* approval inserts an `unavailable` window for `[starts_at, ends_at)`. |
| J125 | CSV employee update still wrote the lifecycle columns J118 locked on the form | S | done | **P2, Workforce. J118 residual.** `protect_employee_lifecycle_fields` refuses a real change to status, hire_date or facility_id. A title-and-phone re-import that also carried a different status failed mid-apply. *Fixed:* `stripEmployeeLifecycleFromImportUpdate` drops those columns on update and warns when the CSV asked for a change; create still writes them. |
| J126 | Publishing a draft that already had Called Off / No Show never posted coverage | S | done | **P1, Workforce / scheduling. J112 residual.** J112/J122 left draft Called Off and No Show as planning marks. `publish_schedule` only flipped `schedules.status`. After publish, `record_shift_call_off` refuses the row (`Only a scheduled or confirmed shift can be called off`) and the no-show trigger does not re-fire. Week-one pattern: manager plans a hole, publishes, empty claim cards. *Fixed:* `20260908030000` posts each uncovered assignment through `app_private.post_unfilled_published_shift` after the roster is live. |
| J127 | New hires never received an Act 33 credential shell | S | done | **P2, Workforce / Survey Day. I26 residual.** `instantiate_missing_requirements` inserted Act 34 and TB only. Inspection Readiness and Survey Day grade background checks from rows that exist, so a missing Act 33 shell read Ready. *Fixed:* the instantiator also inserts `act33_child_abuse` and re-runs for every active employee. Act 73 still comes from `derive_fbi_requirement_from_residency`. Gitleaks `generic-api-key` flagged the pgTAP fixture that names that credential type (entropy 3.62), the same heuristic I33 hit; `.gitleaks.toml` allowlists the literal in the one test file. |
| J128 | Lifecycle Apply still demanded `enterprise_scope_admin` after J14 moved Create | S | done | **P1, Workforce. J14 residual.** `create_employee_lifecycle_case` asserts `operational_admin` (not in the sensitive baseline). `apply_employee_lifecycle_transition` still called `assert_phase2_aal2` (`enterprise_scope_admin`). A non-demo manager without MFA could assemble a ready terminate/leave/transfer/rehire case and fail at Apply. *Fixed:* Apply asserts `operational_admin`. Invite / identity-admin stay on H10. |
| J129 | Notification health counted every `unknown` forever | S | done | **P2, Notifications. I13 residual.** `run_phase1_synthetic_checks` was bounded to 24 hours in `20260906250000`. `get_notification_delivery_health` still did `count(*) filter (where final_outcome = 'unknown')`, so the console contradicted the job and one historic timeout reddened it forever. *Fixed:* the RPC uses the same 24-hour window. |
| J130 | A second coverage loss on the same slot did not raise open-shift capacity | S | done | **P1, Workforce / scheduling. J122/J126 residual. Codex on #504.** `post_unfilled_published_shift` and `record_shift_call_off` treated an existing draft/open opportunity for the same schedule/date/start/unit as success. The first claim filled the only slot while another assignment stayed uncovered. Distinct end times and shift definitions that shared those fields were also collapsed. *Fixed:* `20260908040000` increments `slots` on the matching opening (match includes end_time and shift_definition_id) via `ensure_published_open_shift_opportunity`. |
| J131 | Correcting a published no-show left the opening and the unfilled-shift work item live | S | done | **P1, Workforce / scheduling. J122 residual. Codex on #504.** The trigger posted coverage on entering `no_show` and did nothing on the way back, even though the published status menu allows `scheduled`/`confirmed`/`completed`. A replacement could still claim a shift the original person was covering again. *Fixed:* leaving `called_off`/`no_show` for a covering status retracts the work item and shrinks or cancels the matching opening. A later no-show reopens the canceled work item. |
| J132 | Status-callback URL override was also applied to inbound SMS consent | S | done | **P1, Notifications. J119 residual. Codex on #504.** `twilio-notification-webhook` handles status callbacks and STOP/START. J119 unconditionally rebuilt the signed URL from `TWILIO_NOTIFICATION_STATUS_CALLBACK_URL`. If outbound status uses a public alias while Messaging is configured with the function URL, STOP/START 403s and opt-outs are never recorded. *Fixed:* kind is determined before verification; the status override applies only to status callbacks. Optional `TWILIO_NOTIFICATION_CONSENT_CALLBACK_URL` for an aliased inbound webhook. |
| J133 | Call-off did not refresh schedule workload or acuity | S | done | **P2, Workforce / scheduling. J112 residual. Codex on #504.** `useRecordShiftCallOff` invalidated assignments and the queue. `ScheduleDetail` also renders `schedule-service-workload` and `schedule-acuity-roster`, which count only scheduled/confirmed assignments, so the called-off employee stayed in coverage until remount. *Fixed:* those query keys invalidate on call-off success, and no-show updates also invalidate acuity. |
| J134 | create-user ignored the chosen role and organization | S | done | **P1, Identity.** GoTrue writes `app_metadata` after the `auth.users` INSERT that fires `handle_new_user`, so the trigger always inserted `employee` / `organization_id=null`. `invite-user` already compensated with `admin_update_profile`; create-user did not. "Add User" with a temporary password created an unscoped employee regardless of the role picked. *Fixed:* create-user calls `admin_update_profile` after `createUser` and deletes the auth user if the RPC fails. Platform-admin provisioning (create, invite, or `admin-update-user` org move) into a public demo tenant is refused the same way demo members already are -- the caller-org check never fires for `platform_admin`. The Users page create dropdown skips demo orgs. Handlers extracted with runtime tests; live local stack ran create, invite, admin-update (including `reset_mfa`), impersonate start/bind/end, and revoke. |

### Tier K — CI/CD workflow review (2026-09-07)

Findings from the review of every workflow under `.github/`, recorded in
[docs/ops/CI_CD_WORKFLOW_REVIEW_2026-09-07.md](docs/ops/CI_CD_WORKFLOW_REVIEW_2026-09-07.md), which
holds the inventory, the run-history evidence, the order and the gates; it carries no status. The
review read every workflow, composite action and invoked script in full and checked each file's
claims against the live run history (last 60 `main` pushes, last 40 deploys, every scheduled run)
and the issue tracker. The rows were written `open` because the owner asked for the plan first;
they were then implemented in the same branch, in the order section 6 sets out, and every one is
verified below by something other than reading the diff.

The through-line: this repository's automated alerts could raise a signal but never retire one,
and two of them raised signals that were not true. K1–K3 fixed what was wrong on the day, K4–K5
closed the last two paths by which a merged migration could sit undeployed with nothing saying so,
and K6–K10 closed the gaps between what the workflows claimed and what they did.

| ID | Ticket | Size | Status | Notes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| K1 | Automated issues open themselves and never close | S | done | **P1, and it is the reason 28 stale `[deploy]` issues had to be closed by hand on 2026-09-05 (I24).** All three issue-opening workflows raised alerts and none retired them; #495 and #500 sat open for deploys runs 186 and 189 had already superseded. Closed by a new `.github/actions/reconcile-issue` composite action holding both halves in one place — open-or-bump, and close-what-this-run-resolved — used by all four alerting workflows. Retiring is per-condition, not per-title: a green deploy closes every `[deploy] …` issue by prefix, because `db push` is cumulative and `functions deploy` pushes every function, so a later green deploy supersedes an earlier failure whatever SHA its title carries; a clean advisory audit closes both `[deps]` titles; a clean DHS run closes its own. **The rewrite also fixed a bug in the half that already existed:** the three copies dededuplicated against the first page of open issues only (`per_page: 30` in deploy, `50` in the two audits) and read "not on that page" as "not open" — which is how a second issue gets opened for a condition that already has one, and precisely the state the repo was in with 28 open at once. The shared action paginates, and filters out pull requests so it can never close one. **It is also the first workflow script here with committed coverage** — `scripts/check-reconcile-issue.mjs`, in `check:all`, extracts the action's own inline script out of the YAML and drives it against a mocked issues API through nine behaviours: that a pull request returned by the issues endpoint is never closed, that a prefix match never reaches an unrelated issue, that an unknown `state` fails loudly instead of no-opping, and that a missing label costs a create but not the alert. Mutation-tested three ways (drop the pull-request filter, match by substring, swallow a bad state — each caught by the right assertion). Writing it found two bugs in the harness rather than the action, including an environment restored while the script was still suspended at its first `await`, which is what proved the harness was driving the real script and not a retyped copy of it. |
| K2 | The daily advisory audit reported a registry outage as a vulnerability | S | done | **P1, false security signal.** `check-dependencies.mjs` exited 1 both for a real advisory and for an unreachable endpoint, so run 32 on 2026-09-04 — three 60-second timeouts, nothing audited — commented "Still failing" on the open advisory issue as though main shipped the vulnerability. The script now raises a distinct `AdvisoryTransportError` and exits **3**; the workflow captures that status into a step output (GitHub gives a step's exit code to bash but never to the expression language) and opens a differently-titled `[deps] Advisory audit could not run` instead, which says in its first line that main was *not* audited. It deliberately does not touch the advisory issue — an outage is evidence in neither direction — and a later clean audit closes both. **Verified end-to-end, not just self-tested:** re-pointing the endpoint at a closed port exercises the real path and exits 3 with the right message; four self-test cases pin the exit status and the error class, since 1-versus-3 is now a contract between the script and the workflow. *Deviation from the plan, deliberate:* the plan proposed a warning and a green run. A run that audited nothing and reports success is the silence this repository distrusts everywhere else, so it fails visibly and opens an alert that closes itself. **Extended after a Copilot review of PR #502 found the same shape one step further in:** narrowing only the transport case still left every OTHER failure exiting 1, and the advisory verdict was itself an uncaught `throw` — so "seventeen packages are vulnerable" and "`pnpm-lock.yaml` has no `packages:` section" were the same exit code. Reproduced: a lockfile stripped of that section exits 1 exactly like a real finding, and a pnpm major changing the lockfile shape (11 → 12 is already being advertised) would have opened the advisory issue asserting a vulnerability nobody looked for. There is now a strict contract — 0 clean, 1 verdict, 2 the script failed, 3 the registry was unreachable — with the verdict an explicit `process.exit` and handlers routing everything unhandled to 2. The workflow opens the advisory issue on status 1 and nothing else, so an exit code nobody has defined yet lands in "could not run" rather than inventing a security finding. All four statuses exercised end to end against the real code path, the verdict included, by stubbing the advisory feed. |
| K3 | The planning-register check ran in tree mode inside `check:all` on pull requests | S | done | **P1, and it turned a conforming PR red the day it was found.** `check:all` invokes the check with no arguments, which meant tree mode — walking `stamp..HEAD` commit by commit and failing any single commit that shipped register-affecting work without touching `BACKLOG.md` *in that commit*. A PR that touches a migration in one commit and re-verifies the register in the next is conforming, and the dedicated `planning-registers` job (whole-diff PR mode) passes it. That was inert only while `application` checked out shallow; `717d00e` gave it `fetch-depth: 0` for the dependency gate and switched it on. CI run 2055 (`04d8686`): `application` red on "`2c55f74` chore: record the Support Hub production migration ledger", `planning-registers` green, `ci-result` red. Fixed by `resolveBaseRef(argv, env)`, mirroring `check-migration-immutability.mjs`: an explicit `--base` still wins, `GITHUB_BASE_REF` (set by GitHub on `pull_request` and nowhere else) selects PR mode, everything else stays tree mode. Six fixtures cover the rule, including the no-flag-on-a-PR case that was wrong. **Reproduced before fixing:** a scratch worktree with the two-commit shape fails on the old code and passes on the new, while tree mode with no PR environment still fails on genuine drift — so main's gate is unchanged. |
| K4 | Out-of-band production migrations raced the deploy pipeline | S | done | **P1, process and pipeline.** `ARCHITECTURE.md` and `replit.md` documented schema changes as apply-through-MCP-first, then write the file. That ordering predates the deploy workflow and inverts it: between the apply and the merge of the file, production holds a version with no local file and `supabase db push` refuses to run at all — and because `db push` is the first production step of every deploy, the next merge fails *before* its edge functions deploy, related or not. Run 188 (`d332ddf`, PR #497) is the worked example; #499 unblocked it 34 minutes later. Three parts, all shipped: **(a)** both documents rewritten — commit the migration, let merging it apply the change, and if an out-of-band apply is unavoidable the file recording it goes in the *same* PR; **(b)** a nightly `schedule` on `deploy-migrations.yml` that runs the existing job in dry-run mode, pushing and deploying nothing but running both drift checks, so an orphan version or a pending migration is reported on its own schedule rather than discovered by the next person to merge — `DRY_RUN` is forced true for `schedule` by an explicit `||` rather than a ternary, for the reason the prune step's own comment already records about `A && B || C`; **(c)** `MIGRATION_DEPLOYMENT_AUDIT.md` gains a section 0 saying that applying by hand is now a deploy-blocking event, not merely untidy. The nightly run's failure issue is titled and worded as a drift report, not a broken deploy, because nothing was changed. |
| K5 | A red `main` was silent, and so was the deploy it blocked | S | done | **P2, the last silent path to the failure `deploy-migrations.yml` exists to close.** When CI fails on a push to `main` the deploy job's `if:` is false, both its jobs report `skipped`, nothing opens an issue, and any migration or edge function in that commit stays undeployed until someone pushes again — 8 of the last 40 deploy runs. GitHub emails the pusher; a compliance product should not rest a production-deploy gap on whether one person reads a notification. New `main-branch-alert` job in `ci.yml`, keyed on `ci-result` because that is exactly the predicate the deploy waits on (a `skipped` `ci-result` from a cancelled run is not success either, and the deploy does not fire then), reading the per-job results only to say which job was red. It opens one `[ci] main is red` issue whose body states the real consequence — the deploy did not run — and closes it on the next green push, which is also the push that deploys. It is the only job in `ci.yml` with `issues: write` and it runs no repository code. |
| K6 | The secret scan was described as history-wide and was not | S | done | **P2, security coverage, and the claim was checked before it was acted on.** Read against the pinned `gitleaks/gitleaks-action@e0c47f4f` (v3.0.0) source: `src/index.js` sets a baseRef/headRef for `push` (`commits[0].id`..`commits[last].id`) and for `pull_request`, and `src/gitleaks.js` runs `--log-opts=--no-merges --first-parent <base>^..<head>`; only `workflow_dispatch` and `schedule` get `scanInfo = { gitleaksPath }` with no refs, which is what makes gitleaks walk the whole history. So the two events that scan history were the two nothing fired. `.gitleaksignore` carrying fingerprints for historical commits is evidence such a scan has been run here — by hand, and not since. New `secret-scan-history.yml`, weekly plus dispatch, full clone, issue on a finding that closes itself when a later scan is clean and whose body says to rotate first and triage second. The per-push job keeps its full clone (it needs the commit *before* the push's first commit to exist locally) and its step is renamed to say what it actually does. |
| K7 | Deploy-job hygiene: short-circuit inputs, secret scope, install footprint | S | done | **P2, deploy.** **(a)** The path short-circuit's diff list omitted `prune-removed-edge-functions.mjs`, `removed-edge-functions.json` and both composite actions — the script that decides which functions get *deleted* from production, and the pins for the CLI and toolchain that run the whole thing. Added. **(b)** `SUPABASE_ACCESS_TOKEN` and `SUPABASE_DB_PASSWORD` were job-level `env`, so the production database password sat in the environment of `pnpm install` and of the CLI's function bundler. They are now attached to the six steps that talk to Supabase and to nothing else. **(c)** With the secrets scoped, the install still ran 690 packages' lifecycle scripts in the deploy job for one dependency: the composite action takes an `install-filter`, the deploy passes `.`, and the job installs the root package alone. **Measured, not assumed:** 3 packages instead of 690, and all four scripts the deploy runs (`check-migration-drift`, `check-edge-function-drift`, `prune-removed-edge-functions`, `stamp-edge-function-deploy`) verified working against that install — the first of them is the one that needs `libpg-query`. |
| K8 | Toolchain pins lived in nine places with nothing checking they agreed | S | done | **P2, reproducibility.** Node, pnpm, the Supabase CLI and Deno are each written down in four to six files. They agreed; they have not always — the 2026-07-20 review found the dev container on pnpm 10.28.1 while CI ran 11.13.0 — and a Supabase CLI disagreement is the expensive one, because 2.109.1's base image grants browser roles nothing on migration-created tables where newer images grant write access, so two pgTAP privilege assertions turn on which CLI built the stack (H19). New `check-toolchain-pins.mjs` in `check:all`: one canonical source per tool, every mirror must match its pattern *at least once* — a mirror matching nothing is an error, because a rename that silently drops coverage is the failure the check would otherwise develop itself — and every value must equal the canonical one. It reports 27 agreeing copies across the four tools. **Mutation-tested rather than trusted:** a drifted `.nvmrc`, the exact 2026-07-20 dev-container pnpm regression, and a pattern that stops matching are each caught with the right message. Dependabot: `docker` enabled for `/.devcontainer` (monthly, grouped) now that a bump's blast radius is checked; `npm` deliberately **not** enabled, with the reasoning written into `dependabot.yml` — security is already covered by the live advisory gate and `minimumReleaseAge`, every npm PR would boot the full matrix, and a catalog-pinned workspace needs the catalog entry moved rather than a package range. One consequence handled: `.devcontainer/*` and `AGENTS.md` joined the CI path filter, or a bump to either would change a pin in a diff that runs no job able to notice the other copies now disagree. |
| K9 | Comments and documents that no longer described the pipeline | S | done | **P3, drift — and in this repository the comments are where the reasoning lives, so a stale one costs more than elsewhere.** Corrected: "the 45-minute `database` job" and "581 migrations" in `ci.yml` and `dependabot.yml` (measured: 7 m 36 s and 681, against a 45-minute *timeout*); the `planning-registers` comment asserting `application` "checks out shallow", which stopped being true at `717d00e` and is the root of K3; the `secret-scan` step name and comment (K6); `DEPLOYMENT.md` "Limitations", which described CI as "install, typecheck, unit tests, `deno check`, and production build" — true in July, a fraction of it now — and which now also lists the five pieces of repository automation that open and close their own issues; `MIGRATION_DEPLOYMENT_AUDIT.md`, which still presented the manual `db push` as the deploy path (K4c). |
| K10 | The DHS freshness clock went red before anyone was asked | S | done | **P3.** The 45-day human-review limit is right, but the first signal that it was lapsing was the weekly job failing after it had: run 7 failed at 49 days, the stamp was refreshed at 55. `check-dhs-sources.mjs` now prints a `WARN` line inside the last seven days — the smallest lead that guarantees at least one *passing* weekly run carries the notice — for both the form-library and citation clocks, on stdout so it reaches the run summary of a green run. Both paths verified against the live sources: warning at exit 0, failure at exit 1, mutually exclusive. The issue is retitled away from "check failed" (read as "link dead" when it was usually the clock) and its body now leads with the actual `FAIL` lines lifted from the report, then explains which of the two very different repairs each kind needs. |
| K11 | Dashboard-side deployment settings and observed runtime evidence | S | ops_only | Read-only recheck on 2026-09-08 partially resolves this row; `DEPLOYMENT.md` records the evidence. **(1)** The required-check target remains `ci-result` only, but the connector receives HTTP 403 reading classic `main` branch protection. The repository ruleset list is empty; that does not establish classic protection. **(2)** Scheduled dry run [34200865149](https://github.com/kdeyarmin/PennTrain/actions/runs/34200865149) started three seconds after creation, has no recorded environment approval, and passed secret-presence, link, migration-drift and function-presence checks. The nightly signal therefore ran unattended with usable credentials in that run. The connection cannot read secrets metadata or environment administration; whether credentials are scoped to `production` rather than the repository, and the current reviewer configuration, remain unverified. **(3)** The deployment stamp in [34261253345](https://github.com/kdeyarmin/PennTrain/actions/runs/34261253345) expires exactly 90 days after run creation; the upload step has no retention override. This verifies effective 90-day retention for that latest stamp, not an immutable future setting. |
| K12 | Five findings the automated reviewers made against this pass's own fixes | S | done | **Two P1s, both in code K1–K11 had just added, and one finding that was wrong.** Recorded because four of the five are the same defect class the pass was written to close, which is worth knowing about the pass. **(1) `main-branch-alert` could block the production deploy.** `deploy-migrations.yml` gates on `workflow_run.conclusion == 'success'` — the conclusion of the whole CI run, not of `ci-result` — so a transient Issues-API 5xx in a job whose only purpose is to maintain an *informational* issue would have turned the run red and stopped a validated migration and edge-function deploy, while opening no alert at all (it only alerts when `ci-result` failed, which it had not). `continue-on-error: true`; same treatment given to `close-resolved`, since a red run against a successful deploy is a lie told to whoever reads the Actions list next. **(2) A dry run must not retire deploy alerts.** `close-resolved` originally ran for the nightly on the reasoning that it runs both drift checks. True of migrations, whose content is md5-compared; false of edge functions — `check-edge-function-drift.mjs` compares slug SETS only, and its own header admits "presence + stamp is the practical content gate", so after a deploy that failed while updating an existing function the slug is still present with the old bundle, the dry run passes, and closing would have retired the only alert saying production runs stale code. Now gated on a new `deployed` output set by the last step of the real deploy path, which makes production current by construction rather than by inference. **(3) The new secret-scan workflow had the K2 bug.** `steps.gitleaks.outcome == 'failure'` fires for a failed binary download or an invalid `.gitleaks.toml` exactly as for a finding, so it would have asserted a credential in the history when nothing was scanned. The action declares no outputs, so the discriminator is its report: it runs the binary with `--report-format=sarif --report-path=results.sarif --exit-code=2`, and no findings plus a failed step means the scan failed. Separate `[secrets] … could not run` alert. **(4) The label retry could duplicate an alert.** `reconcile-issue` retried `issues.create` on any error while claiming a label failure — so for the four unlabelled alerts the retry was byte-identical to the first request, and if that request had succeeded with only its response lost, the retry made a second issue for one condition: precisely what the action exists to prevent. Now retries only when labels were supplied *and* the rejection was a 422; anything else rethrows, because a visible red step beats a silent duplicate. Covered by two new cases that model a lost response, both of which fail against the old code. **(5) Not a defect.** The DHS failing-line extraction was reported as depending on `pipefail` that was not set; GitHub does run `shell: bash` steps with `-o pipefail`, and the fallback was verified firing. Rewritten anyway as a plain assignment, because correctness that depends on a default two layers away is not legible at the call site. |
| K13 | The dev-container watcher and the pin check that shipped with it cannot both be satisfied by a Dependabot PR | S | done | **P3, and a K12-class finding one pass later: two things #502 added in the same commit that structurally fight.** `check-toolchain-pins` treats `.devcontainer/Dockerfile` as a *mirror* for all three of its base images — the canonical pins are `.node-version` (Node), `.github/workflows/ci.yml` (Deno) and `.github/actions/setup-supabase-cli/action.yml` (the CLI) — while the `docker` ecosystem enabled in the same PR only ever edits that Dockerfile. So every monthly bump arrives red **by construction**, and #503, the first one, duly did: Node 24.15.0 → 26.8.1 and Deno 2.5.6 → 2.9.6, in the mirror alone. This is not the false-signal shape K1–K3 and K12 were about — the check names exactly which files are still on the old version, and the PR really *is* an incomplete change — but nothing anywhere said so, so the first one reads as a broken check rather than as a proposal needing completion. *Fixed:* `dependabot.yml` now records that these PRs always arrive red, that they are proposals rather than merge-ready changes, and carries an explicit worklist for finishing one -- every mirror of all three tools, named. **Corrected after Codex reviewed this PR, and the correction is the interesting half:** the first version told the reader to use `check:toolchain-pins`'s output *as* that worklist, which is false. `compareMirrors` reports a mirror only when it DISAGREES with the canonical, so a Dockerfile-only bump prints one line per tool naming the Dockerfile and the canonical it contradicts, and says nothing about the mirrors still sitting on the old version. Verified by reproducing #503's exact diff: it named `.devcontainer/Dockerfile` and `.node-version` and stayed silent on `.nvmrc`, `package.json` and `AGENTS.md`, all three of which must also move. So the fix for a comment about an under-specified procedure was itself an under-specified procedure. Codex's other finding -- that corepack is unbundled as of Node 25, so pin alignment could never make such a PR green -- was **wrong**: v25.6.0's changelog updates corepack to 0.34.6 and neither the v25 nor the v26 changelog records a removal. The caution underneath it was fair, though, since this repository runs `corepack enable` in two places without installing it, so the comment now says pin alignment alone is not automatically enough for a major bump and names that dependency as the thing to re-verify. *Decision recorded with it:* #503 was **closed rather than completed**. Per `nodejs/Release`, Node 26 enters LTS on 2026-10-28 and Node 24 drops to Maintenance on 2026-10-20, so taking 26.8.1 in September would put a production healthcare build on the Current line for about seven weeks, where waiting makes the same move Active-LTS-to-Active-LTS. Revisit after 2026-10-28; Dependabot re-raises it on the next monthly cycle. |

## Explicitly not now

| Item | Why |
| --- | --- |
| Capability bundles / config release envelope | Enterprise; post-portfolio |
| Vendor external portal | Until maintenance is the top customer pain |
| Full Spanish i18n retrofit | After SMS + mobile proven |
| Multi-state rule packs | Finish Pennsylvania install → activate first, then decide where expansion actually matters |
| Expanding Essentials/Pro SKUs | Need conversion data |
| Competing on pharmacy eMAR network | Multi-year moat elsewhere |
| New root "comprehensive review" markdown | Update **this** file instead; the check rejects it |
